Technology curriculum proposal — v2

Research proposal for El Salvador, grades 2–6. Not an official allocation or a human-validated curriculum. Confidence is uncalibrated. Source gaps and external prerequisites remain visible.

Grade 2 · Grade 3 · Grade 4 · Grade 5 · Grade 6

Grade 2

Proposed core: 19.00 hours. Scenario capacity: 160 hours. Unallocated capacity: 141.00 hours. These are planning assumptions, not official hours.

Precise instructions

Move from executing one instruction to predicting and correcting a short sequence.

Follow a short, ordered sequence of instructions to achieve a specific outcome.

micro-follow-sequential-instructions-d68c600a3d3c93c2 · computational_thinking · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

Why this learning matters: This foundational skill is crucial for understanding how algorithms work. By physically following instructions, students develop a concrete understanding of sequence and precision, which are abstract concepts in computational thinking. This prepares them for more complex programming concepts.

Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 2, students are developing their ability to follow multi-step directions. Applying this to a 'program' of instructions helps them connect abstract concepts to tangible actions, aligning with their cognitive development stage.

Evidence of mastery: The student successfully executes a given sequence of 3-5 physical instructions without deviation.

Assessment task: Students receive a set of 4 picture cards, each depicting a simple action (e.g., 'take one step forward', 'turn left', 'pick up the red block'). They must arrange the cards in a specific order provided by the teacher and then physically perform the actions in that exact sequence.

Access and participation: Use visual aids (picture cards, gestures) alongside verbal instructions. Allow students to work in pairs, with one reading and the other performing, then switching roles. Provide tactile objects for manipulation.

T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

Evidence limitations: The cited evidence supports the importance of 'following precise and unambiguous instructions' but does not specify the optimal length of sequences or the specific age at which this skill is best introduced. The evidence is from a curriculum framework, not an empirical study.

Safety: All activities involve physical movement. Ensure a clear, safe space free of obstacles. Supervise students closely during movement-based games to prevent collisions or falls. Use only low-voltage, battery-operated components if any simple circuits are introduced in other units; this unit is unplugged.

Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

Success criteria
  • Arranges the instruction cards in the correct order.
  • Performs each action exactly as depicted on the card.
  • Completes the sequence without skipping or adding steps.
Teaching sequence
  • Diagnosis: Ask students to follow a simple 2-step instruction (e.g., 'stand up, then clap your hands') to gauge their current ability to follow sequential commands.
  • Practice: Introduce a 'robot game' where students give each other simple, precise instructions (e.g., 'move forward 3 steps', 'turn right') to navigate an obstacle course.
  • Feedback: Observe students during the robot game, providing immediate feedback on precision and sequence adherence. Discuss how small changes in instructions lead to different outcomes.
Misconceptions
  • Believing that the order of instructions doesn't matter.
  • Assuming implied steps not explicitly stated.
  • Difficulty distinguishing between 'turn left' and 'move left'.
Required resources
  • Picture cards depicting simple actions
  • Open space for movement
  • Small, safe objects for manipulation (e.g., blocks, cones)
External knowledge to verify
  • Basic spatial awareness (left/right, forward/backward)
  • Ability to follow verbal and visual directions
Unit limitations
  • The provided evidence is primarily from curriculum frameworks and does not offer empirical data on the effectiveness of specific teaching methods or the typical developmental progression of these skills in young children. Therefore, the estimated minutes and pedagogical approaches are hypotheses based on general educational best practices.
  • The unit focuses on 'unplugged' activities. While this builds foundational understanding, it does not directly address the implementation of algorithms on digital devices, which is mentioned in some source texts. This transition would require subsequent units.
  • The scope is limited to simple, linear sequences. Concepts like selection (if/then) and repetition (loops) are not explicitly covered in this introductory unit, despite being mentioned in some source documents for broader computational thinking.
  • The 'teacher prep level' is an estimate and can vary significantly based on teacher experience and familiarity with computational thinking concepts.
  • The unit-level 'assessment_minutes' is set to 0 as all assessment is formative and integrated within the 'estimated_minutes' of each micro-skill. The 'estimated_minutes' for each micro-skill includes instruction, practice, and formative assessment activities, not just the assessment task itself. There is no separate summative assessment for the unit.
Publication corrections after model review
    Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
    • minor: The unit's learning goal is 'Follow and debug a short ordered instruction sequence with concrete objects.' However, the micro-skill 'create-simple-instruction-sequence' involves creating algorithms, which is a distinct cognitive process from following and debugging them. Suggested correction: Refine the unit's learning goal to encompass creation, such as 'Follow, create, and debug short, ordered instruction sequences using concrete objects.'
    • minor: The evidence quote 'use sequence, selection, and repetition in programs; work with variables and various forms of input and output' is provided as a source but is not cited by any micro-skill. It also refers to concepts like selection, repetition, and variables which are explicitly out of scope for this unit. Suggested correction: Remove the unused and out-of-scope evidence source '[eng-computing html ¶paragraph-p110-7c8559df04ecea31]' to avoid confusion about the unit's focus.

    Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

    Sources: source-eng-computing-570e16e9afcbc1c7 (page HTML, Key stage 1, paragraph-p90-7d8109e0e4d36796, original characters 107–164)

    Create a short, ordered sequence of instructions to guide another person or object to a specific goal.

    micro-create-simple-instruction-sequence-58a50eec23ca48f4 · computational_thinking · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

    Why this learning matters: Moving from following to creating instructions is a critical step in developing algorithmic thinking. It requires students to decompose a problem (reaching a goal) into smaller, actionable steps and to think logically about the order and precision needed for success.

    Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 2, students are capable of simple problem formulation and expressing ideas sequentially. This activity encourages them to apply their understanding of sequence in a creative, problem-solving context, which is appropriate for their developmental stage.

    Evidence of mastery: The student designs a sequence of 3-5 instructions that, when followed, successfully leads a peer or object to a predetermined target.

    Assessment task: Students are given a starting point and a target point on a grid or map. They must draw or write a sequence of 3-5 simple instructions (e.g., 'move forward 2 squares', 'turn right') that, if followed by a classmate, would lead them from the start to the target.

    Access and participation: Provide pre-made instruction cards that students can arrange, rather than writing from scratch. Use large-print grids or tactile maps. Allow verbal instruction creation for students with writing difficulties.

    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

    Evidence limitations: The Argentine curriculum reference highlights 'creating strategies' and 'ordered sequences' but does not specify the complexity or length of sequences appropriate for this age. The quote emphasizes creativity and experimentation with error, which are pedagogical approaches rather than direct skill definitions.

    Safety: All activities involve physical movement. Ensure a clear, safe space free of obstacles. Supervise students closely during movement-based games to prevent collisions or falls. Use only low-voltage, battery-operated components if any simple circuits are introduced in other units; this unit is unplugged.

    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

    Success criteria
    • Instructions are clear and unambiguous.
    • The sequence of instructions is logically ordered.
    • Following the instructions leads precisely to the target.
    Teaching sequence
    • Diagnosis: Ask students to describe how to make a simple sandwich. Look for sequential thinking and clarity of steps.
    • Practice: Introduce a 'treasure hunt' activity where students create simple instruction sets (e.g., 'take 3 steps forward, turn left, take 2 steps') for a partner to find a hidden object.
    • Feedback: Have students test each other's instruction sets. Discuss any ambiguities or errors and guide them to refine their instructions for greater precision.
    Misconceptions
    • Assuming the 'robot' knows what they mean without explicit instructions.
    • Forgetting to include turns or changes in direction.
    • Creating instructions that are too vague (e.g., 'go over there').
    Required resources
    • Grids or simple maps (drawn or printed)
    • Markers or pencils
    • Small objects to serve as 'treasure' or targets
    External knowledge to verify
    • Basic directional vocabulary (forward, backward, left, right)
    • Understanding of simple counting
    Unit limitations
    • The provided evidence is primarily from curriculum frameworks and does not offer empirical data on the effectiveness of specific teaching methods or the typical developmental progression of these skills in young children. Therefore, the estimated minutes and pedagogical approaches are hypotheses based on general educational best practices.
    • The unit focuses on 'unplugged' activities. While this builds foundational understanding, it does not directly address the implementation of algorithms on digital devices, which is mentioned in some source texts. This transition would require subsequent units.
    • The scope is limited to simple, linear sequences. Concepts like selection (if/then) and repetition (loops) are not explicitly covered in this introductory unit, despite being mentioned in some source documents for broader computational thinking.
    • The 'teacher prep level' is an estimate and can vary significantly based on teacher experience and familiarity with computational thinking concepts.
    • The unit-level 'assessment_minutes' is set to 0 as all assessment is formative and integrated within the 'estimated_minutes' of each micro-skill. The 'estimated_minutes' for each micro-skill includes instruction, practice, and formative assessment activities, not just the assessment task itself. There is no separate summative assessment for the unit.
    Publication corrections after model review

However, the micro-skill 'create-simple-instruction-sequence' involves creating algorithms, which is a distinct cognitive process from following and debugging them. Suggested correction: Refine the unit's learning goal to encompass creation, such as 'Follow, create, and debug short, ordered instruction sequences using concrete objects.'
      • minor: The evidence quote 'use sequence, selection, and repetition in programs; work with variables and various forms of input and output' is provided as a source but is not cited by any micro-skill. It also refers to concepts like selection, repetition, and variables which are explicitly out of scope for this unit. Suggested correction: Remove the unused and out-of-scope evidence source '[eng-computing html ¶paragraph-p110-7c8559df04ecea31]' to avoid confusion about the unit's focus.

      Internal prerequisite IDs: micro-follow-sequential-instructions-d68c600a3d3c93c2

      Identify an error in a short, ordered sequence of instructions that prevents it from achieving its intended outcome.

      micro-identify-errors-in-sequence-7ee3a679a8a84404 · computational_thinking · T0 · 40 instruction/practice minutes

      Why this learning matters: Debugging is a core computational thinking skill. By identifying errors, students develop logical reasoning and critical thinking. This skill is essential for understanding why programs fail and how to improve them, fostering resilience and problem-solving.

      Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 2, students are beginning to develop logical reasoning. Identifying errors in a concrete, observable sequence is an age-appropriate way to introduce debugging, allowing them to see the direct consequence of an incorrect instruction.

      Evidence of mastery: Given a sequence of 3-5 instructions with one deliberate error, the student points out the specific instruction that causes the incorrect outcome.

      Assessment task: The teacher presents a sequence of 4 instructions (e.g., 'step forward', 'turn left', 'step forward', 'turn right') designed to lead to a specific object. The teacher then performs the sequence with one deliberate error (e.g., turning right instead of left). Students must identify which instruction was incorrect.

      Access and participation: Use large, clear visual aids for instructions. Allow students to physically 'walk through' the sequence themselves to identify the error. Provide sentence starters for explaining the error.

      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

      Evidence limitations: The quote broadly states 'detect and correct errors' but does not specify the complexity of errors or the context (physical vs. digital) for this age group. It also doesn't provide guidance on the number of errors or the length of the sequence appropriate for Grade 2.

      Safety: All activities involve physical movement. Ensure a clear, safe space free of obstacles. Supervise students closely during movement-based games to prevent collisions or falls. Use only low-voltage, battery-operated components if any simple circuits are introduced in other units; this unit is unplugged.

      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

      Success criteria
      • Observes the execution of the instruction sequence carefully.
      • Pinpoints the exact instruction that caused the deviation from the intended path.
      • Points to the specific instruction that caused the deviation from the intended path.
      Teaching sequence
      • Diagnosis: Present a simple 'broken' sequence (e.g., 'put on shoes, put on socks') and ask students what's wrong.
      • Practice: Play 'Spot the Robot Error'. The teacher or a student acts as a robot following a sequence, but deliberately makes one mistake. Other students identify the error.
      • Feedback: Discuss common types of errors (e.g., wrong direction, wrong number of steps, incorrect order). Emphasize that errors are opportunities for learning and improvement.
      Misconceptions
      • Blaming the 'robot' for not understanding, rather than the instruction.
      • Identifying the wrong step as the error.
      • Thinking the entire sequence is wrong, instead of just one part.
      Required resources
      • Instruction cards or written sequences
      • Open space for demonstration
      • Objects for target points
      External knowledge to verify
      • Ability to compare observed actions with intended actions
      • Basic understanding of cause and effect
      Unit limitations
      • The provided evidence is primarily from curriculum frameworks and does not offer empirical data on the effectiveness of specific teaching methods or the typical developmental progression of these skills in young children. Therefore, the estimated minutes and pedagogical approaches are hypotheses based on general educational best practices.
      • The unit focuses on 'unplugged' activities. While this builds foundational understanding, it does not directly address the implementation of algorithms on digital devices, which is mentioned in some source texts. This transition would require subsequent units.
      • The scope is limited to simple, linear sequences. Concepts like selection (if/then) and repetition (loops) are not explicitly covered in this introductory unit, despite being mentioned in some source documents for broader computational thinking.
      • The 'teacher prep level' is an estimate and can vary significantly based on teacher experience and familiarity with computational thinking concepts.
      • The unit-level 'assessment_minutes' is set to 0 as all assessment is formative and integrated within the 'estimated_minutes' of each micro-skill. The 'estimated_minutes' for each micro-skill includes instruction, practice, and formative assessment activities, not just the assessment task itself. There is no separate summative assessment for the unit.
      Publication corrections after model review
        Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
        • minor: The unit's learning goal is 'Follow and debug a short ordered instruction sequence with concrete objects.' However, the micro-skill 'create-simple-instruction-sequence' involves creating algorithms, which is a distinct cognitive process from following and debugging them. Suggested correction: Refine the unit's learning goal to encompass creation, such as 'Follow, create, and debug short, ordered instruction sequences using concrete objects.'
        • minor: The success criteria for this skill are 'Observes the execution of the instruction sequence carefully.', 'Pinpoints the exact instruction that caused the deviation from the intended path.', and 'Points to the specific instruction that caused the deviation from the intended path.' The last two criteria are identical. Suggested correction: Consolidate the redundant success criteria. For example, combine them into a single criterion: 'Identifies the specific instruction that caused the deviation from the intended outcome.'
        • minor: The evidence quote 'use sequence, selection, and repetition in programs; work with variables and various forms of input and output' is provided as a source but is not cited by any micro-skill. It also refers to concepts like selection, repetition, and variables which are explicitly out of scope for this unit. Suggested correction: Remove the unused and out-of-scope evidence source '[eng-computing html ¶paragraph-p110-7c8559df04ecea31]' to avoid confusion about the unit's focus.

        Correct a single error in a short, ordered sequence of instructions to achieve the intended outcome.

        micro-correct-simple-instruction-error-73c9ed98b1c933ff · computational_thinking · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

        Why this learning matters: Correction builds directly on identification, moving students from passive observation to active problem-solving. This reinforces the iterative nature of design and programming, where testing and refining are crucial steps.

        Proposed grade placement rationale: After identifying errors, Grade 2 students are ready to propose solutions. This step encourages them to think critically about how to fix a problem, fostering a growth mindset and practical application of their understanding of sequence and precision.

        Evidence of mastery: Given a sequence of 3-5 instructions with one deliberate error, the student modifies the incorrect instruction so that the sequence now achieves its intended goal.

        Assessment task: Students are given a written or pictorial sequence of 4 instructions that has one error preventing a 'robot' (classmate or teacher) from reaching a specific target. Students must rewrite or replace the incorrect instruction to make the sequence work correctly.

        Access and participation: Provide editable instruction cards (e.g., laminated cards with dry-erase markers). Offer a bank of correct instruction options for students to choose from, rather than generating from scratch. Allow verbal correction followed by teacher transcription.

        T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

        Evidence limitations: Similar to error identification, the evidence supports the general concept of 'correcting errors' but does not specify the type or complexity of corrections suitable for Grade 2. The context of 'algorithms and programs' is broad and needs adaptation for this age group.

        Safety: All activities involve physical movement. Ensure a clear, safe space free of obstacles. Supervise students closely during movement-based games to prevent collisions or falls. Use only low-voltage, battery-operated components if any simple circuits are introduced in other units; this unit is unplugged.

        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

        Success criteria
        • Identifies the incorrect instruction.
        • Proposes a modification that directly addresses the error.
        • The modified sequence, when executed, successfully reaches the target.
        Teaching sequence
        • Diagnosis: After 'Spot the Robot Error', ask students not just to identify, but to suggest how to fix the error.
        • Practice: Provide students with 'broken' instruction sets (e.g., for drawing a simple shape, or navigating a maze). In pairs, they must find and fix the error, then test their corrected sequence.
        • Feedback: Have pairs demonstrate their corrected sequences. Discuss different ways to fix an error and the importance of testing after making changes.
        Misconceptions
        • Changing multiple instructions when only one is incorrect.
        • Proposing a fix that introduces a new error.
        • Not re-testing the sequence after making a correction.
        Required resources
        • Pre-prepared 'broken' instruction sequences (written or pictorial)
        • Materials for modifying instructions (e.g., pencils, erasers, alternative cards)
        • Space and objects for testing corrected sequences
        External knowledge to verify
        • Understanding of how to modify existing text or drawings
        • Ability to re-evaluate a plan based on new information
        Unit limitations
        • The provided evidence is primarily from curriculum frameworks and does not offer empirical data on the effectiveness of specific teaching methods or the typical developmental progression of these skills in young children. Therefore, the estimated minutes and pedagogical approaches are hypotheses based on general educational best practices.
        • The unit focuses on 'unplugged' activities. While this builds foundational understanding, it does not directly address the implementation of algorithms on digital devices, which is mentioned in some source texts. This transition would require subsequent units.
        • The scope is limited to simple, linear sequences. Concepts like selection (if/then) and repetition (loops) are not explicitly covered in this introductory unit, despite being mentioned in some source documents for broader computational thinking.
        • The 'teacher prep level' is an estimate and can vary significantly based on teacher experience and familiarity with computational thinking concepts.
        • The unit-level 'assessment_minutes' is set to 0 as all assessment is formative and integrated within the 'estimated_minutes' of each micro-skill. The 'estimated_minutes' for each micro-skill includes instruction, practice, and formative assessment activities, not just the assessment task itself. There is no separate summative assessment for the unit.

          • minor: The evidence quote 'use sequence, selection, and repetition in programs; work with variables and various forms of input and output' is provided as a source but is not cited by any micro-skill. It also refers to concepts like selection, repetition, and variables which are explicitly out of scope for this unit. Suggested correction: Remove the unused and out-of-scope evidence source '[eng-computing html ¶paragraph-p110-7c8559df04ecea31]' to avoid confusion about the unit's focus.

          Safe participation

          Practice concrete decisions in fictional situations without requiring personal accounts.

          Students can identify examples of personal information that should be kept private online.

          micro-identify-personal-information-bd6f668cf1b6d48f · digital_citizenship · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

          Why this learning matters: Understanding what constitutes personal information is foundational for digital privacy. This skill focuses on recognition, a prerequisite for applying privacy rules. It's crucial to establish this concept early to prevent sharing sensitive data.

          Proposed grade placement rationale: At second grade, students are developing abstract thinking but still benefit from concrete examples. Identifying personal information through scenarios is appropriate for their cognitive stage, preparing them for more complex privacy decisions.

          Evidence of mastery: Students correctly categorize information as personal/private or shareable in various scenarios.

          Assessment task: Given a set of cards with different types of information (e.g., 'favorite color', 'home address', 'pet's name', 'school name'), students sort them into two piles: 'Private' and 'Shareable'.

          Access and participation: Provide visual aids (pictures for information types), allow verbal responses instead of sorting cards for students with fine motor challenges, and use simplified language.

          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

          Evidence limitations: The quote 'keeping personal information private' indicates the importance of privacy but does not detail what constitutes personal information or how to teach its identification. The interpretation of 'private' for this age group is pedagogical.

          Safety: All online activities must be supervised by an adult. Emphasize that students should never share personal information online without explicit adult permission. Discussions about 'concerning content' should be handled sensitively, focusing on reporting mechanisms rather than dwelling on specific negative examples.

          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

          Success criteria
          • Correctly sorts at least 4 out of 5 'Private' information cards.
          • Correctly sorts at least 4 out of 5 'Shareable' information cards.
          Teaching sequence
          • Diagnosis: Ask students to brainstorm what information they share with friends vs. strangers.
          • Practice: Present various information types and discuss as a class whether they are private or shareable, explaining 'why'.
          • Practice: Use a 'thumbs up/down' activity for quick classification of information types.
          • Feedback: Review sorted cards, correcting misconceptions and reinforcing the definition of personal information.
          Misconceptions
          • Believing that information shared with friends online is always private.
          • Confusing 'secret' information with 'private' information.
          • Thinking that any information about themselves is automatically private.
          Required resources
          • Set of information cards (physical or digital)
          • Whiteboard or large paper for categorization
          External knowledge to verify
          • Basic understanding of personal identity (name, age, family)
          Unit limitations
          • The provided evidence is primarily from international curricula (DigComp, England Computing) and requires adaptation for the specific cultural and educational context of El Salvador.
          • The 'estimated minutes' are pedagogical hypotheses and do not account for variations in student learning pace or classroom management challenges.
          • The unit focuses on foundational concepts and does not cover advanced topics like digital footprints, online reputation, or complex privacy settings, which are more appropriate for higher grades.
          • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on consistent reinforcement at home and across other subjects, which is outside the scope of this unit.
          Publication corrections after model review
            Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
            • minor: The total estimated time for the unit (45 + 50 + 60 = 155 minutes) significantly exceeds the proposed assessment time (30 minutes). This suggests a misalignment between the time allocated for teaching and the time allocated for summative assessment. Suggested correction: Either increase the 'assessment_minutes' to reflect a more comprehensive final evaluation of the skills, or clarify that the 30 minutes refers to a specific, limited summative task and that mastery is primarily assessed formatively within each micro-skill's activities.

            Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

            Sources: source-eng-computing-570e16e9afcbc1c7 (page HTML, Key stage 1, paragraph-p100-0b2af1b9bdc27581, original characters 40–76)

            Students can identify a trusted adult to seek help from when encountering concerning online content or contact.

            micro-seek-trusted-adult-help-952027ab6e8993f2 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

            Why this learning matters: Empowering students to seek help is critical for their online safety. This skill focuses on practical application by identifying specific individuals, making the concept concrete and actionable. It addresses the 'what to do' aspect of online safety.

            Proposed grade placement rationale: Second graders are capable of understanding the concept of trusted adults and applying it to new situations. This skill reinforces existing safety lessons (e.g., 'stranger danger') and extends them to the digital realm.

            Evidence of mastery: Students can name at least two trusted adults and explain why they would go to them for help with an online concern.

            Assessment task: After listening to a short story about a child encountering something uncomfortable online, students draw or write the names of two trusted adults they would tell and explain their choice.

            Access and participation: Provide sentence starters for explanations, allow students to point to pictures of trusted adults, or use a communication board for non-verbal students.

            T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

            Evidence limitations: The quote 'identify where to go for help and support' is broad. This micro-skill specifically interprets 'where to go' as identifying trusted adults, which is a common and age-appropriate strategy for young children.

            Safety: All online activities must be supervised by an adult. Emphasize that students should never share personal information online without explicit adult permission. Discussions about 'concerning content' should be handled sensitively, focusing on reporting mechanisms rather than dwelling on specific negative examples.

            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

            Success criteria
            • Names or draws at least two specific trusted adults (e.g., parent, teacher, guardian).
            • Provides a simple, logical reason for choosing each adult (e.g., 'they help me', 'they keep me safe').
            Teaching sequence
            • Diagnosis: Ask students what they would do if they saw something scary on TV.
            • Practice: Discuss what makes an adult 'trusted' and brainstorm examples (parents, teachers, guardians).
            • Practice: Role-play scenarios where students encounter concerning online content and practice telling a trusted adult.
            • Feedback: Review drawings/writings, ensuring students understand the importance of telling an adult and reinforcing who their trusted adults are.
            Misconceptions
            • Believing they can handle online problems by themselves.
            • Feeling embarrassed or scared to tell an adult about online issues.
            • Not knowing who a 'trusted adult' is in the context of online safety.
            Required resources
            • Story about an online concern (written or video)
            • Drawing materials or paper for writing names
            External knowledge to verify
            • Concept of 'trusted adults' from general safety education
            Unit limitations
            • The provided evidence is primarily from international curricula (DigComp, England Computing) and requires adaptation for the specific cultural and educational context of El Salvador.
            • The 'estimated minutes' are pedagogical hypotheses and do not account for variations in student learning pace or classroom management challenges.
            • The unit focuses on foundational concepts and does not cover advanced topics like digital footprints, online reputation, or complex privacy settings, which are more appropriate for higher grades.
            • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on consistent reinforcement at home and across other subjects, which is outside the scope of this unit.
            Publication corrections after model review
              Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
              • minor: The total estimated time for the unit (45 + 50 + 60 = 155 minutes) significantly exceeds the proposed assessment time (30 minutes). This suggests a misalignment between the time allocated for teaching and the time allocated for summative assessment. Suggested correction: Either increase the 'assessment_minutes' to reflect a more comprehensive final evaluation of the skills, or clarify that the 30 minutes refers to a specific, limited summative task and that mastery is primarily assessed formatively within each micro-skill's activities.

              Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

              Sources: source-eng-computing-570e16e9afcbc1c7 (page HTML, Key stage 1, paragraph-p100-0b2af1b9bdc27581, original characters 78–119)

              Students can distinguish between personal information and shareable classroom work in digital contexts.

              micro-distinguish-shareable-classroom-work-e3894aaa7c8dcdad · digital_citizenship · T1 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

              Why this learning matters: This skill builds on identifying personal information by applying it to a common digital activity: sharing schoolwork. It helps students understand the practical implications of privacy in a relevant context, preparing them for collaborative online environments.

              Proposed grade placement rationale: Second graders often engage in creating digital projects. Learning to differentiate between what is appropriate to share from their work and what is private is a practical application of digital citizenship at this age.

              Evidence of mastery: Students correctly identify which parts of a digital classroom project are appropriate to share publicly and which contain private information.

              Assessment task: Given a mock digital classroom project (e.g., a presentation slide with a student's name, age, and a drawing of their family, alongside project content), students use a digital tool (e.g., drawing app, simple presentation software) to highlight or circle the parts that are safe to share publicly and cross out or hide the private information.

              Access and participation: Provide pre-made digital templates with clear sections for private/shareable info, use verbal prompts, or allow peer support for digital tool navigation.

              T0 alternative and limits: Without digital devices, students can still distinguish between personal information and shareable classroom work using paper-based mock-ups. However, they lose the operational experience of interacting with digital tools to modify or redact information, which is a key aspect of digital literacy.

              Evidence limitations: The quote 'identify simple ways to use and share personally' supports the concept of managing personal information but does not specifically address classroom work or digital contexts. The application to classroom work is a pedagogical interpretation for relevance.

              Safety: All online activities must be supervised by an adult. Emphasize that students should never share personal information online without explicit adult permission. Discussions about 'concerning content' should be handled sensitively, focusing on reporting mechanisms rather than dwelling on specific negative examples.

              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

              Success criteria
              • Accurately identifies and marks all private information for removal/hiding.
              • Accurately identifies and marks all shareable project content.
              • Explains the reasoning for at least two choices (one private, one shareable).
              • Successfully uses the digital tool to mark at least 90% of the designated private and shareable items.
              Teaching sequence
              • Diagnosis: Show an example of a classroom project and ask what parts they think are okay to show to anyone.
              • Practice: Present various mock digital projects and discuss what information is part of the project vs. personal data.
              • Practice: Guide students through editing a sample project to remove private details before 'sharing' it.
              • Feedback: Review student work, providing specific feedback on correctly identified private and shareable elements, and discussing any remaining private information.
              Misconceptions
              • Believing that anything created for school is automatically shareable.
              • Not understanding that their name or picture can be personal information in a public context.
              • Thinking that if a teacher sees it, it's not private.
              Required resources
              • Teacher device (T1) to display mock projects
              • Shared devices (T2) or individual devices (T3) with a simple drawing/presentation app
              • Mock digital classroom project examples
              External knowledge to verify
              • Basic understanding of what constitutes 'school work' or 'projects'
              Unit limitations
              • The provided evidence is primarily from international curricula (DigComp, England Computing) and requires adaptation for the specific cultural and educational context of El Salvador.
              • The 'estimated minutes' are pedagogical hypotheses and do not account for variations in student learning pace or classroom management challenges.
              • The unit focuses on foundational concepts and does not cover advanced topics like digital footprints, online reputation, or complex privacy settings, which are more appropriate for higher grades.
              • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on consistent reinforcement at home and across other subjects, which is outside the scope of this unit.
              Publication corrections after model review
                Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                • minor: The evidence cited for the skill 'distinguish-shareable-classroom-work' does not support the proposed activity. The quote 'identify simple ways to use and share personally identifiable information' refers to managing one's own information, not distinguishing it from academic content within a classroom project. Suggested correction: Cite evidence that specifically addresses the context of sharing schoolwork or digital artifacts, or acknowledge that this skill is a pedagogical extension beyond the direct scope of the provided evidence.
                • minor: The success criteria for 'distinguish-shareable-classroom-work' are not atomic. 'Successfully uses the digital tool to mark at least 90% of the designated private and shareable items' measures tool proficiency, not the cognitive skill of distinguishing information types, which is already covered by other criteria. Suggested correction: Remove the success criterion related to tool proficiency. Assess the ability to use the digital tool separately if it is a distinct learning objective, or treat it as an enabling skill for the assessment task.
                • minor: The total estimated time for the unit (45 + 50 + 60 = 155 minutes) significantly exceeds the proposed assessment time (30 minutes). This suggests a misalignment between the time allocated for teaching and the time allocated for summative assessment.                 Design for a need

                Make and explain one improvement based on observation, not decoration.

                Designs a simple artifact to address a user need, communicating ideas through drawing.

                micro-identify-need-and-design-solution-20ba08acfd226895 · design_process · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                Why this learning matters: At this grade level, students are developing their ability to connect problems with solutions. This micro-skill focuses on the initial conceptualization phase of design, emphasizing purpose and communication through drawing, which is a natural and accessible medium for young learners. It lays the foundation for more complex design thinking, integrating specific scientific concepts from the Salvadoran curriculum by framing the 'user need' around structural integrity, balance, or energy transformation.

                Proposed grade placement rationale: Second graders can identify simple problems in their environment and express their ideas visually. This skill aligns with their cognitive development, encouraging creative problem-solving without requiring advanced technical skills. By focusing on needs related to structures, balance, or energy, it directly supports the conceptual content of the Salvadoran curriculum for this grade.

                Evidence of mastery: Students will produce a drawing of an artifact that clearly shows how it addresses a specific, identified user need, potentially related to structure, balance, or energy transformation.

                Assessment task: Given a scenario (e.g., 'A small toy keeps falling off a shelf, how can you make a stable support?' or 'How can you make something move without touching it?'), students will draw an artifact that solves this problem, labeling its key features and explaining how it works.

                Access and participation: Provide visual aids and real-world examples of problems and solutions. Allow verbal explanations alongside drawings for students with fine motor skill challenges. Offer templates or starting points for drawing if needed.

                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                Evidence limitations: The quote supports designing purposeful products but does not explicitly detail the communication method (drawing) or the specific focus on 'user need' as a starting point, which are pedagogical choices for this grade level. It also does not specify the integration of scientific concepts like structures or energy transformation, which is a pedagogical adaptation to align with the Salvadoran curriculum.

                Safety: All construction activities must be supervised. Only low-voltage batteries (if used) and child-safe tools (e.g., safety scissors, blunt needles) are permitted. Emphasize proper handling of materials and tools to prevent injury. Ensure a clean and organized workspace to minimize hazards.

                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                Success criteria
                • The drawing clearly depicts an artifact.
                • The artifact's design directly addresses the identified user need.
                • Key features of the artifact are labeled or explained.
                • The drawing is clear enough for another person to understand the idea.
                Teaching sequence
                • Introduce the concept of 'needs' and 'problems' through examples relevant to students' lives, specifically linking to ideas of stability, movement, or balance.
                • Facilitate a discussion on how everyday objects solve problems, including examples of simple structures or devices that move things.
                • Present a simple user need scenario (e.g., 'design a stable platform' or 'design a way to make a ball roll down') and brainstorm potential solutions as a class.
                • Model how to draw a simple design, labeling parts and explaining its function.
                • Provide individual practice time for students to draw their own solutions to a new scenario, encouraging them to think about how their design provides stability, balance, or causes movement.
                • Offer constructive feedback on drawings, focusing on clarity and problem-solving effectiveness.
                Misconceptions
                • Designs must be complex to be effective.
                • A drawing needs to be 'perfect' to communicate an idea.
                • All problems have only one correct solution.
                Required resources
                • Paper
                • Pencils
                • Crayons/markers
                External knowledge to verify
                • Basic drawing skills
                • Understanding of simple cause and effect
                Unit limitations
                • The scope of 'design criteria' for this grade is limited to the artifact's primary function and basic stability, balance, or ability to cause movement.
                • The 'improvement' phase is restricted to identifying one simple modification, not necessarily implementing it within the unit.
                • The unit does not delve into complex material science or engineering principles, focusing instead on observable properties and practical application relevant to structures, balance, and energy transformation.
                • The unit does not require digital devices, which means certain aspects of modern design communication (e.g., CAD, digital mock-ups) are not covered.
                • While the unit fosters the attitude of 'Autoconfianza en su creatividad y conocimientos para encontrar soluciones ante una situación nueva', its project scope is more general than the specific technical applications (e.g., building a bridge to test resistance) detailed in the source curriculum. The link is thematic, not a direct implementation of the source's procedural content.

                  Selects and uses appropriate materials and simple tools to construct a basic artifact.

                  micro-select-and-use-materials-tools-7fdea3552842f1fd · design_process · T0 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                  Why this learning matters: This micro-skill bridges the gap between design ideas and physical realization. It introduces students to the practical aspects of making, including material selection and tool handling, which are fundamental for any design and technology activity. Emphasis is placed on safety and purpose-driven selection. By constructing artifacts related to structures, balance, or energy, students gain hands-on experience with the scientific concepts outlined in the Salvadoran curriculum.

                  Proposed grade placement rationale: Second graders are developing fine motor skills and can follow multi-step instructions. They benefit from hands-on activities that allow them to manipulate objects and see their designs come to life. The focus is on simple tools and materials to ensure accessibility and safety, while also providing a tangible way to explore concepts like rigid structures or energy transformation.

                  Evidence of mastery: Students will successfully assemble a simple artifact (e.g., a stable structure, a device that demonstrates balance, or a simple energy transformer) using provided materials and tools, demonstrating safe and appropriate use.

                  Assessment task: Given a design drawing (either their own or a provided one) for a simple artifact (e.g., a stable bridge model, a simple balance scale, or a device to make a marble roll), students will select from a variety of provided materials (cardboard, paper, tape, glue) and use simple tools (safety scissors) to construct the artifact.

                  Access and participation: Provide pre-cut materials or adaptive tools for students with fine motor challenges. Use visual step-by-step instructions. Pair students for collaborative construction if needed. Ensure materials are easy to manipulate.

                  T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                  Evidence limitations: The quote broadly supports selecting and using tools and materials. It does not specify the 'appropriateness' of selection or the 'basic' nature of the artifact, which are pedagogical refinements for this grade level. It also does not explicitly mention the integration of specific scientific concepts like structures or energy transformation, which is a pedagogical adaptation to align with the Salvadoran curriculum.

                  Safety: All construction activities must be supervised. Only low-voltage batteries (if used) and child-safe tools (e.g., safety scissors, blunt needles) are permitted. Emphasize proper handling of materials and tools to prevent injury. Ensure a clean and organized workspace to minimize hazards.

                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                  Success criteria
                  • The constructed artifact resembles the design drawing.
                  • The artifact is stable enough to perform its intended function (e.g., hold weight, balance, cause movement).
                  • Materials are chosen appropriately for the artifact's purpose.
                  • Tools are used safely and correctly.
                  Teaching sequence
                  • Review the design drawing and discuss the function of the artifact, specifically how it relates to stability, balance, or energy transformation.
                  • Introduce available materials and simple tools, discussing their properties and safe use, linking material choice to the artifact's intended function (e.g., rigid for structures, flexible for movement).
                  • Model the construction process step-by-step, demonstrating how to cut, fold, and join materials.
                  • Provide guided practice, offering support as students begin construction.
                  • Circulate to provide individual feedback on material selection and tool use.
                  • Allow time for students to complete their construction.
                  Misconceptions
                  • Any material can be used for any purpose.
                  • Tools are only for cutting or joining.
                  • Construction must be perfect on the first try.
                  Required resources
                  • Various construction materials (e.g., cardboard, paper, craft sticks, pipe cleaners)
                  • Joining materials (e.g., tape, glue, staples)
                  • Simple tools (e.g., safety scissors, rulers)
                  • Design drawings (student-generated or provided)
                  External knowledge to verify
                  • Basic understanding of material properties (e.g., rigid, flexible)
                  • Safe handling of classroom tools
                  Unit limitations
                  • The scope of 'design criteria' for this grade is limited to the artifact's primary function and basic stability, balance, or ability to cause movement.
                  • The 'improvement' phase is restricted to identifying one simple modification, not necessarily implementing it within the unit.
                  • The unit does not delve into complex material science or engineering principles, focusing instead on observable properties and practical application relevant to structures, balance, and energy transformation.
                  • The unit does not require digital devices, which means certain aspects of modern design communication (e.g., CAD, digital mock-ups) are not covered.
                  • While the unit fosters the attitude of 'Autoconfianza en su creatividad y conocimientos para encontrar soluciones ante una situación nueva', its project scope is more general than the specific technical applications (e.g., building a bridge to test resistance) detailed in the source curriculum. The link is thematic, not a direct implementation of the source's procedural content.

                    Tests a constructed artifact to determine if it serves its intended purpose and identifies one area for improvement.

                    micro-test-and-evaluate-artifact-16bc996531e4848e · design_process · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                    Why this learning matters: Evaluation is a critical step in the design process. For second graders, this micro-skill focuses on direct observation of an artifact's function and the initial idea of iterative improvement. It encourages critical thinking about 'what works' and 'what could be better' in a tangible way. By testing artifacts related to structures, balance, or energy, students directly engage with the 'Indicadores avanzados' from the Salvadoran curriculum, such as 'Comprueba la resistencia de una estructura' and 'Realiza sus propias adecuaciones a los dispositivos construidos'.

                    Proposed grade placement rationale: Second graders can observe and describe simple outcomes. This skill introduces them to the concept of testing and feedback, fostering a growth mindset where 'failure' is an opportunity for learning and improvement, rather than an end point. It directly supports the Salvadoran curriculum's emphasis on checking resistance and making adjustments to constructed devices.

                    Evidence of mastery: Students will conduct a simple test on their artifact (e.g., a bridge's resistance, a balance scale's accuracy, a device's ability to transform energy), observe its performance, and verbally or visually suggest one specific improvement based on the test results.

                    Assessment task: Students will test their constructed artifact (e.g., a bridge model for resistance, a balance scale for accuracy, a device for moving an object) to see if it fulfills its purpose. They will then identify one specific aspect that could be improved and explain why, either verbally or by drawing a modification.

                    Access and participation: Provide clear, simple testing instructions. Use visual cues or checklists for observations. Allow students to express improvements through drawing, pointing, or verbal communication. Pair students for observation and discussion.

                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                    Evidence limitations: The quote supports evaluating products against criteria but does not explicitly mention 'identifying one area for improvement' or the 'simple' nature of the test, which are pedagogical adaptations for this age group. It also does not explicitly mention the integration of specific scientific concepts like structures or energy transformation, which is a pedagogical adaptation to align with the Salvadoran curriculum.

                    Safety: All construction activities must be supervised. Only low-voltage batteries (if used) and child-safe tools (e.g., safety scissors, blunt needles) are permitted. Emphasize proper handling of materials and tools to prevent injury. Ensure a clean and organized workspace to minimize hazards.

                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                    Success criteria
                    • The student performs the test as instructed.
                    • The student accurately describes whether the artifact met its purpose.
                    • The student identifies one specific, actionable improvement.
                    • The suggested improvement is directly related to the artifact's performance during the test.
                    Teaching sequence
                    • Review the intended purpose of the artifact, specifically how it relates to concepts like resistance, balance, or energy transformation.
                    • Model a simple test for a similar artifact, demonstrating how to observe and record results (e.g., how much weight a bridge holds, if a balance is level, if a device moves an object).
                    • Guide students in performing their own tests, encouraging careful observation.
                    • Facilitate a discussion on what worked well and what didn't, linking observations to the artifact's purpose and the scientific concepts involved.
                    • Introduce the concept of 'improvement' and brainstorm ways to make changes, referencing the Salvadoran curriculum's 'Realiza sus propias adecuaciones a los dispositivos construidos'.
                    • Provide individual support as students identify and articulate one improvement.
                    Misconceptions
                    • An artifact is either perfect or completely broken.
                    • Improvements are always about making it look nicer.
                    • Testing is only done once.
                    Required resources
                    • Constructed artifacts
                    • Materials for testing (e.g., weights for a bridge, small objects for a balance, marbles for a ramp)
                    • Paper and pencils for recording observations/improvements
                    External knowledge to verify
                    • Basic observation skills
                    • Understanding of 'better' or 'worse' in a functional context
                    Unit limitations
                    • The scope of 'design criteria' for this grade is limited to the artifact's primary function and basic stability, balance, or ability to cause movement.
                    • The 'improvement' phase is restricted to identifying one simple modification, not necessarily implementing it within the unit.
                    • The unit does not delve into complex material science or engineering principles, focusing instead on observable properties and practical application relevant to structures, balance, and energy transformation.
                    • The unit does not require digital devices, which means certain aspects of modern design communication (e.g., CAD, digital mock-ups) are not covered.
                    • While the unit fosters the attitude of 'Autoconfianza en su creatividad y conocimientos para encontrar soluciones ante una situación nueva', its project scope is more general than the specific technical applications (e.g., building a bridge to test resistance) detailed in the source curriculum. The link is thematic, not a direct implementation of the source's procedural content.

                      Measuring artifacts and structures

                      Connect material, shape and function through observable performance.

                      Students will be able to build a simple balance to compare the mass of objects.

                      micro-build-simple-balance-29bea183d8534a0c · technical_systems · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                      Why this learning matters: Building a simple balance introduces fundamental concepts of measurement and equilibrium in a tangible way. It allows students to explore physical properties of objects (mass) without relying on abstract numerical scales initially. This hands-on experience fosters problem-solving and fine motor skills.

                      Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 2, students are developing their understanding of comparative properties. A simple balance provides a concrete tool for comparing 'heavier' and 'lighter' before formal units of mass are introduced, aligning with the 'Equilibrio' content for this grade.

                      Evidence of mastery: The student constructs a functional balance that can visually indicate which of two objects is heavier or lighter.

                      Assessment task: Given a selection of common classroom objects (e.g., eraser, pencil, small toy, paperclip), construct a simple balance using provided materials (e.g., hanger, string, paper cups) and demonstrate its function by comparing the mass of two different objects.

                      Access and participation: Provide pre-cut materials for students with fine motor skill challenges. Use larger, easier-to-handle objects for comparison. Offer visual step-by-step instructions alongside verbal ones.

                      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                      Evidence limitations: The provided evidence supports the construction of balances and the use of tools, but does not specify the type of balance or the exact materials. The 'make' and 'design' aspects are general and require interpretation for this specific artifact.

                      Safety: All construction activities should use child-safe tools (e.g., blunt scissors, non-toxic glue). When testing structures, ensure masses are added carefully to prevent objects from falling and causing injury. Adult supervision is required for all building and testing activities.

                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                      Success criteria
                      • The balance is assembled correctly and is stable.
                      • The balance clearly shows a difference when objects of unequal mass are placed on it.
                      • The student can explain how the balance indicates which object is heavier.
                      Teaching sequence
                      • Diagnosis: Ask students how they know if something is heavy or light. Show two objects and ask which is heavier/lighter and why.
                      • Practice: Provide materials and guide students through the construction of a simple balance, emphasizing the concept of equilibrium. Allow time for experimentation with different objects.
                      • Feedback: Observe students' construction and use. Provide immediate feedback on stability and functionality. Discuss observations about object mass.
                      Misconceptions
                      • A larger object is always heavier.
                      • The balance needs to be perfectly still to work (ignoring minor oscillations).
                      Required resources
                      • Coat hangers or straight sticks
                      • String or yarn
                      • Paper cups or small containers
                      • Various small classroom objects (e.g., erasers, pencils, small toys, paperclips)
                      • Scissors (child-safe)
                      External knowledge to verify
                      • Basic understanding of 'heavier' and 'lighter' concepts.
                      Unit limitations
                      • The provided source documents offer general guidance on 'designing and making' and specific content for El Salvador Grade 2. However, they lack detailed pedagogical instructions or explicit connections between international and local curricula.
                      • The evidence does not provide empirical data on the efficacy of these specific activities or their transferability across different educational contexts.
                      • The total estimated time for the four micro-skills is 210 minutes (3.5 hours). The source document 'sv-cyt' indicates a duration of 5 weeks for the entire Unit 5, which includes several other topics not covered in this proposal (energy transformers, animal care). The proposed time allocation may be disproportionately high for this subset of the unit's content, suggesting that these activities might be spread across multiple sessions or integrated with other subjects to manage the workload effectively within the broader unit context.

                        Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

                        Sources: source-eng-dt-bac6168fed01542b (page 2, Key stage 1, paragraph-p12-7d97250bc813bdad, original characters 39–75); source-sv-cyt-e86fe5c5cf1a4905 (page 41, Grade 2, unit 5, paragraph-p45-2ce0d569a2872e8e, original characters 106–131); source-sv-cyt-e86fe5c5cf1a4905 (page 41, Grade 2, unit 5, paragraph-p45-2ce0d569a2872e8e, original characters 237–259)

                        Students will be able to record magnitude values using a self-made measuring instrument.

                        micro-record-magnitude-self-made-instrument-9edfee64c154bdc8 · technical_systems · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                        Why this learning matters: This micro-skill builds on the previous one by introducing the concept of documenting observations from a self-made tool. It reinforces the idea that measurement involves not just the act of comparing but also the systematic recording of results, a foundational skill for scientific inquiry and data collection. By introducing standard units (grams) in the recording, it bridges the gap between qualitative comparison and quantitative measurement.

                        Proposed grade placement rationale: For Grade 2, recording 'magnitude values' with a self-made instrument means documenting comparative results (e.g., 'more', 'less', 'equal') in relation to known standard units (grams) rather than precise numerical values. This aligns with the developmental stage of understanding measurement and data representation, as the curriculum explicitly mentions 'kilogramos' and 'gramos'.

                        Evidence of mastery: The student accurately records the comparative results (e.g., 'Object A > 50g', 'Object B < 50g') from their self-made balance using simple symbols or words, referencing standard mass units.

                        Assessment task: Using the balance constructed previously, compare the mass of three different objects against a known 50g mass. For each object, record whether it is heavier than, lighter than, or approximately equal to 50g, using a simple drawing or a pre-defined symbol (e.g., '>', '<', or '=') and the unit 'g'.

                        Access and participation: Provide large-print recording sheets or allow verbal recording for students with writing difficulties. Use tactile objects for comparison. Offer pre-drawn symbols for students to circle or point to.

                        T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                        Evidence limitations: The evidence mentions 'Registro de valores de magnitud' and specifies 'kilogramos'/'gramos' but does not detail the format or complexity of recording for this grade level. The interpretation for Grade 2 focuses on comparative results in relation to standard units, rather than direct measurement by the self-made instrument.

                        Safety: All construction activities should use child-safe tools (e.g., blunt scissors, non-toxic glue). When testing structures, ensure masses are added carefully to prevent objects from falling and causing injury. Adult supervision is required for all building and testing activities.

                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                        Success criteria
                        • The student correctly identifies if an object is heavier, lighter, or equal to the reference mass.
                        • The student uses the designated symbols or drawings consistently and accurately to represent the comparison.
                        • The recorded data is legible and understandable and includes the unit 'g'.
                        Teaching sequence
                        • Diagnosis: Ask students how they would tell someone else what they found out about the objects they weighed. Introduce the idea of recording findings and the concept of standard units like 'grams'.
                        • Practice: Model how to compare an object to a known gram weight using the balance and record the comparison using symbols and 'g'. Provide a worksheet or space for students to record their observations.
                        • Feedback: Review recorded observations. Discuss any discrepancies and help students refine their recording methods. Emphasize clarity, accuracy, and the use of the 'g' unit.
                        Misconceptions
                        • Recording is just writing down numbers, not comparisons.
                        • The order of recording doesn't matter.
                        • The balance directly measures grams, rather than comparing to known gram weights.
                        Required resources
                        • Self-made balances (from previous micro-skill)
                        • Various small classroom objects
                        • Known masses (e.g., small bags of sand/rice labeled 50g, 100g, or actual gram weights)
                        • Recording sheets with spaces for drawings/symbols
                        • Pencils or crayons
                        External knowledge to verify
                        • Ability to draw simple representations or use basic symbols.
                        • Basic understanding of the concept of 'grams' as a unit of mass.
                        Unit limitations
                        • The provided source documents offer general guidance on 'designing and making' and specific content for El Salvador Grade 2. However, they lack detailed pedagogical instructions or explicit connections between international and local curricula.
                        • The evidence does not provide empirical data on the efficacy of these specific activities or their transferability across different educational contexts.
                        • The total estimated time for the four micro-skills is 210 minutes (3.5 hours). The source document 'sv-cyt' indicates a duration of 5 weeks for the entire Unit 5, which includes several other topics not covered in this proposal (energy transformers, animal care). The proposed time allocation may be disproportionately high for this subset of the unit's content, suggesting that these activities might be spread across multiple sessions or integrated with other subjects to manage the workload effectively within the broader unit context.
                        Publication corrections after model review
                          Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                          • major: The assessment task for 'record-magnitude-self-made-instrument' requires comparing objects to a standard 50g mass, but the prerequisite skill 'build-simple-balance' only involves comparing two unknown objects. The teaching sequence does not adequately bridge the gap from qualitative comparison ('heavier/lighter') to semi-quantitative comparison against a standard unit. Suggested correction: Modify the teaching sequence for 'build-simple-balance' to include a step where students use their balance to compare an object against a known mass, or add a bridging activity before 'record-magnitude-self-made-instrument' that explicitly teaches this new procedure.
                          • minor: The assessment task for 'record-magnitude-self-made-instrument' requires using a known 50g mass. The source 'sv-cyt' notes that mass should be expressed in 'kilograms' or 'grams', but does not specify the use of standard masses for comparison. This method is an interpretation of how to achieve the learning goal.                           Students will be able to build a rigid bridge model.

                          micro-build-rigid-bridge-model-74c1ae80586d7dc4 · technical_systems · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                          Why this learning matters: Constructing a bridge model introduces students to structural engineering principles, focusing on rigidity and stability. This activity encourages spatial reasoning, problem-solving, and understanding how materials and shapes contribute to structural integrity. It's a practical application of design and construction skills.

                          Proposed grade placement rationale: For Grade 2, building a 'rigid structure' like a bridge model is an age-appropriate challenge that allows exploration of basic engineering concepts. It aligns with the 'Estructuras' content and the goal of understanding how structures can be made stronger and more stable.

                          Evidence of mastery: The student constructs a bridge model that spans a given gap and demonstrates rigidity, resisting deformation under light pressure.

                          Assessment task: Given a set of materials (e.g., craft sticks, paper, tape, glue) and a specified gap distance (e.g., between two blocks), construct a bridge model that spans the gap and can support its own weight without collapsing or significantly bending.

                          Access and participation: Provide pre-cut materials or templates for students with motor challenges. Work in small groups to allow peer support. Use larger, easier-to-manipulate materials. Offer visual examples of rigid structures.

                          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                          Evidence limitations: The evidence specifies 'modelo de puente' and 'estructura rígida' but does not detail the complexity, materials, or specific design principles expected for Grade 2. The interpretation focuses on basic rigidity and spanning.

                          Safety: All construction activities should use child-safe tools (e.g., blunt scissors, non-toxic glue). When testing structures, ensure masses are added carefully to prevent objects from falling and causing injury. Adult supervision is required for all building and testing activities.

                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                          Success criteria
                          • The bridge model successfully spans the specified gap.
                          • The bridge model remains stable and rigid when placed across the gap.
                          • The student can identify at least one feature that makes their bridge rigid (e.g., triangles, folded paper, strong joints).
                          Teaching sequence
                          • Diagnosis: Show pictures of different bridges and ask students what makes them strong. Discuss the concept of 'rigidity' (not bending easily).
                          • Practice: Introduce various materials and demonstrate basic joining techniques. Guide students in small groups to design and build their bridge models, encouraging experimentation with shapes and supports.
                          • Feedback: Test each bridge model for rigidity and spanning ability. Discuss what worked well and what could be improved. Highlight effective structural elements.
                          Misconceptions
                          • More material always means stronger.
                          • A flat surface is always rigid.
                          Required resources
                          • Craft sticks, cardboard, paper, straws
                          • Tape, glue
                          • Blocks or books to create a gap
                          • Rulers or measuring tapes (for gap distance)
                          External knowledge to verify
