                              Students will be able to record the maximum resistance values for a self-made rigid structure.

                              micro-record-max-resistance-structure-ce237fccf06a2de4 · technical_systems · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                              Why this learning matters: This micro-skill introduces the concept of testing and quantifying structural strength. By systematically adding known masses and recording the 'maximum resistance' in grams, students engage in a basic form of engineering testing and data collection. It reinforces the importance of empirical evidence in design and introduces the practical application of standard mass units.

                              Proposed grade placement rationale: For Grade 2, 'maximum resistance values' are interpreted as the total mass (in grams) a structure can hold before failure. This provides a concrete, countable measure of strength using standard units, aligning with the 'Estructuras' content and the 'Indicadores avanzados' of checking structure resistance, which explicitly mandates the use of kilograms and grams.

                              Evidence of mastery: The student systematically adds small, uniform masses (measured in grams) to their bridge model until it collapses or significantly deforms, and records the total mass it supported in grams.

                              Assessment task: Using the bridge model constructed previously, place a small container on its deck. Systematically add uniform masses (e.g., small bags of sand/rice, actual weights) of known gram values (e.g., 10g, 20g) one by one into the container until the bridge collapses or touches the ground. Record the total mass in grams supported by the bridge.

                              Access and participation: Use larger, easier-to-handle masses. Provide a counter or visual aid for summing masses. Allow peer assistance for adding masses and recording. Use a clear, simple recording sheet.

                              T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                              Evidence limitations: The evidence mentions 'Registro de valores de la máxima resistencia' and specifies 'kilogramos'/'gramos'. While the activity uses standard units, the evidence does not specify the testing methodology or the precision expected for Grade 2.

                              Safety: All construction activities should use child-safe tools (e.g., blunt scissors, non-toxic glue). When testing structures, ensure masses are added carefully to prevent objects from falling and causing injury. Adult supervision is required for all building and testing activities.

                              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                              Success criteria
                              • The student adds masses one by one in a controlled manner.
                              • The student accurately sums and records the total mass (in grams) supported.
                              • The student can describe what happened to the bridge when it reached its maximum resistance.
                              Teaching sequence
                              • Diagnosis: Ask students how they would know which bridge is stronger. Introduce the idea of testing strength by adding weight and measuring it in grams.
                              • Practice: Model the process of adding uniform known masses and summing their values. Provide a recording sheet. Students test their bridge models and record their findings in grams.
                              • Feedback: Compare results across different bridge designs. Discuss why some bridges held more mass than others. Emphasize the importance of fair testing and accurate measurement in grams.
                              Misconceptions
                              • The first mass added is the 'maximum resistance'.
                              • The bridge should never break, even with infinite mass.
                              • Any weight can be used, regardless of its known mass.
                              Required resources
                              • Self-made bridge models (from previous micro-skill)
                              • Small containers (e.g., paper cups)
                              • Uniform known masses (e.g., small bags of sand/rice labeled 10g, 20g, or actual gram weights)
                              • Recording sheets
                              • Pencils
                              External knowledge to verify
                              • Basic counting skills.
                              • Basic addition skills.
                              Unit limitations
                              • The provided source documents offer general guidance on 'designing and making' and specific content for El Salvador Grade 2. However, they lack detailed pedagogical instructions or explicit connections between international and local curricula.
                              • The evidence does not provide empirical data on the efficacy of these specific activities or their transferability across different educational contexts.
                              • The total estimated time for the four micro-skills is 210 minutes (3.5 hours). The source document 'sv-cyt' indicates a duration of 5 weeks for the entire Unit 5, which includes several other topics not covered in this proposal (energy transformers, animal care). The proposed time allocation may be disproportionately high for this subset of the unit's content, suggesting that these activities might be spread across multiple sessions or integrated with other subjects to manage the workload effectively within the broader unit context.

                                Sorting and descriptions

                                A classification rule is a human decision; do not label every sorting activity artificial intelligence.

                                Identify observable attributes of concrete objects relevant for classification.

                                micro-identify-object-attributes-0c04403f9cca2875 · data_and_ai · T0 · 30 instruction/practice minutes

                                Why this learning matters: Before students can sort objects, they must be able to perceive and articulate their characteristics. This micro-skill focuses on developing observational skills and vocabulary for identifying attributes, which is foundational for data classification. It is a prerequisite for any data analysis, digital or physical. The quote supports the idea of identifying 'characteristics' and 'attributes' as fundamental to data organization.

                                Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 2, students are developing their descriptive language and observational skills. Focusing on concrete objects makes the concept tangible and accessible, aligning with early childhood cognitive development.

                                Evidence of mastery: Students can list at least three distinct attributes for a given object.

                                Assessment task: Provide students with a collection of diverse objects (e.g., toys, classroom items). Ask them to choose one object and list at least three different attributes (e.g., color, size, material, shape).

                                Access and participation: Provide visual aids and tactile objects for students with visual impairments. Use sentence starters and word banks for students with language processing difficulties. Allow verbal or drawn responses.

                                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                Evidence limitations: The cited evidence refers to 'characteristics of two attributes' in the context of sorting data using Venn diagrams. Its application here is an analogical extension to identifying attributes of physical objects, recognizing that the underlying concept of 'attribute' is transferable. The quote does not explicitly mention the initial observation or identification of these attributes on physical objects.

                                Safety: Ensure all concrete objects used are age-appropriate, non-toxic, and free of small parts that could be choking hazards. Supervise students during hands-on activities to prevent misuse of materials.

                                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 1

                                Success criteria
                                • Student correctly identifies and names at least three distinct attributes.
                                • Student uses appropriate descriptive vocabulary.
                                • Student's listed attributes are accurate for the chosen object.
                                Teaching sequence
                                • Diagnosis: Show an object and ask 'What do you notice about this?' to gauge prior descriptive skills.
                                • Practice: Introduce various objects. Model identifying different attributes. Guide students to identify attributes of other objects in pairs or small groups.
                                • Feedback: Provide specific feedback on the clarity and accuracy of their identified attributes, encouraging the use of more precise vocabulary.
                                Misconceptions
                                • Confusing an object's function with its physical attribute.
                                • Using subjective descriptions instead of objective attributes (e.g., 'nice' instead of 'red').
                                Required resources
                                • Collection of diverse concrete objects (e.g., blocks, toys, natural items)
                                • Whiteboard or large paper for attribute lists
                                External knowledge to verify
                                • Basic vocabulary for colors, shapes, sizes, materials
                                • Observational skills (Science)
                                Unit limitations
                                • The provided evidence primarily focuses on digital literacy and information evaluation at higher cognitive levels, requiring significant adaptation and interpretation to derive foundational skills for Grade 2 physical object classification. While new evidence from Ontario Math has been incorporated, it still requires analogical extension from data representation/sorting in diagrams to physical object manipulation.
                                • The concept of 'data' for this grade level is limited to observable attributes of concrete objects, not abstract or digital data sets.
                                • The unit does not cover digital tools for sorting or classification, as the focus is on conceptual understanding through physical manipulation.

                                  Sort concrete objects into groups based on a single, explicit attribute.

                                  micro-sort-by-single-attribute-42ded0b0eaa0edb3 · data_and_ai · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                  Why this learning matters: This micro-skill moves from identifying attributes to using them for organization, a core concept in data literacy. Sorting by a single attribute is the simplest form of classification, directly supporting the unit's learning goal. The quote supports the idea of sorting data based on characteristics, which is directly applicable to sorting physical objects by their attributes.

                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 2 students are capable of following simple rules and applying them to concrete tasks. Sorting by one attribute reinforces logical thinking and prepares them for more complex classification tasks.

                                  Evidence of mastery: Students can correctly group a set of objects according to a specified attribute (e.g., all red objects together, all square objects together).

                                  Assessment task: Provide students with a mixed collection of objects. Instruct them to sort the objects into two or three groups based on a specific attribute (e.g., 'Sort all the objects that are blue into one pile, and all the objects that are not blue into another pile').

                                  Access and participation: Use larger, high-contrast objects for students with visual impairments. Provide clear, concise verbal instructions and visual examples. Allow students to physically manipulate objects at their own pace.

                                  T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                  Evidence limitations: The cited evidence refers to sorting data using Venn diagrams based on characteristics. Its application to physical object sorting is an analogical extension, recognizing that the underlying principle of organization by attribute is similar, but the context (diagrams vs. physical objects) is different. The quote does not explicitly mention sorting by attributes of physical objects.

                                  Safety: Ensure all concrete objects used are age-appropriate, non-toxic, and free of small parts that could be choking hazards. Supervise students during hands-on activities to prevent misuse of materials.

                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 1

                                  Success criteria
                                  • Student correctly identifies the specified attribute for sorting.
                                  • Student accurately places all objects into the correct groups.
                                  • Student can explain the rule used for sorting.
                                  Teaching sequence
                                  • Diagnosis: Present a pre-sorted group of objects and ask 'How are these grouped?' to check understanding of sorting rules.
                                  • Practice: Model sorting a set of objects by color. Provide students with different sets of objects and ask them to sort by various attributes (size, shape, material).
                                  • Feedback: Review sorted groups, asking students to articulate their sorting rule. Correct any misplacements and discuss why they don't fit the rule.
                                  Misconceptions
                                  • Sorting by multiple attributes simultaneously when only one is specified.
                                  • Inconsistently applying the sorting rule.
                                  Required resources
                                  • Multiple sets of diverse concrete objects (e.g., colored blocks, shape cutouts, different textured items)

                                  • Understanding of 'same' and 'different'

                                    Explain why a specific object does not fit into a given classification group.

                                    micro-explain-non-fitting-choice-4d8248c0545aa5a9 · data_and_ai · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                    Why this learning matters: This micro-skill addresses the 'explain a choice that does not fit' part of the learning goal. It requires students to analyze a classification rule and identify discrepancies, fostering critical thinking and early debugging skills. The quote supports the idea of sorting into 'complementary sets' (e.g., 'red – not red'), which directly relates to identifying why an object does not fit a specific characteristic of a group.

                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 2, students are developing their ability to reason and justify their answers. Explaining why something doesn't fit a rule helps them solidify their understanding of classification and logical consistency.

                                    Evidence of mastery: Students can articulate the specific attribute(s) that cause an object to be an outlier in a given group.

                                    Assessment task: Present students with a group of objects that are mostly sorted by a clear rule, but include one 'outlier' object. Ask students to identify the object that doesn't belong and explain why it doesn't fit the group's rule.

                                    Access and participation: Use objects with very distinct attributes for the outlier. Provide sentence frames for explanations (e.g., 'This object doesn't belong because it is ____, but the others are ____.'). Allow students to point or use gestures.

                                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                    Evidence limitations: The cited evidence refers to sorting data into complementary sets using a Carroll diagram. Its application here is an analogical extension to identifying an outlier in a physical group and explaining its non-conformance. While the concept of 'not red' directly supports identifying what doesn't fit, the original context is diagrammatic sorting, not direct explanation of an outlier in a physical set.

                                    Safety: Ensure all concrete objects used are age-appropriate, non-toxic, and free of small parts that could be choking hazards. Supervise students during hands-on activities to prevent misuse of materials.

                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 1

                                    Success criteria
                                    • Student correctly identifies the outlier object.
                                    • Student clearly states the classification rule of the main group.
                                    • Student accurately explains how the outlier object violates that rule.
                                    Teaching sequence
                                    • Diagnosis: Show a group with an obvious outlier and ask 'Which one is different and why?' to assess initial reasoning.
                                    • Practice: Present several pre-sorted groups, each with one outlier. Guide students to identify the outlier and articulate the rule and the reason for the mismatch.
                                    • Feedback: Discuss student explanations, emphasizing clear and precise language. Provide examples of good explanations and help students refine their own.
                                    Misconceptions
                                    • Identifying an object as an outlier based on a subjective preference rather than an objective attribute.
                                    • Stating a different, unrelated attribute of the outlier instead of how it violates the group's rule.
                                    Required resources
                                    • Multiple sets of concrete objects, each with one clear outlier based on a simple attribute
                                    External knowledge to verify
                                    • Understanding of logical negation (e.g., 'not red')
                                    • Basic comparison skills
                                    Unit limitations
                                    • The provided evidence primarily focuses on digital literacy and information evaluation at higher cognitive levels, requiring significant adaptation and interpretation to derive foundational skills for Grade 2 physical object classification. While new evidence from Ontario Math has been incorporated, it still requires analogical extension from data representation/sorting in diagrams to physical object manipulation.

                                      Patterns and repetition

                                      Use a known sequence as a unit; distinguish a repeated block from an unlimited loop.

                                      Identifies repeated sequences of instructions within a given algorithm or process.

                                      micro-identify-repeated-sequences-a87d5c4aebe27698 · computational_thinking · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                      Why this learning matters: This micro-skill is foundational for understanding repetition in algorithms. By first identifying patterns, students build the observational skills necessary to later abstract these patterns into repeatable blocks. It addresses the 'recognize repeated actions' part of the learning goal.

                                      Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 3, students are developing their pattern recognition skills across various subjects. Applying this to sequences of instructions helps bridge concrete observations to abstract computational concepts, aligning with the 'Primer Ciclo' focus on problem-solving and instruction sequences.

                                      Evidence of mastery: Students can accurately circle or highlight all instances of a repeating pattern in a visual or textual sequence of instructions.

                                      Assessment task: Given a series of illustrated steps for a simple task (e.g., building a tower with blocks, a dance routine), students will circle or highlight all identical sequences of 2-3 steps that repeat.

                                      Access and participation: Use visual aids (pictures, diagrams) for instructions. Allow verbal responses or pointing for students with fine motor skill challenges. Provide pre-printed sequences with large fonts.

                                      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                      Evidence limitations: The quote supports the concept of 'repetition in programs' but does not specify the age or method for teaching identification of such patterns outside of a programming context. This micro-skill is a pedagogical scaffolding step inferred from the goal of using repetition, rather than being directly described in the source curriculum.

                                      Safety: All activities are screen-free or use low-voltage, safe devices. No electrical hazards are present. Ensure adequate space for physical activities if algorithms are 'acted out'.

                                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                      Success criteria
                                      • Identifies all instances of a given repeating pattern within a provided sequence.
                                      • Distinguishes between unique steps and repeating sequences.
                                      • Explains why a particular sequence is considered a repetition.
                                      Teaching sequence
                                      • Diagnosis: Present a simple, short sequence with one obvious repetition. Ask students to describe what they see happening multiple times.
                                      • Practice: Provide several examples of daily routines or simple algorithms (e.g., making a sandwich, a short dance). Students work in pairs to identify and mark repeated actions.
                                      • Feedback: Review student work, discussing different interpretations of 'repetition' and guiding them towards identifying exact, consecutive sequences.
                                      Misconceptions
                                      • Confusing similar but not identical actions as repetitions.
                                      • Identifying only single repeated actions instead of a sequence of actions.
                                      • Overlooking repetitions if they are not immediately consecutive.
                                      Required resources
                                      • Worksheets with visual or textual sequences of instructions
                                      • Highlighters or colored pencils
                                      External knowledge to verify
                                      • Basic understanding of sequential order
                                      • Ability to compare and contrast visual or textual elements
                                      Unit limitations
                                      • The provided source material offers general guidance on computational thinking concepts but lacks specific pedagogical strategies or age-appropriate examples for teaching repetition to Grade 3 students in El Salvador.
                                      • The 'Primer Ciclo' reference is broad and requires significant interpretation to derive specific micro-skills for this grade level.
                                      • The unit does not cover unlimited loops, focusing specifically on bounded repetition as per the learning goal.
                                      • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on the teacher's ability to create engaging, context-rich activities that resonate with students' daily experiences.
                                      Publication corrections after model review
                                        Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                        • minor: The provided evidence from the England Computing curriculum ('use sequence, selection, and repetition in programs') mandates the use of repetition within programs. The proposed micro-skills focus on pre-programming, unplugged activities like identifying and representing patterns on paper. While these are pedagogically sound scaffolding steps, they are not directly supported by the cited evidence, which presumes a programming context. Suggested correction: Acknowledge that these micro-skills are pedagogical precursors to the explicit curriculum goal. Consider citing evidence related to pattern recognition in early years or mathematical education if available, or add a micro-skill that involves a simple block-based programming environment to directly meet the evidence's context.

                                        Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

                                        Sources: source-eng-computing-570e16e9afcbc1c7 (page HTML, Key stage 2, paragraph-p110-7c8559df04ecea31, original characters 0–51)

                                        Represents identified repeated sequences using a simplified notation or block structure.

                                        micro-represent-repeated-sequences-31db125dd509a5c6 · computational_thinking · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                        Why this learning matters: This skill moves students from identifying to abstracting patterns. Representing repetitions with a simplified notation is a direct step towards understanding bounded loops in programming, fulfilling the 'represent them as a bounded repeat' part of the learning goal. It also introduces the concept of abstraction.

                                        Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 3, students are ready to use symbolic representations for concepts. This skill aligns with 'la construcción de estrategias para su resolución, incluyendo su descomposición en pequeñas partes, utilizando secuencias ordenadas de instrucciones' by simplifying complex sequences.

                                        Evidence of mastery: Students can correctly replace a repeated sequence of instructions with a designated 'repeat' block or symbol, indicating the number of repetitions.

                                        Assessment task: Given a set of instructions with identified repeated sequences, students will rewrite the instructions using a 'repeat X times' block or a designated symbol to represent each repetition, ensuring the original meaning is preserved.

                                        Access and participation: Provide pre-made 'repeat' blocks or stickers for students to physically place. Use color-coding to link original sequences to their simplified representations. Allow verbal dictation of the simplified sequence.

                                        T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                        Evidence limitations: The quote broadly mentions 'repetition in programs' but does not detail the specific notation or pedagogical steps for introducing this concept to young learners. Using a 'repeat X times' block is a common and effective pedagogical tool, but it is an instructional design choice not explicitly mandated by the provided source evidence.

                                        Safety: All activities are screen-free or use low-voltage, safe devices. No electrical hazards are present. Ensure adequate space for physical activities if algorithms are 'acted out'.

                                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                        Success criteria
                                        • Replaces all identified repeated sequences with the correct 'repeat' notation.
                                        • Accurately specifies the number of repetitions for each block.
                                        • The simplified algorithm correctly reflects the original sequence of actions.
                                        Teaching sequence
                                        • Diagnosis: Show a simple sequence with a clear repetition. Ask students how they could write it shorter without losing information.
                                        • Practice: Introduce a 'repeat X times' block/symbol. Work through examples where students convert long sequences into shorter ones using this notation. Start with visual examples, then move to textual.
                                        • Feedback: Review student conversions, focusing on correct placement of the repeat block and accurate count. Discuss how this makes instructions clearer and shorter.
                                        Misconceptions
                                        • Incorrectly counting the number of repetitions.
                                        • Placing the 'repeat' block around non-repeating parts of the sequence.
                                        • Not understanding that the 'repeat' block contains the actions to be repeated.
                                        Required resources
                                        • Worksheets with sequences for simplification
                                        • Pre-printed 'repeat' blocks/symbols or drawing tools
                                        External knowledge to verify
                                        • Counting skills
                                        • Understanding of symbols representing actions
                                        Unit limitations
                                        • The provided source material offers general guidance on computational thinking concepts but lacks specific pedagogical strategies or age-appropriate examples for teaching repetition to Grade 3 students in El Salvador.
                                        • The 'Primer Ciclo' reference is broad and requires significant interpretation to derive specific micro-skills for this grade level.
                                        • The unit does not cover unlimited loops, focusing specifically on bounded repetition as per the learning goal.
                                        • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on the teacher's ability to create engaging, context-rich activities that resonate with students' daily experiences.

                                          Detects and corrects errors in algorithms that use repetition.

                                          micro-debug-algorithms-with-repetition-06d9660713974428 · computational_thinking · T0 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                          Why this learning matters: Debugging is a critical computational thinking skill. Applying it to algorithms with repetition reinforces understanding of how repetition works and helps students develop logical reasoning to trace execution paths, aligning with 'experimentando con el error como parte del proceso'.

                                          Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 3, students are encouraged to 'experimentar con el error como parte del proceso'. Debugging simple algorithms with repetition provides a concrete context for this, fostering problem-solving and critical thinking.

                                          Evidence of mastery: Students can identify and fix common errors in algorithms involving repetition, such as incorrect repetition counts or misplaced repeated blocks, resulting in the correct execution of the algorithm.

                                          Assessment task: Given a simple algorithm with a repeated block that contains one or two errors (e.g., wrong number of repetitions, incorrect action within the loop, or the loop placed in the wrong order), students will identify the errors and propose corrections to make the algorithm work as intended.

                                          Access and participation: Use physical manipulatives (e.g., blocks, cards) to represent algorithm steps and allow students to physically re-arrange them. Provide checklists of common errors. Pair students for collaborative debugging.

                                          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                          Evidence limitations: The quote supports the general idea of 'detect and correct errors in algorithms' but does not specifically mention debugging repetition structures. Applying general debugging principles to the specific topic of repetition is a logical extension of the curriculum, not an explicit requirement within the cited text.

                                          Safety: All activities are screen-free or use low-voltage, safe devices. No electrical hazards are present. Ensure adequate space for physical activities if algorithms are 'acted out'.

                                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                          Success criteria
                                          • Accurately identifies the location and nature of the error(s).
                                          • Proposes a correction that resolves the error(s).
                                          • Explains why the original algorithm was flawed and how the correction fixes it.
                                          Teaching sequence
                                          • Diagnosis: Present a simple algorithm with a subtle error in a repeat block. Ask students to 'act out' the algorithm and identify where it goes wrong.
                                          • Practice: Introduce common errors in repetition (off-by-one counts, wrong actions inside the loop). Provide several 'broken' algorithms for students to debug, working individually or in small groups.
                                          • Feedback: Discuss identified errors and proposed solutions. Emphasize systematic checking of each step and the impact of repetition on the overall outcome.
                                          Misconceptions
                                          • Believing that the error is always in the first step of the repetition.
                                          • Changing the entire algorithm instead of just the erroneous part.
                                          • Not understanding that a small error in a repeated block can have a large cumulative effect.
                                          Required resources
                                          • Worksheets with 'broken' algorithms
                                          • Physical manipulatives or visual aids for tracing algorithms
                                          External knowledge to verify
                                          • Logical reasoning
                                          • Attention to detail
                                          Unit limitations
                                          • The provided source material offers general guidance on computational thinking concepts but lacks specific pedagogical strategies or age-appropriate examples for teaching repetition to Grade 3 students in El Salvador.
                                          • The 'Primer Ciclo' reference is broad and requires significant interpretation to derive specific micro-skills for this grade level.
                                          • The unit does not cover unlimited loops, focusing specifically on bounded repetition as per the learning goal.
                                          • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on the teacher's ability to create engaging, context-rich activities that resonate with students' daily experiences.
                                          Publication corrections after model review
                                            Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                            • minor: The evidence for debugging ('detect and correct errors in algorithms and programs') is general. The proposal applies this to the specific context of repetition, which is a logical extension but not explicitly required by the source text. The proposal's own 'Evidence Limitations' section correctly identifies this gap. Suggested correction: This is a well-justified pedagogical choice, but for full evidence alignment, it's important to maintain the distinction that this specific application (debugging loops) is an interpretation rather than a direct mandate from the source curriculum.
                                            • minor: The assessment task for 'debug-algorithms-with-repetition' requires students to identify errors and propose corrections. However, the success criteria also include 'Explains why the original algorithm was flawed and how the correction fixes it.' This explanatory component is not explicitly captured in the assessment task description. Suggested correction: Align the assessment task with the success criteria by adding a requirement for students to briefly explain their reasoning, either verbally or in writing. For example: '...students will identify the errors, propose corrections, and explain why their change fixes the problem.'

                                            Identify examples of personal data that should be protected in digital environments.

                                            micro-identify-personal-data-0cc5155d1b555491 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                            Why this learning matters: Understanding what constitutes personal data is foundational for digital privacy. This micro-skill focuses on recognition before moving to protection strategies, ensuring students grasp the 'what' before the 'how'.

                                            Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 3, students are developing abstract thinking but still benefit from concrete examples. Identifying personal data aligns with their cognitive stage, preparing them for more complex privacy concepts.

                                            Evidence of mastery: Students correctly categorize information as personal or non-personal data in a given scenario.

                                            Assessment task: Given a set of cards with different types of information (e.g., name, favorite color, address, pet's name, phone number, school name), students sort them into two piles: 'Personal Data' and 'Not Personal Data'.

                                            Access and participation: Provide visual aids and real-world examples. Use simplified language and allow students to respond verbally or by pointing if writing is a barrier. Group work supports peer learning.

                                            T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                            Evidence limitations: The provided evidence from DigComp indicates the need to protect personal data but does not specify the exact age or grade at which students should learn to identify it, nor does it detail specific pedagogical approaches for this age group.

                                            Safety: All digital activities must be conducted under direct teacher supervision. Ensure that any online platforms used are age-appropriate and have robust privacy settings. Emphasize that students should never share personal information online without explicit permission from a trusted adult. For any collaborative activities, ensure content moderation is in place to prevent inappropriate sharing or communication.

                                            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                            Success criteria
                                            • Correctly sorts at least 80% of the information cards.
                                            • Can explain why at least two items are considered personal data.
                                            Teaching sequence
                                            • Diagnosis: Ask students to list information they think is 'private' about themselves.
                                            • Practice: Present various information types and lead a class discussion on whether each is personal data, providing clear definitions and examples.
                                            • Practice: Students work in small groups to categorize information cards.
                                            • Feedback: Review group categorizations, clarifying misconceptions and reinforcing correct understanding.
                                            Misconceptions
                                            • All information about me is personal data.
                                            • Only 'secret' information is personal data.
                                            • Information shared with friends is no longer personal data.
                                            Required resources
                                            • Set of information cards (physical or digital)
                                            • Whiteboard or projector for discussion
                                            External knowledge to verify
                                            • Social Studies: Understanding of personal identity and community.
                                            • Language Arts: Vocabulary related to personal information.
                                            Unit limitations
                                            • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on consistent reinforcement across all subjects and contexts, not just in technology class.
                                            • The rapid evolution of digital technologies means that specific tools and platforms may change, requiring continuous adaptation of teaching examples.
                                            • The provided evidence does not offer specific pedagogical strategies or age-appropriate content for El Salvador, requiring significant adaptation by the curriculum designer.
                                            • The unit does not cover all aspects of digital citizenship, focusing specifically on collaboration, privacy, and attribution. Other areas like online safety from strangers or cyberbullying would require separate units.
                                            Publication corrections after model review
                                              Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                              • minor: The success criteria for 'identify-personal-data' includes 'Can explain why at least two items are considered personal data.' This requires an 'explain' cognitive process, which is more advanced than the skill's stated verb 'Identify'. Suggested correction: Align the success criterion with the skill's verb. Change it to 'Can state a simple rule for why an item is personal data' or move the 'explain' component to a more advanced skill. Alternatively, acknowledge this as a stretch goal within the assessment.

                                              Explain the importance of consent before sharing personal data or digital artifacts.

                                              micro-explain-consent-for-sharing-7b50a872118ea568 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                              Why this learning matters: Consent is a core principle of digital citizenship. This skill moves beyond simple rule-following to understanding the 'why' behind asking permission, fostering empathy and responsible decision-making.

                                              Proposed grade placement rationale: Third graders can grasp the concept of fairness and respect for others' wishes. Explaining consent at this age builds a foundation for more complex discussions about digital rights and responsibilities.

                                              Evidence of mastery: Students articulate why asking permission is crucial before sharing digital content or personal information about others.

                                              Assessment task: Students are presented with a short story or scenario where a character wants to share a photo of a friend or a digital drawing made with a classmate. Students must explain to the character why they should ask for permission first, and what might happen if they don't.

                                              Access and participation: Use visual storyboards for scenarios. Allow students to express their explanations through drawing, verbal responses, or short written sentences. Provide sentence starters for explanations.

                                              T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                              Evidence limitations: The provided evidence from DigComp focuses on 'identifying simple ways to use and share personally identifiable information while protecting myself and others from damages'. While it supports the idea of safe sharing, it does not explicitly define 'consent' or directly evidence the pedagogical need to teach students to 'explain the importance of consent' at this age. The micro-skill interprets 'protecting myself and others' as requiring consent, which is a pedagogical inference.

                                              Safety: All digital activities must be conducted under direct teacher supervision. Ensure that any online platforms used are age-appropriate and have robust privacy settings. Emphasize that students should never share personal information online without explicit permission from a trusted adult. For any collaborative activities, ensure content moderation is in place to prevent inappropriate sharing or communication.

                                              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                              Success criteria
                                              • Clearly states that permission is needed from the person whose data/artifact is being shared.
                                              • Provides at least one reason why consent is important (e.g., respect, privacy, potential harm).
                                              • Suggests a polite way to ask for consent.
                                              Teaching sequence
                                              • Diagnosis: Ask students if they've ever shared something online or had something shared about them. Discuss feelings.
                                              • Practice: Use role-playing scenarios (e.g., sharing a drawing, a photo) to demonstrate asking for and giving/denying consent.
                                              • Practice: Discuss real-world examples (age-appropriate) of sharing without consent and its consequences.
                                              • Feedback: Provide constructive feedback on students' explanations, focusing on clarity and completeness.
                                              Misconceptions
                                              • If it's on my device, I can share it.
                                              • My friends won't mind if I share their picture.
                                              • It's okay to share if I don't say who it is (even if they are recognizable).
                                              Required resources
                                              • Scenario cards or story prompts
                                              • Whiteboard or chart paper
                                              External knowledge to verify
                                              • Social Studies: Concepts of respect, rights, and responsibilities.
                                              • Language Arts: Constructing clear explanations.
                                              Unit limitations
                                              • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on consistent reinforcement across all subjects and contexts, not just in technology class.
                                              • The rapid evolution of digital technologies means that specific tools and platforms may change, requiring continuous adaptation of teaching examples.
                                              • The provided evidence does not offer specific pedagogical strategies or age-appropriate content for El Salvador, requiring significant adaptation by the curriculum designer.
                                              • The unit does not cover all aspects of digital citizenship, focusing specifically on collaboration, privacy, and attribution. Other areas like online safety from strangers or cyberbullying would require separate units.
                                              Publication corrections after model review
                                                Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                • minor: The evidence cited for 'explain-consent-for-sharing' ('protecting myself and others from damages') is weakly linked to the specific concept of 'consent'. The proposal correctly identifies this as a pedagogical inference. Suggested correction: Locate evidence that more directly supports teaching 'consent' as a method of protection. If unavailable, retain the skill but explicitly label the connection as a pedagogical interpretation necessary for operationalizing the broader standard.

                                                Attribute sources for images, text, or ideas used in shared digital artifacts.

                                                micro-attribute-sources-digital-artifacts-9e6bac829d816ca9 · digital_citizenship · T1 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                Why this learning matters: Attribution teaches respect for intellectual property and the value of original work. It's a fundamental practice in digital creation and collaboration, fostering honesty and academic integrity from an early age.

                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: Third graders are beginning to create their own digital content and use resources from others. Introducing attribution now establishes good habits before they engage with more complex digital projects.

                                                Evidence of mastery: Students correctly cite the origin of external content (e.g., 'Image by [Artist Name]' or 'Idea from [Website]') within a simple digital project.

                                                Assessment task: Students create a simple digital presentation (e.g., using a slide show tool or a collaborative document) about a topic, incorporating one image and one piece of information found online. They must include a simple attribution for both.

                                                Access and participation: Provide pre-selected, easy-to-attribute resources. Offer templates for attribution. Allow verbal attribution if writing is a barrier. Pair students for collaborative work.

                                                T0 alternative and limits: Without digital devices, students can practice attributing sources for physical images or text cut from magazines/books, but they lose the direct experience of navigating digital environments and using digital tools for creation and attribution. The operational performance of attributing within a digital artifact is lost.

                                                Evidence limitations: The quote from 'eng-computing' refers to students being 'responsible, competent, confident and creative users of information'. While attribution is a component of responsible use, the source does not explicitly mention 'attribution' or provide specific guidance on teaching it. The connection is an inference based on the broader concept of responsible information use.

                                                Safety: All digital activities must be conducted under direct teacher supervision. Ensure that any online platforms used are age-appropriate and have robust privacy settings. Emphasize that students should never share personal information online without explicit permission from a trusted adult. For any collaborative activities, ensure content moderation is in place to prevent inappropriate sharing or communication.

                                                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                Success criteria
                                                • Includes a visual element and a piece of textual information from an external source.
                                                • Provides a clear, simple attribution for the image (e.g., 'Image by [Creator/Source]').
                                                • Provides a clear, simple attribution for the information (e.g., 'Fact from [Website/Book]').
                                                • The attribution is legible and understandable.
                                                Teaching sequence
                                                • Diagnosis: Ask students what they do when they find a cool picture or fact online that they want to use.
                                                • Practice: Demonstrate how to find simple sources for images and facts, and how to write a basic attribution (e.g., 'Image by...', 'Fact from...').
                                                • Practice: Students work on a guided digital project, practicing finding and attributing sources with teacher support.
                                                • Feedback: Review student projects, highlighting correct attributions and providing specific feedback on how to improve unclear ones.
                                                Misconceptions
                                                • If it's on the internet, it's free to use without giving credit.
                                                • I don't need to say where I got it if I changed it a little.
                                                • Only text needs attribution, not images.
                                                Required resources
                                                • Teacher device (T1) for demonstration
                                                • Shared devices (T2) or individual devices (T3) for student work
                                                • Internet access
                                                • Simple digital presentation/document tool (e.g., Google Slides, Jamboard, or similar)
                                                • Curated list of age-appropriate websites with clear source information
                                                External knowledge to verify
                                                • Language Arts: Understanding of authorship and citing sources.
                                                • Art: Respect for artists' work.
                                                • Basic operation of digital presentation/document tools
                                                Unit limitations
                                                • The effectiveness of these micro-skills relies heavily on consistent reinforcement across all subjects and contexts, not just in technology class.
                                                • The rapid evolution of digital technologies means that specific tools and platforms may change, requiring continuous adaptation of teaching examples.
                                                • The provided evidence does not offer specific pedagogical strategies or age-appropriate content for El Salvador, requiring significant adaptation by the curriculum designer.
                                                • The unit does not cover all aspects of digital citizenship, focusing specifically on collaboration, privacy, and attribution. Other areas like online safety from strangers or cyberbullying would require separate units.

                                                  • major: The evidence cited for the micro-skills 'attribute-sources-digital-artifacts' and 'communicate-respectfully-online' does not support the specific skills. The quote 'responsible, competent, confident and creative users of information' is too general to substantiate the teaching of attribution or respectful communication as distinct, required skills. Suggested correction: Find and cite evidence that specifically mentions attribution, giving credit, intellectual property, respectful communication, or netiquette as a learning objective for this age group. Alternatively, acknowledge that these skills are based on pedagogical best practices rather than direct evidence from the provided sources.

                                                  Communicate respectfully and constructively when collaborating on shared digital artifacts.

                                                  micro-communicate-respectfully-online-1ac4494c44b6170d · digital_citizenship · T2 · 70 instruction/practice minutes

                                                  Why this learning matters: Respectful communication is vital for effective collaboration and a positive digital environment. This skill focuses on practical application of social norms in a digital context, preventing common online conflicts.

                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: Third graders are actively engaged in group work and social interactions. Extending these social skills to digital collaboration is a natural progression, addressing potential challenges early.

                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students use polite language, offer helpful suggestions, and respond appropriately to feedback in a shared digital document or platform.

                                                  Assessment task: In a shared digital document (e.g., a collaborative story or presentation), students are given a task to review a peer's contribution and add a comment. The comment must offer a constructive suggestion or positive feedback using respectful language.

                                                  Access and participation: Provide sentence starters for comments. Use emojis to convey tone. Allow students to dictate comments if typing is difficult. Ensure clear guidelines for online interaction are posted.

                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Without shared digital devices, students can practice respectful communication during physical group projects, but they lose the specific context of digital collaboration, including understanding how tone can be misinterpreted online and using digital tools for feedback. The operational performance of communicating respectfully within a shared digital artifact is lost.

                                                  Evidence limitations: The quote from 'eng-computing' broadly refers to students being 'responsible, competent, confident and creative users of information'. While respectful communication is a key aspect of responsible digital use, the source does not explicitly mention 'respectful communication' or 'netiquette' or provide specific guidance on teaching these concepts. The connection is an inference based on the broader concept of responsible digital citizenship.

                                                  Safety: All digital activities must be conducted under direct teacher supervision. Ensure that any online platforms used are age-appropriate and have robust privacy settings. Emphasize that students should never share personal information online without explicit permission from a trusted adult. For any collaborative activities, ensure content moderation is in place to prevent inappropriate sharing or communication.

                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                  Success criteria
                                                  • Comment uses polite and positive language.
                                                  • Comment provides specific, actionable feedback or a helpful suggestion.
                                                  • Comment avoids personal attacks or overly critical language.
                                                  • Student responds appropriately to feedback received from peers.
                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                  • Diagnosis: Ask students about their experiences giving or receiving feedback, both online and offline.
                                                  • Practice: Discuss and model examples of respectful vs. disrespectful online comments. Introduce 'netiquette' rules.
                                                  • Practice: Students engage in a structured collaborative digital activity, practicing giving and receiving feedback under teacher supervision.
                                                  • Feedback: Review comments as a class (anonymously if preferred), discussing what worked well and areas for improvement.
                                                  Misconceptions
                                                  • I can say anything online because it's not face-to-face.
                                                  • Emojis make any message friendly.
                                                  • It's okay to be mean if someone else was mean first.
                                                  Required resources
                                                  • Shared devices (T2) or individual devices (T3)
                                                  • Internet access
                                                  • Collaborative digital platform (e.g., Google Docs, Jamboard, or similar)
                                                  • Examples of respectful and disrespectful comments
                                                  External knowledge to verify
                                                  • Social Studies: Understanding of social norms, empathy, and conflict resolution.
                                                  • Language Arts: Writing clear and polite messages.
                                                  • Basic operation of collaborative digital platforms

                                                    Materials and criteria

                                                    Keep the user need stable while changing one design feature.

                                                    Identify explicit design criteria for a simple product.

                                                    micro-identify-design-criteria-90910a2fb98958ff · design_process · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                    Why this learning matters: Understanding the constraints and goals of a design challenge is fundamental. Without clear criteria, material selection and evaluation become arbitrary. This skill establishes the baseline for purposeful design.

                                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: At this grade level, students are developing their ability to follow instructions and understand specific requirements. Identifying explicit criteria is a foundational step before they can generate or evaluate against them.

                                                    Evidence of mastery: Students correctly point out or state the given criteria for a product.

                                                    Assessment task: Given a simple product description and a list of potential criteria, students will circle the two criteria that are most relevant to the product's purpose.

                                                    Access and participation: Provide visual aids for product examples and criteria. Use simplified language and offer verbal repetition. Allow students to point, draw, or use pre-written cards to indicate criteria.

                                                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                    Evidence limitations: The provided text only mentions 'design criteria' as a concept to design and evaluate against, not how to teach students to identify them. The quote is a statement of purpose, not a pedagogical instruction.

                                                    Safety: All construction activities must be supervised. Ensure child-safe scissors and non-toxic glues are used. Emphasize careful handling of materials and tools. Clear instructions on tool usage and material handling should be provided before any practical work begins.

                                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                    Success criteria
                                                    • Students correctly identify the two most relevant criteria.
                                                    • Students can briefly explain why each chosen criterion is important for the product.
                                                    Teaching sequence
                                                    • Introduce the concept of 'criteria' using everyday examples (e.g., what makes a good backpack?).
                                                    • Present a simple product (e.g., a toy car) and discuss what makes it 'good' or 'bad'.
                                                    • Introduce two explicit criteria for the toy car (e.g., 'must roll smoothly', 'must be durable').
                                                    • Students practice identifying these criteria from a short text or verbal description.
                                                    • Provide feedback on their selections and reasoning.
                                                    Misconceptions
                                                    • Confusing personal preferences with objective design criteria.
                                                    • Difficulty distinguishing between a product's features and its performance criteria.
                                                    Required resources
                                                    • Pictures or physical examples of simple products
                                                    • Cards or worksheets with various criteria listed
                                                    External knowledge to verify
                                                      Unit limitations
                                                      • The provided source material for El Salvador (sv-cyt) focuses heavily on 'Medidas y fuerzas' (Measurements and forces) and specific SI units, which are largely outside the scope of a 'Materials and criteria' design process unit for technology education. Therefore, the micro-skills are primarily derived from the UK Design and Technology curriculum (eng-dt), adapted for the specified grade level and context.
                                                      • The evidence quotes are high-level statements of educational goals rather than detailed pedagogical instructions or empirical validations. They indicate what students 'should' do, not how to teach it or the expected developmental stages.
                                                      • The unit does not claim empirical validation or equivalence with international grade levels. The proposed activities are hypotheses for effective teaching within the given constraints.
                                                      • The unit focuses on low-voltage, non-electrical prototypes. Any mention of 'circuits' or 'multimeters' in the prompt is not applicable to this unit's scope and grade level.
                                                      • The total estimated time for micro-skills (210 minutes) plus setup (20 min), review (15 min), and assessment (30 min) totals 275 minutes (approximately 4.5 hours). While this is a significant time commitment for a single unit at this grade level, it is distributed across five micro-skills, allowing for flexibility in scheduling over several sessions or a week. The estimated times are pedagogical hypotheses and can be adjusted by the teacher based on student needs and pace.
                                                      Publication corrections after model review
                                                        Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                        • major: The unit's stated progression, 'Keep the user need stable while changing one design feature,' is not implemented or assessed in the micro-skills. The current sequence focuses on a single design-build-evaluate cycle, not on iterative changes to a design feature. Suggested correction: To align with the stated progression, add a micro-skill or modify an existing one to have students propose or make a single change to their prototype based on their evaluation, and then re-evaluate. For example, after 'evaluate-prototype-against-criteria', add a skill like 'propose-one-improvement'.
                                                        • minor: The assessment task for 'identify-design-criteria' requires students to circle the 'two most relevant criteria'. This implies a single correct answer, which may not be pedagogically sound as relevance can be subjective and context-dependent. The success criteria are more flexible, asking for two relevant criteria without specifying they must be the 'most' relevant. Suggested correction: Modify the assessment task to align with the success criteria. For example: 'Given a simple product description and a list of potential criteria, students will select two criteria they believe are relevant and explain their choices.' This assesses reasoning rather than finding a single 'correct' answer.

                                                        Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

                                                        Describe observable characteristics of common materials relevant to design criteria.

                                                        micro-describe-material-characteristics-2b7dce104c63cc1a · design_process · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                        Why this learning matters: Before selecting materials, students need to understand their properties. This skill provides the foundational knowledge of material characteristics, enabling informed choices in the subsequent selection process.

                                                        Proposed grade placement rationale: Third graders are developing observational skills and can categorize objects based on simple physical properties. This skill builds on that by focusing on properties relevant to design and construction.

                                                        Evidence of mastery: Students can accurately describe at least two relevant characteristics (e.g., 'stiff', 'flexible', 'absorbent') for three different common materials.

                                                        Assessment task: Given three different common materials (e.g., cardboard, fabric, craft stick), students will verbally describe or write down one or two key characteristics for each that might be important for building something.

                                                        Access and participation: Provide tactile examples of all materials. Use clear, descriptive language and visual cues for characteristics. Allow students to point to or physically demonstrate properties.

                                                        T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                        Evidence limitations: The quote mentions 'according to their characteristics' in the context of selecting materials, implying knowledge of characteristics, but does not provide pedagogical guidance on how to teach students to describe or understand these characteristics.

                                                        Safety: All construction activities must be supervised. Ensure child-safe scissors and non-toxic glues are used. Emphasize careful handling of materials and tools. Clear instructions on tool usage and material handling should be provided before any practical work begins.

                                                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                        Success criteria
                                                        • Students correctly identify and describe at least one relevant characteristic for each material (e.g., 'cardboard is stiff', 'fabric is soft and flexible').
                                                        • Students use appropriate descriptive vocabulary.
                                                        Teaching sequence
                                                        • Introduce various common materials (e.g., cardboard, paper, fabric, craft sticks, pipe cleaners).
                                                        • Lead a discussion on observable characteristics: 'How does it feel?', 'What can it do?', 'Is it strong/weak/flexible/stiff/absorbent?'.
                                                        • Conduct simple, hands-on experiments to test properties (e.g., try to bend, tear, or wet materials).
                                                        • Model describing characteristics using clear language.
                                                        • Students practice describing characteristics of new materials.
                                                        Misconceptions
                                                        • Confusing material characteristics with aesthetic qualities (e.g., 'pretty' instead of 'smooth').
                                                        • Believing all materials of the same type have identical properties (e.g., all paper is equally strong).
                                                        • Difficulty articulating observed properties with appropriate vocabulary.
                                                        Required resources
                                                        • Various common craft materials (cardboard, paper, fabric, craft sticks, pipe cleaners, plastic cups, etc.)
                                                        • Simple testing tools (e.g., small weights, water for absorbency tests)
                                                        External knowledge to verify
