                                                            Select appropriate materials based on their characteristics to meet specific design criteria.

                                                            micro-select-materials-by-characteristics-06b3b7f23db2b3a2 · design_process · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

                                                            Why this learning matters: This skill bridges the gap between understanding criteria and practical application. It requires students to observe and compare material properties, a critical step in the design process for creating functional prototypes.

                                                            Proposed grade placement rationale: Third graders can begin to understand basic material properties (e.g., strong, flexible, waterproof) and make informed choices when presented with clear criteria. This builds on their observational skills.

                                                            Evidence of mastery: Students choose materials that logically align with the given criteria and can articulate their reasoning.

                                                            Assessment task: Given a design challenge (e.g., 'build a small bridge that can hold a toy car') and a selection of materials (e.g., cardboard, craft sticks, pipe cleaners, fabric), students will choose the materials they would use and explain why each choice meets the criteria.

                                                            Access and participation: Provide tactile examples of all materials. Use clear, descriptive language for material characteristics. Allow students to physically manipulate materials during discussion and selection. Pair students for collaborative decision-making.

                                                            T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                            Evidence limitations: The quote broadly states 'select from and use a wide range of materials and components' but does not detail the pedagogical approach for teaching selection based on characteristics or criteria. It implies the act of selection, not the cognitive process behind it.

                                                            Safety: All construction activities must be supervised. Ensure child-safe scissors and non-toxic glues are used. Emphasize careful handling of materials and tools. Clear instructions on tool usage and material handling should be provided before any practical work begins.

                                                            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                            Success criteria
                                                            • Students select materials that are suitable for the bridge's purpose (e.g., strong for support).
                                                            • Students can articulate how the chosen material's characteristic (e.g., 'cardboard is stiff') helps meet a specific criterion (e.g., 'to hold the car').
                                                            Teaching sequence
                                                            • Review the identified design criteria from the previous micro-skill.
                                                            • Review the characteristics of various common materials.
                                                            • Model the process of selecting a material based on a specific criterion, explaining the 'why'.
                                                            • Students practice selecting materials for different parts of a simple design, justifying their choices.
                                                            • Provide feedback on their selections and reasoning.
                                                            Misconceptions
                                                            • Believing that more expensive or complex materials are always 'better'.
                                                            • Overlooking simple, readily available materials that could meet criteria.
                                                            • Not considering the interaction between different materials.
                                                            Required resources

                                                                Construct a simple prototype using selected materials and basic joining techniques.

                                                                micro-construct-simple-prototype-7eba27c62175e96a · design_process · T0 · 70 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                Why this learning matters: Hands-on construction is central to the design process. This skill allows students to translate their material choices into a tangible object, developing fine motor skills and problem-solving abilities related to assembly.

                                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: Third graders are capable of using simple tools and materials to build. This activity fosters creativity and practical skills, reinforcing the connection between design ideas and physical realization.

                                                                Evidence of mastery: Students produce a physical prototype that demonstrates an attempt to meet the design criteria, using appropriate tools and techniques.

                                                                Assessment task: Using the materials selected in the previous task, students will build their small bridge prototype. The prototype should be stable enough to stand on its own and attempt to span a small gap.

                                                                Access and participation: Provide adaptive tools if needed (e.g., easy-grip scissors). Offer pre-cut shapes or pre-prepared components for students with fine motor challenges. Use visual step-by-step instructions. Allow for collaborative building in small groups.

                                                                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                Evidence limitations: The quote mentions 'select from and use a range of tools and equipment to perform practical tasks' but does not specify the types of tasks, tools, or the level of complexity for construction.                                                                 Safety: All construction activities must be supervised. Ensure child-safe scissors and non-toxic glues are used. Emphasize careful handling of materials and tools. Clear instructions on tool usage and material handling should be provided before any practical work begins.

                                                                • The prototype is stable and can stand independently.

                                                                Teaching sequence
                                                                • Review basic safety rules for using tools and materials.
                                                                • Demonstrate various simple joining techniques (e.g., taping, gluing, slotting, folding).
                                                                • Provide examples of simple prototypes and discuss their construction.
                                                                • Students plan their prototype construction, possibly with a simple sketch.
                                                                • Students construct their prototypes, with teacher guidance and support.
                                                                • Encourage peer support and problem-solving during construction.
                                                                Misconceptions
                                                                • Believing a prototype must be perfect or fully functional.
                                                                • Underestimating the difficulty of joining materials securely.
                                                                • Not considering the structural integrity of their design during construction.

                                                                • Selected materials from previous task

                                                                  Evaluate a prototype's effectiveness against two explicit design criteria.

                                                                  micro-evaluate-prototype-against-criteria-b370c50bb88b889e · design_process · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                  Why this learning matters: Evaluation is crucial for iterative design. This skill teaches students to critically assess their work, identify areas for improvement, and connect their design choices back to the initial goals.

                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: At this age, students can begin to make simple judgments about whether something 'works' or 'doesn't work' based on clear rules. This introduces them to objective assessment in design.

                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students can articulate whether a prototype meets the criteria and provide specific observations to support their evaluation.

                                                                  Assessment task: Students will test a teacher-provided prototype (e.g., a simple bridge) against two explicit criteria (e.g., 'must hold a toy car', 'must span a gap'). They will then complete a simple checklist or verbal report, stating whether the prototype met the criteria and explaining why or why not, based on their observations.

                                                                  Access and participation: Provide clear visual cues for testing procedures. Use a simple 'yes/no' or 'met/not met' checklist for evaluation. Allow verbal responses or drawing to express findings. Pair students to assist with testing and observation.

                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                  Evidence limitations: The quote states 'evaluate their ideas and products against design criteria' but does not specify the method of evaluation, the complexity of criteria, or the expected depth of analysis for this age group. It's a general statement of the evaluation step.

                                                                  Safety: All construction activities must be supervised. Ensure child-safe scissors and non-toxic glues are used. Emphasize careful handling of materials and tools. Clear instructions on tool usage and material handling should be provided before any practical work begins.

                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                  • Students accurately report the outcome of the test (e.g., 'the bridge held the car' or 'the bridge collapsed').
                                                                  • Students connect the test outcome to the specific design criteria.
                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                  • Reiterate the two explicit design criteria for the prototype.
                                                                  • Demonstrate how to test a prototype against each criterion (e.g., 'Does it roll smoothly? Let's try it.').
                                                                  • Model how to record observations and make a judgment (e.g., 'It rolled smoothly, so it met that criterion.').
                                                                  • Students test a teacher-provided prototype and record their findings.
                                                                  • Students test their own prototypes and record their findings.
                                                                  • Facilitate a class discussion where students share their evaluations.
                                                                  • Provide feedback on the clarity and accuracy of their evaluations.
                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                  • Focusing only on aesthetics rather than functionality during evaluation.
                                                                  • Blaming the materials or tools instead of their own design choices.
                                                                  • Difficulty separating personal feelings about their creation from objective performance against criteria.
                                                                  Required resources
                                                                  • Completed prototypes (student-made and teacher-provided)
                                                                  • Items for testing (e.g., toy cars, small weights)
                                                                  • Simple evaluation checklists or recording sheets
                                                                  External knowledge to verify

                                                                      Identifies the main components of a simple mechanism.

                                                                      micro-identify-mechanism-components-efc2fb72cfda9594 · technical_systems · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                      Why this learning matters: Understanding the individual parts of a mechanism is foundational for comprehending how it functions as a whole. This skill builds a vocabulary for discussing technical systems.

                                                                      Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 3, students are developing their observational skills and ability to categorize. Identifying components of familiar mechanisms aligns with their cognitive development and introduces basic engineering concepts.

                                                                      Evidence of mastery: The student correctly draws a given simple mechanism and labels at least three distinct components (e.g., a lever, a wheel and axle, a slider).

                                                                      Assessment task: Given three different simple mechanisms (e.g., a toy car, a pair of scissors, a stapler), students will draw each mechanism and label at least three of its main components.

                                                                      Access and participation: Provide tactile models for students with visual impairments. Use large print labels and clear, simple language. Allow verbal responses instead of written ones if needed.

                                                                      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                      Evidence limitations: The quote supports the exploration of mechanisms and their examples, but does not explicitly detail the identification of individual components as a distinct skill. It implies this understanding as part of 'exploring and using'. The evidence is from Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is below the target Grade 3 (ages 8-9).

                                                                      Safety: All activities involving mechanisms must be conducted with appropriate supervision. Ensure that all materials and tools are age-appropriate and safe for student use. Emphasize the use of low-voltage batteries for any electrical components, and never use mains current. Students should be instructed on safe handling of tools like scissors (if used for 'Make' activities, though not explicitly in these micro-skills).

                                                                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                      Success criteria
                                                                      • Accurately draws the mechanism.
                                                                      • Labels at least three distinct components for each mechanism.
                                                                      • Uses appropriate terminology for the identified components.
                                                                      Teaching sequence
                                                                      • Diagnosis: Ask students to describe how a simple toy works. Observe if they mention specific parts.
                                                                      • Practice: Present various simple mechanisms. Guide students to disassemble and reassemble them (if safe and appropriate), identifying and naming parts like levers, wheels, axles, sliders. Use visual aids and physical models.
                                                                      • Feedback: Review student drawings and labels, providing corrective feedback on component identification and terminology. Encourage peer-to-peer explanation of parts.
                                                                      Misconceptions
                                                                        Required resources
                                                                        • Assortment of simple mechanisms (e.g., toy cars, scissors, staplers, door hinges, wheelbarrows)
                                                                        • Drawing materials (paper, pencils, crayons)
                                                                        External knowledge to verify
                                                                          Unit limitations
                                                                          • The primary evidence source, 'eng-dt', is for Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is below the proposed Grade 3 level (ages 8-9). This creates a mismatch between the evidence's intended age group and the target grade, requiring pedagogical interpretation to adapt to Grade 3.
                                                                          • The evidence cited for 'test-mechanism-change' refers to building and strengthening 'structures', not testing or modifying 'mechanisms' to alter their output. This is a significant inferential leap.
                                                                          • The 'sv-cyt' document focuses heavily on measurement, forces, and motion in a scientific context, which is referenced as external knowledge but does not directly support the 'technical systems' aspect of identifying and testing mechanism components, inputs, and outputs. It has been removed as a primary source for these skills.
                                                                          • The quotes are brief and require significant interpretation to derive specific observable performances related to inputs and outputs of mechanisms, especially for the 'testing changes' skill.
                                                                          • The curriculum design is based on pedagogical hypotheses for Grade 3 in El Salvador, not on empirical validation or international grade equivalency studies.

                                                                            • major: The primary evidence source, 'eng-dt', is for Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is significantly below the proposed Grade 3 level (ages 8-9). All skills in this unit are justified using this below-grade-level source. Suggested correction: Locate and cite evidence from a curriculum standard for Grade 3 or an equivalent age range (e.g., Key Stage 2) that supports the proposed skills.                                                                             Describes the function of individual components within a simple mechanism.

                                                                            micro-describe-component-function-1ec4fe5da0807fc0 · technical_systems · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                            Why this learning matters: Moving beyond mere identification, understanding component function is crucial for grasping how mechanisms work. This skill fosters analytical thinking about design and purpose.

                                                                            Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 3 students can begin to connect parts to their roles. This skill builds on component identification and prepares them for analyzing inputs and outputs, aligning with the unit's learning goal.

                                                                            Evidence of mastery: The student accurately explains the purpose of at least two identified components in a simple mechanism, relating their action to the mechanism's overall operation.

                                                                            Assessment task: Students will choose one simple mechanism, identify two of its components, and write or verbally explain what each component does and how it contributes to the mechanism's overall function.

                                                                            Access and participation: Provide sentence starters or visual cues for describing functions. Allow students to demonstrate the function physically if verbal explanation is challenging. Pair students for collaborative explanation.

                                                                            T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                            Evidence limitations: The quote emphasizes designing 'functional products', which implies an understanding of how parts contribute to function. However, it does not directly address the skill of describing individual component functions. The evidence is from Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is below the target Grade 3 (ages 8-9).

                                                                            Safety: All activities involving mechanisms must be conducted with appropriate supervision. Ensure that all materials and tools are age-appropriate and safe for student use. Emphasize the use of low-voltage batteries for any electrical components, and never use mains current. Students should be instructed on safe handling of tools like scissors (if used for 'Make' activities, though not explicitly in these micro-skills).

                                                                            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                            Success criteria
                                                                            • Clearly identifies two components of the chosen mechanism.
                                                                            • Accurately describes the function of each identified component.
                                                                            • Explains how the component's function contributes to the mechanism's purpose.
                                                                            Teaching sequence
                                                                            • Diagnosis: Ask students to explain 'why' a specific part of a mechanism is there. Observe if they can articulate its purpose.
                                                                            • Practice: After identifying components, discuss with students what each part 'does'. Use questions like 'What would happen if this part wasn't there?' or 'How does this part help the mechanism work?'. Encourage experimentation with mechanisms.
                                                                            • Feedback: Review explanations, highlighting clear descriptions and correcting misunderstandings about component roles. Facilitate group discussions where students justify their functional descriptions.
                                                                            Misconceptions
                                                                            • Confusing a component's function with its name (e.g., calling a lever 'the pushing part').
                                                                            • Overlooking small but critical components.
                                                                            • Believing all parts are equally important.
                                                                            • Attributing a component's function to the entire mechanism.
                                                                            • Confusing the input action with the component's function (e.g., 'the handle's function is to be pushed').
                                                                            • Believing a component has only one function, even if it has multiple.
                                                                            Required resources
                                                                            • Simple mechanisms (same as previous skill)
                                                                            • Worksheets or whiteboards for writing/drawing explanations
                                                                            External knowledge to verify
                                                                            • Basic vocabulary for common objects (e.g., wheel, handle, blade)
                                                                            • Basic cause-and-effect relationships
                                                                            Unit limitations
                                                                            • The primary evidence source, 'eng-dt', is for Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is below the proposed Grade 3 level (ages 8-9). This creates a mismatch between the evidence's intended age group and the target grade, requiring pedagogical interpretation to adapt to Grade 3.
                                                                            • The evidence cited for 'test-mechanism-change' refers to building and strengthening 'structures', not testing or modifying 'mechanisms' to alter their output. This is a significant inferential leap.
                                                                            • The 'sv-cyt' document focuses heavily on measurement, forces, and motion in a scientific context, which is referenced as external knowledge but does not directly support the 'technical systems' aspect of identifying and testing mechanism components, inputs, and outputs. It has been removed as a primary source for these skills.
                                                                            • The quotes are brief and require significant interpretation to derive specific observable performances related to inputs and outputs of mechanisms, especially for the 'testing changes' skill.
                                                                            • The curriculum design is based on pedagogical hypotheses for Grade 3 in El Salvador, not on empirical validation or international grade equivalency studies.

                                                                              Identifies the inputs and outputs of a simple mechanism.

                                                                              micro-identify-mechanism-inputs-outputs-cee059d902a7bb80 · technical_systems · T0 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                              Why this learning matters: Understanding inputs and outputs is fundamental to systems thinking. It allows students to see mechanisms as transformers of energy or motion, a core concept in technical systems.

                                                                              Proposed grade placement rationale: This skill directly addresses the unit's learning goal for Grade 3. It requires students to synthesize their understanding of components and their functions to analyze the overall system behavior.

                                                                              Evidence of mastery: The student correctly identifies at least one input and one output for a given simple mechanism, explaining what goes in and what comes out.

                                                                              Assessment task: For three different simple mechanisms, students will draw a simple diagram showing the mechanism and use arrows to indicate the input (what makes it work) and the output (what it does).

                                                                              Access and participation: Use color-coding for inputs and outputs in diagrams. Provide physical demonstrations and allow students to manipulate mechanisms to feel the input and observe the output. Offer sentence frames for verbal descriptions.

                                                                              T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                              Evidence limitations: The quote mentions 'mechanisms' but does not explicitly define or refer to 'inputs' and 'outputs'. The connection is inferential, as understanding how mechanisms 'work' implies understanding their cause and effect. The evidence is from Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is below the target Grade 3 (ages 8-9).

                                                                              Safety: All activities involving mechanisms must be conducted with appropriate supervision. Ensure that all materials and tools are age-appropriate and safe for student use. Emphasize the use of low-voltage batteries for any electrical components, and never use mains current. Students should be instructed on safe handling of tools like scissors (if used for 'Make' activities, though not explicitly in these micro-skills).

                                                                              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                              Success criteria
                                                                              • Accurately draws a representation of each mechanism.
                                                                              • Correctly identifies and labels at least one input for each mechanism.
                                                                              • Correctly identifies and labels at least one output for each mechanism.
                                                                              Teaching sequence
                                                                              • Diagnosis: Ask students 'What do you do to make this work?' and 'What happens when it works?'. Observe their initial understanding of cause and effect.
                                                                              • Practice: Introduce the terms 'input' and 'output'. Use various mechanisms and guide students to identify what they put into the mechanism (e.g., pushing, turning, pulling) and what the mechanism produces (e.g., cutting, moving, lifting). Use flowcharts or simple diagrams.
                                                                              • Feedback: Review diagrams and explanations. Correct any confusion between internal processes and external inputs/outputs. Emphasize that inputs are what we provide, and outputs are the results.
                                                                              Misconceptions
                                                                              • Confusing the mechanism itself with its output.
                                                                              • Identifying an internal component's action as an input or output.
                                                                              • Believing that only human interaction can be an input.
                                                                              Required resources
                                                                              • Simple mechanisms (e.g., pencil sharpener, toy car, simple pulley system)
                                                                              • Drawing materials, large paper or whiteboard
                                                                              External knowledge to verify
                                                                              • Basic understanding of cause and effect
                                                                              Unit limitations
                                                                              • The primary evidence source, 'eng-dt', is for Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is below the proposed Grade 3 level (ages 8-9). This creates a mismatch between the evidence's intended age group and the target grade, requiring pedagogical interpretation to adapt to Grade 3.
                                                                              • The evidence cited for 'test-mechanism-change' refers to building and strengthening 'structures', not testing or modifying 'mechanisms' to alter their output. This is a significant inferential leap.
                                                                              • The 'sv-cyt' document focuses heavily on measurement, forces, and motion in a scientific context, which is referenced as external knowledge but does not directly support the 'technical systems' aspect of identifying and testing mechanism components, inputs, and outputs. It has been removed as a primary source for these skills.
                                                                              • The quotes are brief and require significant interpretation to derive specific observable performances related to inputs and outputs of mechanisms, especially for the 'testing changes' skill.
                                                                              • The curriculum design is based on pedagogical hypotheses for Grade 3 in El Salvador, not on empirical validation or international grade equivalency studies.

This connection is inferred from 'explore and use'.                                                                                 Tests the effect of a simple change on a mechanism's output and explains how it affects the input-output relationship.

                                                                                micro-test-mechanism-change-011e1fc8fe2259c1 · technical_systems · T0 · 90 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                Why this learning matters: This skill introduces experimental design and problem-solving. By modifying and testing, students gain a deeper understanding of how design choices impact function, fostering an engineering mindset.

                                                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 3, students can begin to manipulate variables in a controlled way. This skill encourages active learning and critical thinking about how changes affect system performance, directly supporting the unit's learning goal.

                                                                                Evidence of mastery: The student performs a simple modification to a mechanism (e.g., changing a lever's pivot point, adding weight), observes and describes the resulting change in its output, and explains how this change affected the relationship between the input and the output.

                                                                                Assessment task: Given a simple lever mechanism, students will be asked to make it easier to lift a specific object. They must make one change (e.g., move the fulcrum) and then demonstrate and explain how their change affected the effort needed (input) and the lifting action (output).

                                                                                Access and participation: Provide pre-made components for easy modification. Use visual aids to illustrate changes and their effects. Allow for collaborative testing and observation. Provide structured recording sheets.

                                                                                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                Evidence limitations: The quote refers to 'building structures' and 'exploring how they can be made stronger, stiffer and more stable', which implies testing changes. However, it does not explicitly mention testing changes in 'mechanisms' or their 'outputs' in the context of inputs and outputs. This citation is a weak inference for the specific skill of testing changes in mechanisms to alter their output. The evidence is from Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is below the target Grade 3 (ages 8-9).

                                                                                Safety: All activities involving mechanisms must be conducted with appropriate supervision. Ensure that all materials and tools are age-appropriate and safe for student use. Emphasize the use of low-voltage batteries for any electrical components, and never use mains current. Students should be instructed on safe handling of tools like scissors (if used for 'Make' activities, though not explicitly in these micro-skills).

                                                                                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                Success criteria
                                                                                • Proposes a single, testable change to the mechanism.
                                                                                • Successfully implements the proposed change.
                                                                                • Observes and accurately describes the effect of the change on the mechanism's output (e.g., easier/harder to lift).
                                                                                • Explains why the change had that effect, linking it to the input-output relationship.
                                                                                Teaching sequence
                                                                                • Diagnosis: Ask students 'What could we change to make this work better/differently?'. Observe their initial ideas about modification.
                                                                                • Practice: Provide simple mechanisms (e.g., levers, simple ramps). Guide students to identify a variable they can change (e.g., fulcrum position, ramp angle). Help them predict the outcome, make the change, observe, and record results. Emphasize changing only one thing at a time.
                                                                                • Feedback: Discuss observations and explanations. Highlight the connection between the change made and the observed effect. Correct any misinterpretations of cause and effect. Encourage students to refine their explanations.
                                                                                Misconceptions
                                                                                • Changing multiple variables at once, making it hard to isolate the effect.
                                                                                • Misinterpreting the cause of the observed change.
                                                                                • Believing that all changes will lead to an improvement.
                                                                                Required resources
                                                                                • Simple mechanisms designed for modification (e.g., lever kits, ramp kits, simple pulley systems)
                                                                                • Objects of varying weights
                                                                                • Measuring tools (e.g., rulers, spring scales if appropriate and safe)
                                                                                • Recording sheets
                                                                                External knowledge to verify
                                                                                • Basic understanding of force and motion (e.g., pushing, pulling, lifting)
                                                                                • Observation skills
                                                                                Unit limitations
                                                                                • The primary evidence source, 'eng-dt', is for Key Stage 1 (ages 5-7), which is below the proposed Grade 3 level (ages 8-9). This creates a mismatch between the evidence's intended age group and the target grade, requiring pedagogical interpretation to adapt to Grade 3.
                                                                                • The evidence cited for 'test-mechanism-change' refers to building and strengthening 'structures', not testing or modifying 'mechanisms' to alter their output. This is a significant inferential leap.
                                                                                • The 'sv-cyt' document focuses heavily on measurement, forces, and motion in a scientific context, which is referenced as external knowledge but does not directly support the 'technical systems' aspect of identifying and testing mechanism components, inputs, and outputs. It has been removed as a primary source for these skills.
                                                                                • The quotes are brief and require significant interpretation to derive specific observable performances related to inputs and outputs of mechanisms, especially for the 'testing changes' skill.
                                                                                • The curriculum design is based on pedagogical hypotheses for Grade 3 in El Salvador, not on empirical validation or international grade equivalency studies.

                                                                                  Data and representations

                                                                                  Distinguish the objects, their recorded attributes and the question being answered.

                                                                                  Identify relevant attributes of objects for data collection to answer a specific question.

                                                                                  micro-identify-data-attributes-3b093a67356adc39 · data_and_ai · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                  Why this learning matters: This skill is foundational for data collection. Students need to understand that data is not just random numbers but specific characteristics (attributes) of things, chosen because they help answer a question. This prevents collecting irrelevant information.

                                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: At 3rd grade, students are developing logical reasoning and can categorize. Focusing on observable attributes for simple objects aligns with their cognitive development and the unit's goal of collecting small datasets.

                                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students correctly list 3-5 observable attributes for a given set of objects that directly relate to a posed question.

                                                                                  Assessment task: Given a collection of 5-7 classroom objects (e.g., different colored pencils, erasers, rulers) and a question (e.g., 'What is the most common color of writing tool in our classroom?'), students will list the attributes they would record for each object to answer the question.

                                                                                  Access and participation: Provide visual aids for objects and questions. Allow students to verbally state attributes before writing. Pair students for collaborative identification.

                                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                  Evidence limitations: The provided evidence 'answer questions of interest' broadly supports the purpose of data collection but does not explicitly detail the process of identifying relevant attributes for data collection. This skill is a pedagogical prerequisite derived from the unit's learning goal and progression rather than directly from external source documents.

                                                                                  Safety: All activities involve low-risk classroom materials and digital tools. Ensure proper supervision during digital activities to prevent misuse or access to inappropriate content. Emphasize responsible handling of shared devices. When collecting data, ensure it is non-personal and respects privacy.

                                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                                  • Lists attributes that are directly observable on the objects.
                                                                                  • Lists attributes that are relevant to answering the specific question.
                                                                                  • Avoids listing personal opinions or non-observable characteristics.
                                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                                  • Diagnosis: Present a simple question and a set of objects. Ask students what information they would write down about each object. Observe if they focus on relevant attributes.
                                                                                  • Practice: Model identifying attributes for a different set of objects and question. Guide students through identifying attributes for several examples, providing immediate feedback.
                                                                                  • Feedback: Review student lists, highlighting attributes that are relevant and explaining why others might not be. Discuss common errors like subjectivity or irrelevance.
                                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                                  • All information about an object is data.
                                                                                  • Data must be numerical.
                                                                                  • Attributes are subjective opinions.
                                                                                  Required resources
                                                                                  • Various classroom objects (e.g., pencils, books, toys)
                                                                                  • Whiteboard or large paper for listing attributes
                                                                                  External knowledge to verify
                                                                                  • Basic classification and observation skills (Science)
                                                                                  Unit limitations
                                                                                  • The provided source material offers general guidance on data literacy and digital skills but lacks specific pedagogical strategies or detailed content for 3rd-grade data collection and representation.
                                                                                  • The 'credibility and reliability' aspect mentioned in DigComp is considered too advanced for 3rd grade in this unit, as the focus is on collecting primary, observable data rather than evaluating external sources.
                                                                                  • The unit assumes basic digital literacy skills (e.g., using a keyboard, mouse, or touch screen) which may require additional foundational support for some students.
                                                                                  • The estimated minutes are pedagogical hypotheses and do not account for potential classroom management, unexpected technical issues, or individual student learning pace variations. The total estimated pedagogical time for the four micro-skills is 250 minutes (45+60+75+70), which does not include the stated 30 minutes for assessment, 20 for setup, or time for transitions and potential reteaching. This suggests a significant time commitment that may exceed typical allocations for a single unit.
                                                                                  Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                    Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                    • minor: The total estimated pedagogical time for the four micro-skills is 250 minutes, which is a significant time commitment for a single unit at this grade level. The proposal itself notes this in the limitations section. Suggested correction: Consider breaking the unit into two smaller units (e.g., one on data collection and another on digital organization/representation) or reducing the estimated time for some skills, particularly 'organize-data-simple-digital' (75 min) which primarily involves data entry.

                                                                                    Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

                                                                                    Sources: source-ontario-math-751e847718f44c0d (page 204, Mathematics, Grade 3, paragraph-p2215-cc40466fa72aaf02, original characters 66–94)

                                                                                    Collect a small, non-personal dataset by recording identified attributes from multiple objects.

                                                                                    micro-collect-simple-data-7954451c5e5b67fc · data_and_ai · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                    Why this learning matters: This skill moves from identifying what to collect to the actual act of collection. It emphasizes accuracy and systematic recording, which are crucial for reliable data analysis later. The 'non-personal' aspect ensures ethical considerations are implicitly introduced.

                                                                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: At 3rd grade, students can follow simple instructions for recording information. Practicing with non-personal data avoids privacy issues and keeps the focus on the data itself, not sensitive information.

                                                                                    Evidence of mastery: Students accurately record the chosen attributes for at least 10 objects in a simple table or list format.

                                                                                    Assessment task: Using the attributes identified in the previous task, students will collect data for 10-15 classroom objects, recording each object's attributes in a simple table (e.g., columns for 'Object', 'Color', 'Material').

                                                                                    Access and participation: Provide pre-formatted tables with clear headings. Allow students to use checkmarks or simple drawings if writing is a barrier. Offer verbal prompts for each object.

                                                                                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                    Evidence limitations: The provided evidence 'collect data through observations' supports the act of data collection but does not specify the pedagogical approach for collecting a small non-personal dataset or the use of tables. This skill is derived directly from the unit's learning goal rather than from external source documents.

                                                                                    Safety: All activities involve low-risk classroom materials and digital tools. Ensure proper supervision during digital activities to prevent misuse or access to inappropriate content. Emphasize responsible handling of shared devices. When collecting data, ensure it is non-personal and respects privacy.

                                                                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                    Success criteria
                                                                                    • Records data for all specified objects.
                                                                                    • Records the correct attribute value for each object.
                                                                                    • Uses a consistent format (e.g., table rows/columns) for recording.
                                                                                    Teaching sequence
                                                                                    • Diagnosis: Provide a pre-filled table with some errors or omissions. Ask students to identify what's wrong or missing, assessing their understanding of systematic recording.
                                                                                    • Practice: Model how to systematically go through each object and record its attributes in a table. Have students practice collecting data for a small set of objects, providing immediate feedback on accuracy and consistency.
                                                                                    • Feedback: Review collected datasets, pointing out discrepancies, missing data, or inconsistent entries. Emphasize the importance of careful observation and recording.
                                                                                    Misconceptions
                                                                                    • It's okay to guess if unsure about an attribute.
                                                                                    • The order of recording doesn't matter.
                                                                                    • Only 'important' objects need data recorded.
                                                                                    Required resources
                                                                                    • Classroom objects (same as previous task)
                                                                                    • Paper or digital template for data recording (table format)
                                                                                    External knowledge to verify
                                                                                    • Basic writing and organizational skills
                                                                                    Unit limitations
                                                                                    • The provided source material offers general guidance on data literacy and digital skills but lacks specific pedagogical strategies or detailed content for 3rd-grade data collection and representation.
                                                                                    • The 'credibility and reliability' aspect mentioned in DigComp is considered too advanced for 3rd grade in this unit, as the focus is on collecting primary, observable data rather than evaluating external sources.

                                                                                      Organize collected data in a simple digital environment for clarity and retrieval.

                                                                                      micro-organize-data-simple-digital-2c1634ec3f25c4f0 · data_and_ai · T2 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                      Why this learning matters: This skill introduces students to digital tools for data management, moving beyond paper-based collection. It reinforces the importance of structured organization for future analysis and retrieval, a core concept in data literacy.

                                                                                      Proposed grade placement rationale: At 3rd grade, students can begin to interact with simple digital interfaces. Using shared devices (T2) allows for guided practice and collaboration, making the transition to digital organization manageable.

                                                                                      Evidence of mastery: Students successfully input their collected data into a shared digital spreadsheet or simple database, with correct labels and entries, demonstrating basic organization.

                                                                                      Assessment task: Students will transfer their previously collected paper-based data into a shared digital spreadsheet (e.g., Google Sheets, Excel) or a simple online form, ensuring each attribute is in its correct column/field and all entries are accurate.

                                                                                      Access and participation: Provide clear, step-by-step visual instructions for using the digital tool. Use large fonts and high-contrast colors. Allow for verbal input if typing is a barrier, with teacher or peer assistance.

                                                                                      T0 alternative and limits: Without digital devices, students can organize data using physical cards, sorting them into labeled bins or drawing large tables on poster paper. This loses the experience of digital data entry and the benefits of easy digital retrieval and manipulation.

                                                                                      Evidence limitations: The evidence supports the general concept of organizing, storing, and retrieving data in digital environments but does not specify the complexity or type of digital tool appropriate for 3rd grade, nor does it detail pedagogical approaches.

                                                                                      Safety: All activities involve low-risk classroom materials and digital tools. Ensure proper supervision during digital activities to prevent misuse or access to inappropriate content. Emphasize responsible handling of shared devices. When collecting data, ensure it is non-personal and respects privacy.

                                                                                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                      Success criteria
                                                                                      • All data from the paper collection is accurately entered into the digital tool.
                                                                                      • Data is organized into appropriate columns/fields with clear labels.
                                                                                      • No data is missing or misplaced.
                                                                                      Teaching sequence
                                                                                      • Diagnosis: Show an example of poorly organized digital data and ask students to identify problems. Show a well-organized example and ask them to explain why it's better.
                                                                                      • Practice: Model how to create a simple digital table/form and input data. Guide students in pairs or small groups to input their own collected data, providing support for navigation and data entry.
                                                                                      • Feedback: Review student digital datasets, providing specific feedback on accuracy, formatting, and completeness. Demonstrate how proper organization makes data easier to read and use.
                                                                                      Misconceptions
                                                                                      • Digital organization is just like writing on paper.
                                                                                      • The computer will automatically fix my mistakes.
                                                                                      • It doesn't matter if data is in the wrong column.
                                                                                      Required resources
                                                                                      • Shared computers or tablets (T2) with spreadsheet software or online form access
                                                                                      • Previously collected paper-based data
                                                                                      External knowledge to verify
                                                                                      • Basic computer navigation (opening applications, typing)
                                                                                      • Understanding of rows and columns
                                                                                      Unit limitations
                                                                                      • The provided source material offers general guidance on data literacy and digital skills but lacks specific pedagogical strategies or detailed content for 3rd-grade data collection and representation.

                                                                                        Sources: source-digcomp-d371a960318a1a45 (page 18, Information and data literacy, paragraph-p104-29afeb5525927063, original characters 101–150)

                                                                                        Represent collected data visually using simple charts or graphs to answer a specific question.

                                                                                        micro-represent-data-visual-daf498313cb667f9 · data_and_ai · T1 · 70 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                        Why this learning matters: Visual representation is key to making data understandable and drawing conclusions. This skill teaches students how to transform raw data into a meaningful format, directly addressing the unit's learning goal of choosing a clear representation to answer a question.

                                                                                        Proposed grade placement rationale: At 3rd grade, students are familiar with basic counting and comparison. Simple bar graphs and pictographs are accessible and allow them to visually interpret data without complex calculations.

                                                                                        Evidence of mastery: Students create a simple bar graph or pictograph that accurately reflects their collected data and clearly answers the initial question.

                                                                                        Assessment task: Using their organized digital data, students will create a simple bar graph or pictograph (either on paper or using a teacher-guided digital tool) that visually summarizes the data and helps answer the initial question (e.g., 'What is the most common color of writing tool?'). For paper-based creation, the teacher will project the digital data or provide a printout.

                                                                                        Access and participation: Provide graph paper with pre-drawn axes or templates. Use large, clear examples. Allow students to use physical blocks or objects to build a 'graph' before drawing. Teacher can project and guide digital graph creation.

                                                                                        T0 alternative and limits: Without digital tools, students can create bar graphs or pictographs manually on paper or poster board. This is a viable alternative, but it loses the efficiency and potential for dynamic exploration offered by digital graphing tools.

                                                                                        Evidence limitations: The evidence 'utilización de recursos digitales para la' supports the use of digital resources for representation but does not specify types of graphs, the level of complexity appropriate for 3rd grade, or specific pedagogical approaches for teaching this skill.

                                                                                        Safety: All activities involve low-risk classroom materials and digital tools. Ensure proper supervision during digital activities to prevent misuse or access to inappropriate content. Emphasize responsible handling of shared devices. When collecting data, ensure it is non-personal and respects privacy.

                                                                                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                        Success criteria
                                                                                        • Graph accurately reflects the collected data.
                                                                                        • Graph has a clear title and labeled axes/categories.
                                                                                        • The chosen representation effectively highlights the answer to the question.
                                                                                        • Uses appropriate scale for the data.
                                                                                        Teaching sequence
                                                                                        • Diagnosis: Show various simple graphs (some good, some with errors). Ask students to identify which graph is easiest to understand and why, and what makes other graphs confusing.
                                                                                        • Practice: Model how to create a simple bar graph from a dataset, emphasizing labels, scale, and clarity. Guide students through creating their own graphs, providing feedback on accuracy and visual effectiveness.
                                                                                        • Feedback: Review student graphs, discussing how well they communicate the data and answer the question. Highlight examples of clear representation and suggest improvements for others.
                                                                                        Misconceptions
                                                                                        • Graphs are just pretty pictures, not tools for understanding.
                                                                                        • The order of bars on a graph doesn't matter.
                                                                                        • More colors make a graph better, even if confusing.
                                                                                        Required resources
                                                                                        • Organized digital data (from previous task)
                                                                                        • Graph paper or digital graphing tool (e.g., simple online chart maker, spreadsheet software)
                                                                                        • Colored pencils or markers
                                                                                        External knowledge to verify
                                                                                        • Basic counting and comparison (Mathematics)
                                                                                        • Understanding of categories
                                                                                        Unit limitations
                                                                                        • The provided source material offers general guidance on data literacy and digital skills but lacks specific pedagogical strategies or detailed content for 3rd-grade data collection and representation.
                                                                                        • The 'credibility and reliability' aspect mentioned in DigComp is considered too advanced for 3rd grade in this unit, as the focus is on collecting primary, observable data rather than evaluating external sources.
                                                                                        • The unit assumes basic digital literacy skills (e.g., using a keyboard, mouse, or touch screen) which may require additional foundational support for some students.
                                                                                        • The estimated minutes are pedagogical hypotheses and do not account for potential classroom management, unexpected technical issues, or individual student learning pace variations. The total estimated pedagogical time for the four micro-skills is 250 minutes (45+60+75+70), which does not include the stated 30 minutes for assessment, 20 for setup, or time for transitions and potential reteaching. This suggests a significant time commitment that may exceed typical allocations for a single unit.
                                                                                        Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                          Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                          • minor: The total estimated pedagogical time for the four micro-skills is 250 minutes, which is a significant time commitment for a single unit at this grade level. The proposal itself notes this in the limitations section. Suggested correction: Consider breaking the unit into two smaller units (e.g., one on data collection and another on digital organization/representation) or reducing the estimated time for some skills, particularly 'organize-data-simple-digital' (75 min) which primarily involves data entry.
