                                                                                          Decisions and testing

                                                                                          Add selection to sequences and bounded repeats; explain a failing example.

                                                                                          Identifies situations where a decision depends on a single observable condition.

                                                                                          micro-identify-conditional-logic-16ef3a93f825734d · computational_thinking · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                          Why this learning matters: Understanding conditional logic is foundational for programming decisions. Before implementing 'if/then' statements, students must recognize when such a structure is necessary in real-world or abstract problems. This micro-skill focuses on the conceptual understanding of conditions.

                                                                                          Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 4, students are developing logical reasoning skills. Identifying conditions is a prerequisite for applying selection in simple algorithms, aligning with the progression of adding selection to sequences.

                                                                                          Evidence of mastery: Students can correctly sort a set of scenarios into 'always happens' and 'depends on a condition' categories, explaining their reasoning and identifying the specific condition.

                                                                                          Assessment task: Given a set of 5-7 illustrated scenarios, students will categorize each as either 'always happens' or 'depends on a condition'. For scenarios categorized as 'depends on a condition', students must verbally identify the specific condition that triggers the action.

                                                                                          Access and participation: Use visual aids and real-life examples. Allow students to respond verbally, through drawing, or by pointing. Provide sentence starters for justifications.

                                                                                          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                          Evidence limitations: The quote supports the inclusion of 'selection' but does not specify the cognitive level or age-appropriateness for identifying conditions. The pedagogical rationale bridges this gap by focusing on conceptual understanding.

                                                                                          Safety: All activities involving digital devices should be conducted in a supervised environment. When discussing real-world scenarios, ensure examples are age-appropriate and do not involve sensitive topics. No electrical circuits or high-voltage components are used in this unit.

                                                                                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                          Success criteria
                                                                                          • Correctly categorizes at least 4 out of 5 scenarios.
                                                                                          • For conditional scenarios, accurately identifies the specific condition.
                                                                                          • Provides a clear, logical justification for each categorization.
                                                                                          Teaching sequence
                                                                                          • Introduce the concept of 'conditions' and 'decisions' using everyday examples (e.g., 'If the light is red, stop').
                                                                                          • Discuss how some actions always happen, while others depend on something else.
                                                                                          • Provide various scenarios and ask students to identify the condition and the resulting action.
                                                                                          • Practice categorizing scenarios as 'always' or 'conditional', explicitly stating the condition for the latter.
                                                                                          • Provide feedback on their reasoning and categorization.
                                                                                          Misconceptions
                                                                                          • Confusing a condition with a consequence.
                                                                                          • Believing all actions are conditional or none are.
                                                                                          Required resources
                                                                                          • Scenario cards (text and images)
                                                                                          • Whiteboard or large paper for categorization
                                                                                          External knowledge to verify
                                                                                          • Basic understanding of cause and effect
                                                                                          Unit limitations
                                                                                          • The provided evidence does not specify the exact age or grade level for each computational thinking concept, requiring pedagogical interpretation for grade 4.
                                                                                          • The 'simple code structures' mentioned in the Argentine curriculum are open to interpretation regarding complexity and specific programming paradigms (e.g., block-based vs. text-based).
                                                                                          • The evidence does not provide specific examples of 'observable conditions' or 'outcomes' for this age group, necessitating curriculum designer discretion.
                                                                                          • The effectiveness of these micro-skills is based on pedagogical theory and alignment with international standards, not empirical validation for El Salvador.
                                                                                          • The estimated minutes are hypotheses for teaching and practice, not official time allocations, and do not account for unit-level overhead or extended project work.
                                                                                          Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                            Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                            • minor: The micro-skill 'identify-conditional-logic' focuses on identifying situations requiring conditional logic, which is a pedagogical precursor to using selection. The provided evidence ('use... selection... in programs') supports the application of selection in programming, but does not explicitly mandate the preliminary, abstract identification of conditional situations as a separate, assessed skill. Suggested correction: Acknowledge that this skill is a pedagogical interpretation to scaffold a curriculum requirement, not a direct requirement itself. Consider if this should be an introductory, unassessed activity rather than a formal micro-skill with mastery criteria.

                                                                                            Constructs a simple algorithm using a single 'if/then' or 'if/then/else' selection structure.

                                                                                            micro-construct-simple-selection-f7734e64cbf12381 · computational_thinking · T1 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                            Why this learning matters: This micro-skill moves from identifying conditions to actively implementing them in an algorithmic structure. It introduces the fundamental 'if/then' construct, which is a core component of computational thinking and programming. Using block-based programming or flowcharts allows students to focus on logic without syntax complexities.

                                                                                            Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 4 is an appropriate age to begin constructing simple conditional logic, building on the prior understanding of conditions. The use of 'simple code structures' is explicitly mentioned for this age group.

                                                                                            Evidence of mastery: Students can create a block-based program or a flowchart that correctly implements a given 'if/then' or 'if/then/else' logic.

                                                                                            Assessment task: Given a problem (e.g., 'Make a character move right if the space key is pressed, otherwise stay still'), students will use a block-based programming environment (like Scratch Jr. or Scratch) or draw a flowchart to create an algorithm that solves the problem.

                                                                                            Access and participation: Provide pre-made templates or partially completed algorithms. Use visual cues for blocks/symbols. Allow verbal explanations of their logic. Pair students for collaborative construction.

                                                                                            T0 alternative and limits: Students can draw flowcharts with 'if/then/else' symbols and write out the conditions and actions. This allows for conceptual understanding but loses the immediate feedback and interactive nature of digital programming.

                                                                                            Evidence limitations: The quotes confirm 'selection' and 'simple code structures' but do not prescribe the specific tools (e.g., block-based programming) or the complexity of the conditions. The pedagogical rationale justifies the use of simple, observable conditions.

                                                                                            Safety: All activities involving digital devices should be conducted in a supervised environment. When discussing real-world scenarios, ensure examples are age-appropriate and do not involve sensitive topics. No electrical circuits or high-voltage components are used in this unit.

                                                                                            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                            Success criteria
                                                                                            • The algorithm correctly uses an 'if/then' or 'if/then/else' block.
                                                                                            • The condition specified in the block accurately reflects the problem.
                                                                                            • The actions within the 'if' and 'else' branches are correct.
                                                                                            Teaching sequence
                                                                                            • Review the concept of conditions and actions.
                                                                                            • Introduce the 'if/then' and 'if/then/else' blocks/symbols in the chosen environment (e.g., Scratch Jr., flowcharts).
                                                                                            • Model constructing a simple conditional algorithm step-by-step.
                                                                                            • Provide guided practice with different scenarios, emphasizing identifying the condition and the two possible outcomes.
                                                                                            • Offer opportunities for independent practice and peer feedback.
                                                                                            • Provide feedback on the correctness of their constructed algorithms.
                                                                                            Misconceptions
                                                                                            • Confusing the 'if' part with the 'then' part.
                                                                                            • Not understanding that the 'else' part only executes if the 'if' condition is false.
                                                                                            • Placing actions outside the conditional block when they should be inside.
                                                                                            Required resources
                                                                                            • Teacher device with projector
                                                                                            • Block-based programming environment (e.g., Scratch Jr., Scratch) or flowchart symbols/templates
                                                                                            • Student devices (T2) or paper for flowcharts (T0)
                                                                                            External knowledge to verify
                                                                                            • Basic understanding of sequences of instructions
                                                                                            Unit limitations
                                                                                            • The provided evidence does not specify the exact age or grade level for each computational thinking concept, requiring pedagogical interpretation for grade 4.
                                                                                            • The 'simple code structures' mentioned in the Argentine curriculum are open to interpretation regarding complexity and specific programming paradigms (e.g., block-based vs. text-based).
                                                                                            • The evidence does not provide specific examples of 'observable conditions' or 'outcomes' for this age group, necessitating curriculum designer discretion.
                                                                                            • The effectiveness of these micro-skills is based on pedagogical theory and alignment with international standards, not empirical validation for El Salvador.
                                                                                            • The estimated minutes are hypotheses for teaching and practice, not official time allocations, and do not account for unit-level overhead or extended project work.
                                                                                            Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                              Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                              • minor: The Argentine curriculum source mentions 'simple code structures involving the use of variables'. The proposed micro-skills do not incorporate the use of variables with selection, which is explicitly linked in the evidence. Suggested correction: Consider adding a micro-skill or extending an existing one to include using a variable within the condition (e.g., 'if score > 10') to fully align with the provided evidence. Alternatively, justify why this part of the evidence is not being addressed in this unit.

                                                                                              Sources: source-argentina-d3ce0ed43af1a812 (page 20, Primary second cycle, paragraph-p113-695922b327fa7b1d, original characters 2744–2816); source-eng-computing-570e16e9afcbc1c7 (page HTML, Key stage 2, paragraph-p110-7c8559df04ecea31, original characters 0–51)

                                                                                              Tests a simple algorithm with selection to identify and correct errors in its logic or implementation.

                                                                                              micro-test-and-debug-selection-3492ab48dbe51192 · computational_thinking · T1 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                              Why this learning matters: Debugging is a critical computational thinking skill. For selection structures, students need to systematically test both branches of the condition to ensure the algorithm behaves as expected. This micro-skill develops problem-solving and logical reasoning by requiring students to analyze and fix errors.

                                                                                              Proposed grade placement rationale: Debugging simple algorithms is appropriate for grade 4, building on their ability to construct them. The focus on 'detect and correct errors' and 'depuración' (debugging) is explicitly mentioned in the evidence for this age group.

                                                                                              Evidence of mastery: Students can execute a given algorithm with selection, identify a specific error (e.g., wrong condition, incorrect action), and propose a correct modification.

                                                                                              Assessment task: Given a block-based program or flowchart with a single 'if/then/else' structure that contains one logical error (e.g., condition is reversed, action in 'else' is wrong), students will run/trace the algorithm, identify the error, and explain how to fix it.

                                                                                              Access and participation: Provide checklists for common errors. Use visual highlighting in the programming environment to show execution flow. Allow verbal explanations or drawing corrections. Provide simplified error scenarios.

                                                                                              T0 alternative and limits: Students can trace flowcharts with errors, identify the point of failure, and draw the corrected flowchart. This allows for logical debugging but lacks the dynamic feedback of running a program.

                                                                                              Evidence limitations: The quotes support debugging but do not specify the type of errors or the complexity of the algorithms to be debugged. Specifically, the Argentine curriculum mentions 'debugging of programming and robotics sequences', and applying this to selection structures is a pedagogical interpretation. The pedagogical rationale focuses on simple selection errors appropriate for the grade level.

                                                                                              Safety: All activities involving digital devices should be conducted in a supervised environment. When discussing real-world scenarios, ensure examples are age-appropriate and do not involve sensitive topics. No electrical circuits or high-voltage components are used in this unit.

                                                                                              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                              Success criteria
                                                                                              • Successfully identifies the specific error in the algorithm.
                                                                                              • Clearly explains why it is an error (e.g., 'It should move left when the key is pressed, not right').
                                                                                              • Proposes a correct modification to fix the error.
                                                                                              Teaching sequence
                                                                                              • Review how 'if/then/else' structures work.
                                                                                              • Introduce common errors in conditional logic (e.g., swapped conditions, incorrect actions).
                                                                                              • Model the process of testing an algorithm: running it, observing behavior, comparing to expected behavior.
                                                                                              • Demonstrate how to trace an algorithm step-by-step to find where the logic deviates, emphasizing testing both branches of the condition.
                                                                                              • Provide practice with pre-made algorithms containing errors, guiding students to identify and correct them.
                                                                                              • Encourage peer debugging and discussion of different testing strategies.
                                                                                              • Provide feedback on their debugging process and proposed solutions.
                                                                                              Misconceptions
                                                                                              • Believing the program will 'know' what they intended, even if the code is wrong.
                                                                                              • Only testing one branch of a conditional statement.
                                                                                              • Changing random parts of the code without a systematic approach.
                                                                                              Required resources
                                                                                              • Teacher device with projector
                                                                                              • Block-based programming environment (e.g., Scratch Jr., Scratch) or flowchart examples
                                                                                              • Student devices (T2) or paper for tracing (T0)
                                                                                              External knowledge to verify
                                                                                              • Ability to follow instructions sequentially
                                                                                              • Basic understanding of expected program behavior
                                                                                              Unit limitations
                                                                                              • The provided evidence does not specify the exact age or grade level for each computational thinking concept, requiring pedagogical interpretation for grade 4.
                                                                                              • The 'simple code structures' mentioned in the Argentine curriculum are open to interpretation regarding complexity and specific programming paradigms (e.g., block-based vs. text-based).
                                                                                              • The evidence does not provide specific examples of 'observable conditions' or 'outcomes' for this age group, necessitating curriculum designer discretion.
                                                                                              • The effectiveness of these micro-skills is based on pedagogical theory and alignment with international standards, not empirical validation for El Salvador.
                                                                                              • The estimated minutes are hypotheses for teaching and practice, not official time allocations, and do not account for unit-level overhead or extended project work.
                                                                                              Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                • minor: The learning goal 'test both outcomes' and the micro-skill 'test-and-debug-selection' are not fully supported by the provided evidence. The evidence mentions debugging in general ('detect and correct errors', 'depuración de secuencias') but does not explicitly require testing both branches of a conditional statement. Suggested correction: Either provide evidence that mandates testing both outcomes of a conditional as a specific requirement for this grade level, or rephrase the learning goal and micro-skill to focus on general debugging of selection structures as supported by the evidence.
                                                                                                • minor: The assessment for 'test-and-debug-selection' requires students to 'run/trace the algorithm' with a single error. However, the success criteria ('Successfully identifies the specific error', 'Clearly explains why it is an error', 'Proposes a correct modification') do not explicitly assess the student's ability to test *both* outcomes of the conditional, which is a key part of the unit's learning goal and the skill's pedagogical rationale. Suggested correction: Update the success criteria to include a requirement that the student demonstrates testing both the 'if' and 'else' paths to confirm the program's behavior and locate the error. For example: 'Demonstrates testing of both conditional branches to identify the incorrect outcome.'

                                                                                                Privacy and source checks

                                                                                                Identify types of personal data exposed by simple digital activities based on privacy statements.

                                                                                                micro-identify-personal-data-exposure-967e87e97b91d3c4 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                Why this learning matters: Understanding what personal data is and how it might be collected is foundational for digital privacy. This micro-skill focuses on recognition from explicit statements rather than complex analysis, making it suitable for 4th graders. It directly supports the unit goal of explaining what data a digital activity exposes.

                                                                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 4, students are beginning to interact more independently with digital services. Identifying data exposure from privacy statements is a basic step in developing digital literacy and self-protection, aligning with DigComp's 'basic level' for identifying privacy statements.

                                                                                                Evidence of mastery: Students correctly list at least three types of personal data that a given digital activity might collect, based on a simplified privacy statement provided.

                                                                                                Assessment task: Given a simplified privacy statement for a popular educational app or website, students will list three pieces of personal information they believe the app/website might collect according to the statement. They will briefly explain why each piece of data might be collected.

                                                                                                Access and participation: Provide visual aids with icons representing different types of personal data. Use simplified language and provide examples relevant to students' experiences. Allow for verbal responses or drawing instead of written lists for students with writing difficulties.

                                                                                                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                Evidence limitations: The DigComp reference indicates identifying 'simple privacy policy statements,' but does not specify the depth of understanding required for data types or the complexity of the digital services. The 'simple' nature of the statements is key.

                                                                                                Safety: All digital activities should be conducted under direct teacher supervision. Emphasize that students should never share real personal information online without explicit parental consent and guidance. Discussions about online safety should be handled sensitively, focusing on protective measures rather than instilling fear.

                                                                                                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                Success criteria
                                                                                                • Lists at least three distinct types of personal data explicitly mentioned or clearly implied by the privacy statement.
                                                                                                • Provides a plausible reason for the collection of each data type, referencing the statement.
                                                                                                • Demonstrates understanding of what constitutes 'personal data' in a digital context.
                                                                                                Teaching sequence
                                                                                                • **Diagnosis:** Ask students what 'personal information' means to them and where they might share it online.
                                                                                                • **Practice:** Present simplified examples of digital activities and their corresponding simplified privacy statements. In small groups, have students identify what information the service might collect based on the statement.
                                                                                                • **Feedback:** Discuss common answers, clarifying what constitutes personal data (name, age, location, photos, etc.) versus non-personal data. Emphasize that privacy statements are where this information is found.
                                                                                                Misconceptions
                                                                                                • Believing that if they don't type it, it's not collected.
                                                                                                • Thinking that all information shared online is public.
                                                                                                • Confusing personal data with private thoughts or feelings.
                                                                                                Required resources
                                                                                                • Whiteboard or projector
                                                                                                • Markers or pens
                                                                                                • Worksheets with digital activity scenarios
                                                                                                • Simplified privacy statement examples (age-appropriate)
                                                                                                External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                • Basic understanding of personal identity (name, age, address)
                                                                                                • Familiarity with common digital activities (online games, video streaming)
                                                                                                Unit limitations
                                                                                                • The provided evidence does not specify the exact 'simple ways' for privacy protection or reliability assessment, requiring pedagogical interpretation.
                                                                                                • The 'basic level' and 'simple ways' mentioned in DigComp are open to interpretation regarding the cognitive load appropriate for 4th graders.
                                                                                                • The effectiveness of these micro-skills in preventing real-world privacy breaches or misinformation is not empirically validated by the provided text.
                                                                                                • The England Computing programmes of study provide general support for evaluating information and using internet services, but do not detail specific reliability indicators or privacy protection methods for this age group. Therefore, the specific pedagogical approach for these details relies on interpretation of 'simple ways'.
                                                                                                Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                  Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                  • minor: The micro-skill 'identify-personal-data-exposure' requires students to identify types of personal data from within privacy statements. The cited evidence supports 'identify[ing] simple privacy policy statements of how personal data is used', which is not the same as interpreting the content of those statements to extract specific data types. Suggested correction: Either find evidence that supports the interpretation of privacy statements for this grade level, or rephrase the micro-skill to focus on identifying the existence and purpose of a privacy statement, which is what the evidence supports.
                                                                                                  • minor: The assessment task for 'identify-personal-data-exposure' requires students to 'briefly explain why each piece of data might be collected'. This requires an inferential leap about the service's purpose that is not explicitly part of the micro-skill statement ('Identify types of personal data exposed...based on privacy statements') and may be too complex for this introductory skill. Suggested correction: Revise the assessment task to focus solely on identifying the types of data collected as stated in the simplified privacy policy, removing the requirement to explain the service's rationale for the collection.

                                                                                                  Select simple ways to protect personal data and privacy in digital environments.

                                                                                                  micro-select-privacy-protection-methods-0d8391096159d964 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                  Why this learning matters: This micro-skill moves from identification to action, empowering students to take control of their digital privacy. It aligns with the unit goal by providing practical strategies for managing data exposure. The focus on 'simple ways' ensures age-appropriateness.

                                                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: For 4th graders, learning to select simple protective measures is crucial as they navigate increasingly complex digital spaces. This competence is explicitly mentioned at the 'basic level' in DigComp, indicating its suitability for this age group.

                                                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students choose appropriate privacy settings or actions from a given list to protect their personal information in a simulated digital scenario, justifying their choices.

                                                                                                  Assessment task: Given a scenario where a friend asks for personal information online (e.g., full name, school, home address), students will choose the best response from a set of options (e.g., 'Don't share it', 'Ask an adult', 'Share only my first name') and explain why their chosen option is the safest.

                                                                                                  Access and participation: Use visual cues for different protection strategies. Provide pre-written options for selection to reduce cognitive load. Allow students to demonstrate understanding through role-play or by pointing to correct options.

                                                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                  Evidence limitations: The evidence specifies 'simple ways' but does not define what constitutes 'simple' for a 4th grader. The effectiveness of these ways in real-world scenarios is not guaranteed by the quote.

                                                                                                  Safety: All digital activities should be conducted under direct teacher supervision. Emphasize that students should never share real personal information online without explicit parental consent and guidance. Discussions about online safety should be handled sensitively, focusing on protective measures rather than instilling fear.

                                                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                                                  • Chooses the safest option for protecting personal data.
                                                                                                  • Provides a clear and logical explanation for their choice.
                                                                                                  • Demonstrates understanding of the risks associated with sharing personal information online.
                                                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                                                  • **Diagnosis:** Ask students what they would do if someone online asked for their phone number. Discuss initial reactions.
                                                                                                  • **Practice:** Introduce various simple protection strategies (e.g., strong passwords, not sharing personal info, asking an adult, using privacy settings). Use role-playing or scenario cards where students practice selecting and explaining protective actions.
                                                                                                  • **Feedback:** Review scenarios and discuss why certain choices are safer than others. Emphasize the importance of adult guidance and not sharing too much information.
                                                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                                                  • Believing that if they know the person online, it's always safe to share.
                                                                                                  • Thinking that privacy settings are too complicated to use.
                                                                                                  • Underestimating the potential harm of sharing seemingly small pieces of information.
                                                                                                  Required resources
                                                                                                  • Scenario cards with privacy dilemmas
                                                                                                  • List of simple privacy protection strategies
                                                                                                  • Whiteboard or projector
                                                                                                  External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                  • Basic understanding of online communication (chat, games)
                                                                                                  • Concept of personal safety and stranger danger
                                                                                                  Unit limitations
                                                                                                  • The provided evidence does not specify the exact 'simple ways' for privacy protection or reliability assessment, requiring pedagogical interpretation.
                                                                                                  • The 'basic level' and 'simple ways' mentioned in DigComp are open to interpretation regarding the cognitive load appropriate for 4th graders.
                                                                                                  • The effectiveness of these micro-skills in preventing real-world privacy breaches or misinformation is not empirically validated by the provided text.
                                                                                                  • The England Computing programmes of study provide general support for evaluating information and using internet services, but do not detail specific reliability indicators or privacy protection methods for this age group. Therefore, the specific pedagogical approach for these details relies on interpretation of 'simple ways'.

                                                                                                    Identify key indicators of reliability in simple information sources.

                                                                                                    micro-identify-information-source-reliability-indicators-3b9b0b03ea04e4d6 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                    Why this learning matters: In an age of abundant information, discerning reliable sources is a critical skill. This micro-skill addresses the unit goal of comparing information sources by focusing on observable indicators of trustworthiness. It's a foundational step for critical thinking about digital content.

                                                                                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 4 students are increasingly exposed to various online information. Teaching them to identify simple reliability indicators helps them develop critical thinking skills early, aligning with DigComp's 'basic level' for having 'due regard to reliability'. The England Computing curriculum supports using internet services to evaluate information.

                                                                                                    Evidence of mastery: Students correctly identify at least two indicators of reliability (e.g., author, date, source type) within a simple information source and explain their relevance.

                                                                                                    Assessment task: Students are given a short, simplified text or image from a single source (e.g., a children's news article). They must identify at least two reasons (indicators) why this source might be considered reliable or unreliable.

                                                                                                    Access and participation: Use highly visual examples of sources. Provide a checklist of reliability indicators. Allow students to verbally explain their reasoning or use a graphic organizer to identify indicators.

                                                                                                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                    Evidence limitations: The quote 'identify simple ways to have due regard to reliability' is broad. It doesn't specify which indicators are considered 'simple' or the complexity of sources students should evaluate. The focus is on identification, not deep analysis. The England Computing quote supports using internet services for evaluation but doesn't detail specific reliability indicators.

                                                                                                    Safety: All digital activities should be conducted under direct teacher supervision. Emphasize that students should never share real personal information online without explicit parental consent and guidance. Discussions about online safety should be handled sensitively, focusing on protective measures rather than instilling fear.

                                                                                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                    Success criteria
                                                                                                    • Identifies at least two valid indicators of reliability (e.g., author's expertise, publication date, type of website) within the given source.
                                                                                                    • Clearly articulates the relevance of each identified indicator to the source's reliability.
                                                                                                    • Avoids making judgments based solely on appearance or personal preference.
                                                                                                    Teaching sequence
                                                                                                    • **Diagnosis:** Ask students how they know if something they read or see online is true. Discuss their initial criteria.
                                                                                                    • **Practice:** Introduce simple reliability indicators (e.g., 'Who wrote it?', 'When was it written?', 'Where did it come from?'). Provide various simple information sources and guide students to identify these indicators within each source.
                                                                                                    • **Feedback:** Discuss student findings, highlighting how different indicators contribute to overall reliability. Emphasize that not all information online is equally trustworthy.
                                                                                                    Misconceptions
                                                                                                    • Believing that anything found online is true.
                                                                                                    • Assuming that a professional-looking website is always reliable.
                                                                                                    • Confusing popularity (e.g., many likes) with reliability.
                                                                                                    Required resources
                                                                                                    • Printed or projected examples of simple information sources (e.g., short articles, images with captions)
                                                                                                    • Whiteboard or projector
                                                                                                    • Checklist of reliability indicators
                                                                                                    External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                    • Basic understanding of different types of information (news, stories, opinions)
                                                                                                    • Concept of authorship and publication
                                                                                                    Unit limitations
                                                                                                    • The provided evidence does not specify the exact 'simple ways' for privacy protection or reliability assessment, requiring pedagogical interpretation.
                                                                                                    • The 'basic level' and 'simple ways' mentioned in DigComp are open to interpretation regarding the cognitive load appropriate for 4th graders.
                                                                                                    • The effectiveness of these micro-skills in preventing real-world privacy breaches or misinformation is not empirically validated by the provided text.
                                                                                                    • The England Computing programmes of study provide general support for evaluating information and using internet services, but do not detail specific reliability indicators or privacy protection methods for this age group. Therefore, the specific pedagogical approach for these details relies on interpretation of 'simple ways'.
                                                                                                    Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                      Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                      • minor: The micro-skill 'identify-information-source-reliability-indicators' cites 'eng-computing html ¶paragraph-p118-b69c613838738b5f' which states students should 'select, use and combine a variety of software (including internet services)...to...evaluating...information'. This evidence supports the general activity of evaluating information using internet services but does not specifically support the identification of reliability indicators like author or date. Suggested correction: Remove the 'eng-computing' citation as it does not directly support the specific skill of identifying reliability indicators. The DigComp citation is sufficient but should be understood as the sole evidence.
                                                                                                      • minor: The micro-skill 'identify-information-source-reliability-indicators' is presented as a prerequisite-free skill. However, understanding how to evaluate a source for reliability is logically dependent on first understanding what personal data is and how to protect it, as sharing information is a key part of interacting with sources. The current parallel structure could be less effective. Suggested correction: Add 'select-privacy-protection-methods' as a prerequisite for 'identify-information-source-reliability-indicators'. This creates a more logical pedagogical sequence where students first learn to protect themselves, then learn to evaluate external content.

                                                                                                      Controlled design tests

                                                                                                      Record failure and connect a revision to evidence rather than preference.

                                                                                                      Define clear, measurable criteria for testing a prototype's functionality.

                                                                                                      micro-define-test-criteria-9036d8004fe0158a · design_process · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                      Why this learning matters: Before testing, students must understand what they are testing for. Defining criteria helps them move beyond subjective opinions to objective evaluation, a foundational step for controlled testing. This skill is durable as it applies to any design challenge.

                                                                                                      Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 4, students can move from general ideas of 'good' or 'bad' to more specific, observable characteristics. This builds on earlier experiences of identifying features and prepares them for more complex evaluation in later grades.

                                                                                                      Evidence of mastery: Students articulate specific, measurable conditions or outcomes that indicate a prototype's success or failure in meeting its intended purpose.

                                                                                                      Assessment task: Given a simple prototype (e.g., a paper airplane, a bridge made of craft sticks), students will list three measurable criteria to determine if it is 'fit for purpose'.

                                                                                                      Access and participation: Provide visual aids and sentence frames for defining criteria. Allow verbal responses or drawing for students with writing difficulties. Pair students for collaborative definition.

                                                                                                      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                      Evidence limitations: The quote 'develop design criteria to inform the design' supports the need for criteria but does not specify how to define *testable* criteria or for what grade level. The application to 'prototype functionality' and its use in testing is a pedagogical extension for this unit.

                                                                                                      Safety: All prototype construction and testing activities must use low-voltage, non-hazardous materials. Ensure proper supervision during any physical testing to prevent injury from falling objects or sharp edges. Emphasize safe handling of tools and materials. No mains electricity or high-voltage components are to be used.

                                                                                                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                      Success criteria
                                                                                                      • Each criterion is specific and not vague (e.g., 'flies straight' instead of 'flies well').
                                                                                                      • Each criterion is measurable or observable (e.g., 'flies at least 2 meters' instead of 'flies far').
                                                                                                      • Criteria directly relate to the prototype's intended function.
                                                                                                      Teaching sequence
                                                                                                      • Diagnose: Ask students how they know if something 'works'. Discuss subjective vs. objective observations.
                                                                                                      • Practice: Provide examples of poorly defined criteria and have students refine them. Work in groups to define criteria for a familiar object.
                                                                                                      • Feedback: Review student-defined criteria, highlighting specificity and measurability. Provide sentence starters for criteria definition.
                                                                                                      Misconceptions
                                                                                                      • Criteria are just opinions or preferences.
                                                                                                      • Criteria can be vague (e.g., 'it looks good').
                                                                                                      • All criteria are equally important.
                                                                                                      Required resources
                                                                                                      • Examples of simple prototypes (e.g., paper airplanes, small ramps, craft stick bridges)
                                                                                                      • Whiteboard or large paper for brainstorming criteria
                                                                                                      External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                      • Basic understanding of measurement (length, time, count)
                                                                                                      • Understanding of cause and effect
                                                                                                      Unit limitations
                                                                                                      • The provided Salvadoran curriculum (sv-cyt) focuses heavily on identifying and classifying simple/complex machines and energy transformations, which are more aligned with science content. It lacks explicit guidance on the methodology of controlled design testing, criteria definition, or evidence-based revision within a design process context for this grade level. Therefore, the micro-skills are primarily derived from the UK Design & Technology curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted to fit the 'Controlled Prototype Evaluation and Revision' learning goal, which is introduced as a key aspect of technological education.
                                                                                                      • The 'evidence_quotes' provide general support for aspects of design and evaluation but do not offer specific pedagogical instructions or grade-level appropriateness for 'controlled testing' or 'repeatable tests'. The interpretation and application to grade 4 are based on general pedagogical principles for design education.
                                                                                                      • The unit does not claim empirical validation for the effectiveness of these specific micro-skills or their sequence. The estimated minutes are hypothetical and may vary significantly based on student prior knowledge and classroom dynamics.
                                                                                                      • The unit does not imply equivalence between international grade levels or curricula. The adaptation is made to fit the stated learning goal within the context of El Salvador's educational framework, as interpreted from the provided documents.
                                                                                                      • The unit's learning goal, as stated in the input ('Use a repeatable test to compare prototypes while keeping relevant conditions consistent'), is broader than the current micro-skills, which focus on evaluating and revising a *single* prototype. The unit's activities are designed to build foundational skills for controlled testing and evidence-based revision, which are prerequisites for effective comparison of multiple prototypes. Future units could build upon this foundation to introduce comparative testing.
                                                                                                      Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                        Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                        • major: The entire unit's methodology, focusing on a design-build-test-evaluate cycle, is absent from the provided Grade 4 Salvadoran curriculum (sv-cyt). The source document focuses on analyzing existing simple/complex machines and energy transformations, which is a science-oriented approach, not a technology/design process one. Suggested correction: Acknowledge that this unit introduces a new pedagogical framework (design process) not present in the local curriculum source. Justify the introduction of this framework as a necessary component of technology education that complements the existing science content.
                                                                                                        • minor: The micro-skill 'define-test-criteria' is supported by the quote 'develop design criteria to inform the design'. The source does not specify that these criteria must be measurable or testable in the context of a prototype test, as required by the skill. Suggested correction: The proposer's 'evidence_limitations' correctly identifies this. The skill's focus on 'measurable' criteria is a necessary pedagogical extension for this unit's goal, but it is an extension nonetheless.
                                                                                                        • minor: The unit's learning goal is 'Use a repeatable test to compare prototypes'. However, the micro-skills focus on testing and revising a single prototype. The comparison of multiple prototypes is not addressed. Suggested correction: Revise the unit's learning goal to more accurately reflect the content, such as 'Use a repeatable test to evaluate and propose revisions for a prototype.' The proposer's 'limitations' section acknowledges this discrepancy; this formalizes the need for correction.

                                                                                                        Conduct a repeatable test on a prototype, maintaining consistent conditions.

                                                                                                        micro-conduct-repeatable-test-c6747970171b1faf · design_process · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                        Why this learning matters: This skill is central to controlled testing. Learning to control variables and repeat tests provides reliable data for evaluation, moving students beyond single, anecdotal observations. It's a durable skill applicable across STEM fields.

                                                                                                        Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 4 students can grasp the concept of 'fair testing' and the importance of consistency. This lays the groundwork for more formal scientific experimentation and engineering design processes in later grades.

                                                                                                        Evidence of mastery: Students perform a test multiple times, ensuring that variables not being tested are kept constant, and record the results for each trial.

                                                                                                        Assessment task: Students will test a paper airplane prototype three times, launching it from the same height and position, and record the distance flown for each trial.

                                                                                                        Access and participation: Provide clear visual instructions for the test setup. Use larger, easier-to-handle prototypes if fine motor skills are a barrier. Allow peer support for recording data.

                                                                                                        T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                        Evidence limitations: The quote 'evaluate their ideas and products against their own design criteria' supports the general idea of evaluation, which implies testing. However, it does not explicitly mention 'repeatable test' or 'maintaining consistent conditions'. These are pedagogical constructs introduced in this unit to ensure robust and reliable evaluation.

                                                                                                        Safety: All prototype construction and testing activities must use low-voltage, non-hazardous materials. Ensure proper supervision during any physical testing to prevent injury from falling objects or sharp edges. Emphasize safe handling of tools and materials. No mains electricity or high-voltage components are to be used.

                                                                                                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                                        Success criteria
                                                                                                        • The prototype is tested at least three times.
                                                                                                        • The launch method (height, angle, force) is consistent across all trials.
                                                                                                        • Results (e.g., distance flown) are recorded accurately for each trial.
                                                                                                        Teaching sequence
                                                                                                        • Diagnose: Ask students why repeating an action might give a better result than doing it once. Discuss what 'fair' means in a test.
                                                                                                        • Practice: Model a repeatable test, explicitly pointing out controlled variables. Students then practice testing a simple prototype (e.g., a toy car on a ramp) with guidance.
                                                                                                        • Feedback: Observe students' testing methods, providing immediate feedback on maintaining consistency. Discuss variations in results and potential causes.
                                                                                                        Misconceptions
                                                                                                        • One test is enough to draw conclusions.
                                                                                                        • It's okay to change things during a test if it seems to help.
                                                                                                        • The goal is to make the prototype 'win' every time, not to gather accurate data.
                                                                                                        Required resources
                                                                                                        • Simple prototypes (e.g., paper airplanes, toy cars, small ramps)
                                                                                                        • Measuring tools (e.g., tape measures, rulers)
                                                                                                        • Recording sheets/notebooks
                                                                                                        External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                        • Understanding of a 'fair test' where only one thing is changed at a time.
                                                                                                        • Ability to measure length or time
                                                                                                        Unit limitations
                                                                                                        • The provided Salvadoran curriculum (sv-cyt) focuses heavily on identifying and classifying simple/complex machines and energy transformations, which are more aligned with science content. It lacks explicit guidance on the methodology of controlled design testing, criteria definition, or evidence-based revision within a design process context for this grade level. Therefore, the micro-skills are primarily derived from the UK Design & Technology curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted to fit the 'Controlled Prototype Evaluation and Revision' learning goal, which is introduced as a key aspect of technological education.
                                                                                                        • The 'evidence_quotes' provide general support for aspects of design and evaluation but do not offer specific pedagogical instructions or grade-level appropriateness for 'controlled testing' or 'repeatable tests'. The interpretation and application to grade 4 are based on general pedagogical principles for design education.
                                                                                                        • The unit does not claim empirical validation for the effectiveness of these specific micro-skills or their sequence. The estimated minutes are hypothetical and may vary significantly based on student prior knowledge and classroom dynamics.
                                                                                                        • The unit does not imply equivalence between international grade levels or curricula. The adaptation is made to fit the stated learning goal within the context of El Salvador's educational framework, as interpreted from the provided documents.
                                                                                                        • The unit's learning goal, as stated in the input ('Use a repeatable test to compare prototypes while keeping relevant conditions consistent'), is broader than the current micro-skills, which focus on evaluating and revising a *single* prototype. The unit's activities are designed to build foundational skills for controlled testing and evidence-based revision, which are prerequisites for effective comparison of multiple prototypes. Future units could build upon this foundation to introduce comparative testing.

Suggested correction: Acknowledge that this unit introduces a new pedagogical framework (design process) not present in the local curriculum source. Suggested correction: The proposer's 'evidence_limitations' correctly identifies this gap.
