                                                                                                          Record quantitative and qualitative data from prototype tests.

                                                                                                          micro-record-test-data-0ce2bc39009e9ce9 · design_process · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                          Why this learning matters: Accurate data recording is crucial for evidence-based design decisions. Distinguishing between quantitative and qualitative data helps students develop a more comprehensive understanding of prototype performance. This is a durable skill for any investigative process.

                                                                                                          Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 4, students can begin to systematically record data beyond simple tallies. Introducing both quantitative and qualitative data enriches their observational skills and prepares them for data analysis in later grades.

                                                                                                          Evidence of mastery: Students accurately document numerical measurements (e.g., distance, time) and descriptive observations (e.g., 'wobbled', 'fell apart') during prototype testing.

                                                                                                          Assessment task: During a prototype test (e.g., a bridge holding weight), students will record the maximum weight held (quantitative) and describe how the bridge failed (qualitative, e.g., 'snapped in the middle', 'bent slowly').

                                                                                                          Access and participation: Provide pre-formatted data sheets with clear labels. Allow students to dictate observations to a peer or teacher. Use visual symbols for qualitative observations if writing is challenging.

                                                                                                          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                          Evidence limitations: The quote 'evaluate their ideas and products against their own design criteria' supports the need for data to perform evaluation. However, it does not explicitly mention the act of recording data, nor the pedagogical distinction between 'quantitative' and 'qualitative' data. This distinction is a refinement introduced in this unit to enable more comprehensive evaluation.

                                                                                                          Safety: All prototype construction and testing activities must use low-voltage, non-hazardous materials. Ensure proper supervision during any physical testing to prevent injury from falling objects or sharp edges. Emphasize safe handling of tools and materials. No mains electricity or high-voltage components are to be used.

                                                                                                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                          Success criteria
                                                                                                          • Quantitative data (e.g., weight, distance, time) is recorded with appropriate units.
                                                                                                          • Qualitative observations are descriptive and relevant to the prototype's performance.
                                                                                                          • Data is organized clearly (e.g., in a simple table).
                                                                                                          Teaching sequence
                                                                                                          • Diagnose: Ask students how they would describe a test result. Introduce the terms 'number data' and 'description data'.
                                                                                                          • Practice: Provide a simple test scenario and guide students in creating a recording sheet with sections for both types of data. Practice recording data from a demonstration.
                                                                                                          • Feedback: Review recorded data, emphasizing clarity, accuracy, and the distinction between quantitative and qualitative observations. Discuss why both are important.
                                                                                                          Misconceptions
                                                                                                          • Only numbers are important data.
                                                                                                          • Qualitative observations are just 'feelings' and not useful.
                                                                                                          • It's okay to make up data if you forget to record it.
                                                                                                          Required resources
                                                                                                          • Prototypes for testing (e.g., craft stick bridges, paper airplanes)
                                                                                                          • Measuring tools (e.g., weights, rulers, stopwatches)
                                                                                                          • Data recording sheets
                                                                                                          External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                          • Basic numeracy and measurement skills
                                                                                                          • Descriptive vocabulary
                                                                                                          Unit limitations
                                                                                                          • The provided Salvadoran curriculum (sv-cyt) focuses heavily on identifying and classifying simple/complex machines and energy transformations, which are more aligned with science content. It lacks explicit guidance on the methodology of controlled design testing, criteria definition, or evidence-based revision within a design process context for this grade level. Therefore, the micro-skills are primarily derived from the UK Design & Technology curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted to fit the 'Controlled Prototype Evaluation and Revision' learning goal, which is introduced as a key aspect of technological education.
                                                                                                          • The 'evidence_quotes' provide general support for aspects of design and evaluation but do not offer specific pedagogical instructions or grade-level appropriateness for 'controlled testing' or 'repeatable tests'. The interpretation and application to grade 4 are based on general pedagogical principles for design education.
                                                                                                          • The unit does not claim empirical validation for the effectiveness of these specific micro-skills or their sequence. The estimated minutes are hypothetical and may vary significantly based on student prior knowledge and classroom dynamics.
                                                                                                          • The unit does not imply equivalence between international grade levels or curricula. The adaptation is made to fit the stated learning goal within the context of El Salvador's educational framework, as interpreted from the provided documents.
                                                                                                          • The unit's learning goal, as stated in the input ('Use a repeatable test to compare prototypes while keeping relevant conditions consistent'), is broader than the current micro-skills, which focus on evaluating and revising a *single* prototype. The unit's activities are designed to build foundational skills for controlled testing and evidence-based revision, which are prerequisites for effective comparison of multiple prototypes. Future units could build upon this foundation to introduce comparative testing.

                                                                                                            Sources: source-eng-dt-bac6168fed01542b (page 3, Key stage 2, paragraph-p31-9e287d9b54a4b40e, original characters 130–197)

                                                                                                            Propose a specific revision to a prototype based on test evidence.

                                                                                                            micro-propose-revision-from-evidence-9f42e2d6eeed7470 · design_process · T0 · 55 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                            Why this learning matters: This skill closes the design loop, connecting evaluation back to improvement. It teaches students to make design decisions based on data rather than intuition or preference, fostering critical thinking and problem-solving. This is a core, durable design process skill.

                                                                                                            Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 4 students can begin to analyze simple data and make logical connections between observed problems and potential solutions. This moves them beyond simply identifying a problem to actively proposing a fix, a key step in iterative design.

                                                                                                            Evidence of mastery: Students identify a specific failure or weakness in the prototype from the recorded data and suggest a concrete change to address it, explaining the connection between the evidence and the proposed revision.

                                                                                                            Assessment task: After testing a paper cup tower for stability and recording its collapse height, students will propose one specific change to the tower's design to make it more stable, explaining why that change would help based on their test results.

                                                                                                            Access and participation: Provide sentence starters for proposing revisions and explaining reasoning. Allow students to draw their proposed revision. Use simplified test scenarios with clear failure points.

                                                                                                            T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                            Evidence limitations: The quote 'consider the views of others to improve their work' supports the general idea of improving work. However, it does not explicitly support the core requirement of using *test evidence* for revisions, but rather refers to peer feedback. Linking revision to test data is a pedagogical goal of this unit that extends beyond the provided evidence to foster data-driven design thinking.

                                                                                                            Safety: All prototype construction and testing activities must use low-voltage, non-hazardous materials. Ensure proper supervision during any physical testing to prevent injury from falling objects or sharp edges. Emphasize safe handling of tools and materials. No mains electricity or high-voltage components are to be used.

                                                                                                            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                            Success criteria
                                                                                                            • The proposed revision is specific and actionable (e.g., 'add tape to the joints' instead of 'make it stronger').
                                                                                                            • The revision directly addresses a problem identified in the test data (e.g., 'it collapsed because the base was too narrow').
                                                                                                            • The explanation clearly links the evidence (what happened) to the proposed solution (what to change and why).
                                                                                                            Teaching sequence
                                                                                                            • Diagnose: Ask students what they would do if something they built didn't work. Discuss the difference between guessing and knowing why something failed.
                                                                                                            • Practice: Present test data from a failed prototype. Guide students through analyzing the data to pinpoint the failure point and brainstorm solutions. Model connecting evidence to a proposed revision.
                                                                                                            • Feedback: Review student proposals, ensuring they are specific and evidence-based. Challenge students to justify their revisions with 'because...' statements referring to their data.
                                                                                                            Misconceptions
                                                                                                            • Revisions are just about making it look better.
                                                                                                            • I should change everything if it didn't work perfectly.
                                                                                                            • My first idea is always the best, even if the test shows otherwise.
                                                                                                            Required resources
                                                                                                            • Recorded test data (from previous micro-skill)
                                                                                                            • Examples of prototypes that failed in specific ways
                                                                                                            • Whiteboard or paper for brainstorming and drawing revisions
                                                                                                            External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                            • Basic problem-solving skills
                                                                                                            • Understanding of cause and effect
                                                                                                            Unit limitations
                                                                                                            • The provided Salvadoran curriculum (sv-cyt) focuses heavily on identifying and classifying simple/complex machines and energy transformations, which are more aligned with science content. It lacks explicit guidance on the methodology of controlled design testing, criteria definition, or evidence-based revision within a design process context for this grade level. Therefore, the micro-skills are primarily derived from the UK Design & Technology curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted to fit the 'Controlled Prototype Evaluation and Revision' learning goal, which is introduced as a key aspect of technological education.
                                                                                                            • The 'evidence_quotes' provide general support for aspects of design and evaluation but do not offer specific pedagogical instructions or grade-level appropriateness for 'controlled testing' or 'repeatable tests'. The interpretation and application to grade 4 are based on general pedagogical principles for design education.
                                                                                                            • The unit does not claim empirical validation for the effectiveness of these specific micro-skills or their sequence. The estimated minutes are hypothetical and may vary significantly based on student prior knowledge and classroom dynamics.
                                                                                                            • The unit does not imply equivalence between international grade levels or curricula. The adaptation is made to fit the stated learning goal within the context of El Salvador's educational framework, as interpreted from the provided documents.
                                                                                                            • The unit's learning goal, as stated in the input ('Use a repeatable test to compare prototypes while keeping relevant conditions consistent'), is broader than the current micro-skills, which focus on evaluating and revising a *single* prototype. The unit's activities are designed to build foundational skills for controlled testing and evidence-based revision, which are prerequisites for effective comparison of multiple prototypes. Future units could build upon this foundation to introduce comparative testing.

                                                                                                              Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                              • major: The entire unit's methodology, focusing on a design-build-test-evaluate cycle, is absent from the provided Grade 4 Salvadoran curriculum (sv-cyt). Suggested correction: Acknowledge that this unit introduces a new pedagogical framework (design process) not present in the local curriculum source. Justify the introduction of this framework as a necessary component of technology education that complements the existing science content.
                                                                                                              • major: The micro-skill 'propose-revision-from-evidence' is justified with a quote about using peer feedback ('consider the views of others to improve their work').                                                                                                               Machines and energy use

                                                                                                              Relate components to their purpose; keep formal physics as an explicit external dependency.

                                                                                                              Identify simple machines within complex devices.

                                                                                                              micro-identify-simple-machines-in-complex-devices-1bd9e7d3d53dddde · technical_systems · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                              Why this learning matters: This micro-skill builds foundational understanding of how complex systems are composed of simpler, understandable components. It encourages analytical thinking and decomposition, which is crucial for later design and troubleshooting. By identifying simple machines, students begin to grasp the underlying principles of mechanical advantage and force transmission.

                                                                                                              Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 4, students are ready to move beyond merely identifying simple machines in isolation to recognizing their integration within more elaborate mechanisms, as suggested by the curriculum's focus on 'Identificación de tipos de máquinas simples dentro de máquinas complejas.'

                                                                                                              Evidence of mastery: Students correctly label simple machines (e.g., levers, pulleys, wheels and axles) present in a given complex device diagram or physical model.

                                                                                                              Assessment task: Provide students with images or disassembled models of common complex devices (e.g., a can opener, a bicycle gear system, a pair of scissors). Ask them to circle and label at least three different simple machines they can identify within each device.

                                                                                                              Access and participation: Provide tactile models of complex machines for students with visual impairments. Use large print and clear diagrams. Allow verbal responses or use assistive technology for labeling.

                                                                                                              T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                              Evidence limitations: The provided evidence indicates the skill is expected but does not detail specific pedagogical approaches or common misconceptions. The complexity of 'complex machines' is not defined, requiring teacher discretion.

                                                                                                              Safety: When working with electrical circuits, use only low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D-cell) and ensure constant teacher supervision. Emphasize that household electrical outlets are dangerous and should never be tampered with. For construction activities, supervise the use of scissors and other cutting tools. Ensure proper ventilation if using glues with strong fumes.

                                                                                                              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                              Success criteria
                                                                                                              • Correctly identifies at least three simple machines in each complex device.
                                                                                                              • Accurately labels the identified simple machines (e.g., lever, wheel and axle, pulley).
                                                                                                              Teaching sequence
                                                                                                              • Diagnostic: Ask students to list simple machines they know and give examples.
                                                                                                              • Instruction: Review simple machines and their basic functions. Introduce the concept of complex machines as combinations of simple machines.
                                                                                                              • Practice: Provide examples of complex machines and guide students through identifying simple machines within them, discussing their roles.
                                                                                                              • Feedback: Review student identifications, correcting misconceptions about machine types or their presence in complex systems.
                                                                                                              Misconceptions
                                                                                                              • Believing that a complex machine is a single, indivisible unit.
                                                                                                              • Confusing the overall function of a complex machine with the function of its simple machine components.
                                                                                                              • Misidentifying a component as a simple machine when it is part of a larger simple machine (e.g., a gear tooth as a wedge instead of part of a wheel and axle system).
                                                                                                              Required resources
                                                                                                              • Images or physical models of various complex devices (e.g., can opener, bicycle, scissors)
                                                                                                              • Worksheets with diagrams of complex machines
                                                                                                              • Markers or pencils
                                                                                                              External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                              • Basic understanding of levers, pulleys, inclined planes, wheels and axles, wedges, and screws.
                                                                                                              • Define and exemplify basic simple machines.
                                                                                                              Unit limitations
                                                                                                              • The Salvadoran curriculum provides conceptual and procedural content but lacks specific details on the complexity or materials for 'construcción de una máquina compleja,' requiring teacher interpretation.
                                                                                                              • The UK curriculum provides practical examples (e.g., series circuits) but is a general guideline for Key Stage 2, not specifically tailored to Grade 4 or El Salvador's context.
                                                                                                              • The unit does not delve into formal physics calculations (e.g., force, work, power) beyond simple identification of energy sources, as per the 'keep formal physics as an explicit external dependency' instruction.
                                                                                                              • The availability of specific materials for circuit building and complex machine construction may vary and impact the feasibility of hands-on activities.
                                                                                                              Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                • major: The Salvadoran curriculum explicitly requires 'Cálculo de las transformaciones de energía en electrodomésticos y el cuerpo humano' (Calculation of energy transformations in appliances and the human body) and the use of formal SI units (Joules, watts). The proposal's limitation states it 'does not delve into formal physics calculations', directly omitting a required procedural content from the primary source curriculum. Suggested correction: Incorporate a micro-skill that addresses the required content of performing simple calculations of energy transformation, as mandated by the 'sv-cyt' source. This is a non-optional component of the cited curriculum.

                                                                                                                Identify the energy sources used by everyday technological objects.

                                                                                                                micro-identify-energy-sources-in-daily-devices-04d9da6745e82813 · technical_systems · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                Why this learning matters: Understanding energy sources is fundamental to comprehending how technology functions and its impact. This skill connects abstract energy concepts to tangible, everyday objects, making the learning relevant and observable. It lays the groundwork for later discussions on energy transformations and efficiency.

                                                                                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: For Grade 4, identifying energy sources in familiar contexts is an appropriate entry point into the 'energy use' aspect of the unit, as specified by 'Identifica las fuentes de energía de objetos tecnológicos de uso diario.'

                                                                                                                Evidence of mastery: Students correctly list the primary energy source for at least 80% of a given set of common household devices.

                                                                                                                Assessment task: Provide students with a list or images of 10 common household items (e.g., flashlight, television, toy car, stove, clock, fan). For each item, ask them to write down the main energy source it uses (e.g., batteries, electricity, human power, gas).

                                                                                                                Access and participation: Use real objects or high-contrast images. Provide a word bank of energy sources. Allow students to point or use communication devices to indicate answers.

                                                                                                                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                Evidence limitations: The evidence states 'Identificación de las fuentes de energía' but does not specify the depth of understanding required (e.g., distinguishing primary vs. secondary sources, or the origin of electricity).

                                                                                                                Safety: When working with electrical circuits, use only low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D-cell) and ensure constant teacher supervision. Emphasize that household electrical outlets are dangerous and should never be tampered with. For construction activities, supervise the use of scissors and other cutting tools. Ensure proper ventilation if using glues with strong fumes.

                                                                                                                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 1

                                                                                                                Success criteria
                                                                                                                • Correctly identifies the primary energy source for at least 8 out of 10 devices.
                                                                                                                • Differentiates between various energy sources (e.g., electrical, chemical, mechanical).
                                                                                                                Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                • Diagnostic: Ask students where the 'power' for different objects comes from. Discuss their ideas about electricity.
                                                                                                                • Instruction: Introduce common energy sources (electrical, chemical from batteries/fuel, mechanical from human effort, solar). Discuss examples for each.
                                                                                                                • Practice: In small groups, students categorize various objects by their energy source. Discuss any ambiguities.
                                                                                                                • Feedback: Review the categorizations, clarifying misconceptions about energy storage vs. source (e.g., battery stores chemical energy, but the source might be the grid for recharging).
                                                                                                                Misconceptions
                                                                                                                • Confusing the energy storage device (e.g., battery) with the ultimate energy source.
                                                                                                                • Believing all devices use electricity directly from a wall outlet.
                                                                                                                • Not recognizing human effort as an energy source for some devices.
                                                                                                                Required resources
                                                                                                                • Images or actual common household objects
                                                                                                                • Whiteboard or chart paper for listing energy sources
                                                                                                                External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                • Basic understanding of different forms of energy (electrical, chemical, mechanical, solar).
                                                                                                                Unit limitations
                                                                                                                • The Salvadoran curriculum provides conceptual and procedural content but lacks specific details on the complexity or materials for 'construcción de una máquina compleja,' requiring teacher interpretation.
                                                                                                                • The UK curriculum provides practical examples (e.g., series circuits) but is a general guideline for Key Stage 2, not specifically tailored to Grade 4 or El Salvador's context.
                                                                                                                • The unit does not delve into formal physics calculations (e.g., force, work, power) beyond simple identification of energy sources, as per the 'keep formal physics as an explicit external dependency' instruction.
                                                                                                                • The availability of specific materials for circuit building and complex machine construction may vary and impact the feasibility of hands-on activities.
                                                                                                                Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                  Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                  • major: The Salvadoran curriculum explicitly requires 'Cálculo de las transformaciones de energía en electrodomésticos y el cuerpo humano' (Calculation of energy transformations in appliances and the human body) and the use of formal SI units (Joules, watts). The proposal's limitation states it 'does not delve into formal physics calculations', directly omitting a required procedural content from the primary source curriculum. Suggested correction: Incorporate a micro-skill that addresses the required content of performing simple calculations of energy transformation, as mandated by the 'sv-cyt' source. This is a non-optional component of the cited curriculum.

                                                                                                                  Construct a simple series circuit incorporating a switch, bulb, and battery.

                                                                                                                  micro-construct-simple-series-circuit-aef85ed916df2faf · technical_systems · T0 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                  Why this learning matters: This micro-skill provides hands-on experience with electrical systems, a key component of modern technology. It allows students to directly observe the flow of energy and the function of components, fostering an intuitive understanding of circuits. This practical application is essential for demystifying electricity and building confidence in working with technical systems.

                                                                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: While the Salvadoran curriculum mentions 'Transformaciones de la energía' and 'producción de electricidad', the UK curriculum explicitly suggests 'series circuits incorporating switches, bulbs, buzzers and motors' for this age group, providing a concrete, observable skill for energy application.

                                                                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students successfully assemble a series circuit that illuminates a bulb when the switch is closed, demonstrating correct polarity and connections.

                                                                                                                  Assessment task: Provide each student or small group with a battery holder with battery, a light bulb in a holder, a simple switch, and connecting wires. Instruct them to build a series circuit that makes the bulb light up. Observe their construction process and the final working circuit.

                                                                                                                  Access and participation: Use large, easy-to-handle components. Provide color-coded wires. Offer pre-stripped wires or assistance with stripping. Use visual aids and step-by-step diagrams. Allow peer support.

                                                                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Without physical components, students can draw circuit diagrams and label components, but they lose the hands-on experience of building and troubleshooting a functional circuit, which is critical for developing practical technical skills and understanding.

                                                                                                                  Evidence limitations: The UK evidence suggests 'series circuits' but doesn't specify the exact components or the level of detail for construction. The Salvadoran curriculum mentions 'producción de electricidad' but not circuit construction directly, making this an interpretation to provide a practical application of energy concepts, as direct evidence for circuit construction at this specific grade level is not present in the provided sources.

                                                                                                                  Safety: When working with electrical circuits, use only low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D-cell) and ensure constant teacher supervision. Emphasize that household electrical outlets are dangerous and should never be tampered with. For construction activities, supervise the use of scissors and other cutting tools. Ensure proper ventilation if using glues with strong fumes.

                                                                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                                                                  • Connects all components in a continuous loop (series).
                                                                                                                  • Ensures correct polarity for the battery and bulb (if applicable).
                                                                                                                  • Successfully closes the switch to illuminate the bulb.
                                                                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                  • Diagnostic: Ask students what makes a flashlight work. Discuss their ideas about electricity.
                                                                                                                  • Instruction: Explain the basic concept of a circuit (closed loop for energy flow). Introduce components: battery (energy source), bulb (load/indicator), switch (control). Demonstrate a simple series circuit construction.
                                                                                                                  • Practice: Students work in pairs or small groups to construct their own series circuits. Provide troubleshooting guidance.
                                                                                                                  • Feedback: Check each group's circuit. Discuss common issues (loose connections, incorrect wiring) and how to fix them. Reinforce safety rules.
                                                                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                                                                  • Believing electricity is 'used up' at the bulb.
                                                                                                                  • Not understanding the need for a complete circuit.
                                                                                                                  • Confusing parallel and series connections (though only series is taught here).
                                                                                                                  • Ignoring polarity requirements for certain components.
                                                                                                                  Required resources
                                                                                                                  • Low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D-cell)
                                                                                                                  • Battery holders
                                                                                                                  • Small light bulbs (e.g., 1.5V or 3V) in holders
                                                                                                                  • Simple switches (e.g., knife switch, slide switch)
                                                                                                                  • Insulated connecting wires
                                                                                                                  • Wire strippers (teacher use or supervised student use)
                                                                                                                  External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                  • Basic understanding of electricity as a form of energy.
                                                                                                                  • Concept of a closed loop for electrical flow.
                                                                                                                  • Identify basic electrical components (battery, bulb, wire).
                                                                                                                  Unit limitations
                                                                                                                  • The Salvadoran curriculum provides conceptual and procedural content but lacks specific details on the complexity or materials for 'construcción de una máquina compleja,' requiring teacher interpretation.
                                                                                                                  • The UK curriculum provides practical examples (e.g., series circuits) but is a general guideline for Key Stage 2, not specifically tailored to Grade 4 or El Salvador's context.
                                                                                                                  • The unit does not delve into formal physics calculations (e.g., force, work, power) beyond simple identification of energy sources, as per the 'keep formal physics as an explicit external dependency' instruction.
                                                                                                                  • The availability of specific materials for circuit building and complex machine construction may vary and impact the feasibility of hands-on activities.
                                                                                                                  Publication corrections after model review

                                                                                                                    Construct a complex machine that incorporates at least two different simple machines to achieve a specific task.

                                                                                                                    micro-construct-complex-machine-with-simple-machines-18926d608f16aca8 · technical_systems · T0 · 90 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                    Why this learning matters: This micro-skill integrates previous learning about simple machines and their identification within complex systems into a practical, creative task. It fosters problem-solving, design thinking, and hands-on construction skills. By building a machine to achieve a task, students solidify their understanding of mechanical advantage and system integration.

                                                                                                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: The Salvadoran curriculum explicitly states 'Construye una máquina compleja' for Grade 4, making this a direct and central learning objective for the unit.

                                                                                                                    Evidence of mastery: Students design and build a functional complex machine using at least two distinct simple machines, and can explain how each simple machine contributes to the overall task.

                                                                                                                    Assessment task: Challenge students to design and build a 'Rube Goldberg'-style machine (or a simpler version) that uses at least two different simple machines (e.g., a lever and an inclined plane, or a pulley and a wheel and axle) to perform a simple task, such as moving a small object from one point to another or ringing a bell. Students must present their machine and explain its operation.

                                                                                                                    Access and participation: Provide a wide range of materials with varying textures and sizes. Offer pre-cut pieces or adaptive tools for cutting/joining. Allow collaborative work and assign roles based on individual strengths. Use visual instructions and models.

                                                                                                                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                    Evidence limitations: The evidence states 'Construcción de una máquina compleja' but does not specify the complexity, materials, or the number/type of simple machines required. This allows for pedagogical flexibility but requires clear teacher-defined parameters.

                                                                                                                    Safety: When working with electrical circuits, use only low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D-cell) and ensure constant teacher supervision. Emphasize that household electrical outlets are dangerous and should never be tampered with. For construction activities, supervise the use of scissors and other cutting tools. Ensure proper ventilation if using glues with strong fumes.

                                                                                                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                                                    Success criteria
                                                                                                                    • The constructed machine successfully performs the intended task.
                                                                                                                    • Incorporates at least two distinct simple machines.
                                                                                                                    • Students can identify and explain the role of each simple machine in their construction.
                                                                                                                    • The machine demonstrates stable construction and connections.
                                                                                                                    Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                    • Diagnostic: Ask students to brainstorm ways to move a heavy object with minimal effort.
                                                                                                                    • Instruction: Review simple machines and their functions. Discuss examples of complex machines and how simple machines work together. Introduce a design challenge with specific criteria.
                                                                                                                    • Practice: Students sketch designs, select materials, and construct their complex machines in small groups. Provide guidance on construction techniques and problem-solving.
                                                                                                                    • Feedback: Students test their machines and receive feedback on functionality and the integration of simple machines. Encourage iteration and refinement.
                                                                                                                    Misconceptions
                                                                                                                    • Believing that more components automatically make a machine 'complex' without integrating simple machines effectively.
                                                                                                                    • Underestimating the importance of stable connections and precise alignment.
                                                                                                                    • Focusing solely on aesthetics over functionality.
                                                                                                                    Required resources
                                                                                                                    • Assorted construction materials (e.g., cardboard, craft sticks, string, plastic cups, small wheels, dowels, paper clips)
                                                                                                                    • Adhesives (glue, tape)
                                                                                                                    • Scissors, rulers
                                                                                                                    • Small objects for the machine to manipulate (e.g., marbles, small blocks)
                                                                                                                    External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                    • Knowledge of various simple machines and their mechanical principles.
                                                                                                                    • Basic understanding of structural stability and material properties.
                                                                                                                    Unit limitations
                                                                                                                    • The Salvadoran curriculum provides conceptual and procedural content but lacks specific details on the complexity or materials for 'construcción de una máquina compleja,' requiring teacher interpretation.
                                                                                                                    • The UK curriculum provides practical examples (e.g., series circuits) but is a general guideline for Key Stage 2, not specifically tailored to Grade 4 or El Salvador's context.
                                                                                                                    • The unit does not delve into formal physics calculations (e.g., force, work, power) beyond simple identification of energy sources, as per the 'keep formal physics as an explicit external dependency' instruction.
                                                                                                                    • The availability of specific materials for circuit building and complex machine construction may vary and impact the feasibility of hands-on activities.
                                                                                                                    Publication corrections after model review

                                                                                                                      • major: The Salvadoran curriculum explicitly requires 'Cálculo de las transformaciones de energía en electrodomésticos y el cuerpo humano' (Calculation of energy transformations in appliances and the human body) and the use of formal SI units (Joules, watts). The proposal's limitation states it 'does not delve into formal physics calculations', directly omitting a required procedural content from the primary source curriculum. Suggested correction: Incorporate a micro-skill that addresses the required content of performing simple calculations of energy transformation, as mandated by the 'sv-cyt' source. This is a non-optional component of the cited curriculum.

                                                                                                                      Rules and examples

                                                                                                                      Treat any paper classifier as a conceptual model, not evidence of trained-software operation.

                                                                                                                      Students will identify relevant attributes of objects or information for classification based on given criteria.

                                                                                                                      micro-identify-classification-attributes-bf5b261ce3e69972 · data_and_ai · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                      Why this learning matters: Before students can apply classification rules, they must understand which characteristics of data or objects are important for the classification process. This micro-skill focuses on discerning relevant features, a foundational step for data analysis and pattern recognition. It aligns with understanding how information is represented and analyzed.

                                                                                                                      Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 4, students are developing their analytical skills. Identifying attributes is a concrete step that prepares them for more abstract rule application, building on their ability to observe and describe.

                                                                                                                      Evidence of mastery: Students correctly select the attributes that are pertinent to a classification task from a set of options.

                                                                                                                      Assessment task: Given a set of animal cards and a classification goal (e.g., 'classify by habitat'), students will circle or list the attributes on each card that are relevant to that goal (e.g., 'forest', 'water', 'desert').

                                                                                                                      Access and participation: Use tactile objects or large print cards for visual impairments. Provide verbal descriptions and allow oral responses for students with writing difficulties. Use visual aids like attribute charts.

                                                                                                                      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                      Evidence limitations: The cited evidence broadly mentions 'analysis' and 'representation' of information, but does not specifically detail the cognitive process of attribute identification for classification. Its applicability is inferred.

                                                                                                                      Safety: All activities involve only paper, cards, and common classroom objects. No electrical components or hazardous materials are used. Ensure adequate space for sorting activities to prevent tripping hazards.

                                                                                                                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                      Success criteria
                                                                                                                      • Students correctly identify at least 4 out of 5 relevant attributes for a given classification goal.
                                                                                                                      • Students can explain why a chosen attribute is relevant to the classification goal.
                                                                                                                      Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                      • Diagnosis: Present students with various objects and ask them to describe them. Observe if they naturally focus on distinguishing features.
                                                                                                                      • Practice: Introduce a simple classification task (e.g., sorting toys by color). Discuss which features (color, size, material) are relevant for that specific task. Guide students to articulate how an identified attribute could be used to form a sorting rule.
                                                                                                                      • Feedback: Provide immediate feedback on attribute selection, guiding students to understand the link between the classification goal and the necessary attributes.
                                                                                                                      Misconceptions
                                                                                                                      • All attributes are equally important for classification.
                                                                                                                      • Confusing descriptive attributes with classification criteria.
                                                                                                                      Required resources
                                                                                                                      • Physical objects or picture cards with varied attributes (e.g., animals, plants, geometric shapes)
                                                                                                                      • Worksheets or whiteboards for listing attributes
                                                                                                                      External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                      • Basic observation skills (Science)
                                                                                                                      • Vocabulary for describing objects (Language Arts)
                                                                                                                      Unit limitations
                                                                                                                      • The provided source documents offer general guidance on data literacy and information analysis but do not contain specific pedagogical instructions or empirical evidence for teaching classification rules to this age group. The micro-skills are derived through interpretation and pedagogical best practices.
                                                                                                                      • The 'T0' tier (no devices) limits the ability to demonstrate real-world digital classification systems, focusing instead on the conceptual model. Students will not interact with actual AI or machine learning algorithms.
                                                                                                                      • The estimated minutes are pedagogical hypotheses and do not account for individual student learning pace or unexpected classroom interruptions.
                                                                                                                      • The cited evidence from the Argentinian curriculum ('La recolección, análisis, evaluación y presentación de información y el reconocimiento de cómo es representada, recolectada, analizada y visualizada por medio de los dispositivos digitales') is a high-level competency about information handling with digital devices. The specific skills of identifying attributes, applying rules, or evaluating rule fit are pedagogical interpretations of this broader competency, not directly mandated by the source. This unit focuses on the conceptual understanding of information processing and representation, which underpins digital classification, rather than direct digital device interaction.
                                                                                                                      • The total estimated time for the three micro-skills (150 minutes) is a pedagogical hypothesis for the combined teaching and practice time. Teachers should adapt this to their specific classroom context, potentially breaking it into shorter sessions or integrating practice with other subjects.

                                                                                                                        Students will apply a given classification rule to sort examples into predefined categories.

                                                                                                                        micro-apply-classification-rule-b726695ad6c9a3ee · data_and_ai · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                        Why this learning matters: This micro-skill directly addresses the core of the unit goal: applying a rule to examples. It develops logical reasoning and the ability to follow instructions precisely, which are crucial for understanding how automated systems classify data. It connects to the 'analysis' and 'evaluation' of information.

                                                                                                                        Proposed grade placement rationale: Fourth graders are capable of following multi-step instructions and applying logical rules. This skill builds on their ability to categorize and sort, moving from intuitive sorting to rule-based classification.

                                                                                                                        Evidence of mastery: Students correctly sort a set of examples according to a stated classification rule.

                                                                                                                        Assessment task: Given a rule (e.g., 'If an animal has wings, it goes in the 'Flying' box; otherwise, it goes in the 'Non-Flying' box') and 10 picture cards of animals, students will sort the cards into the correct boxes.

                                                                                                                        Access and participation: Use large, clear images for visual examples. Provide rules both visually and orally. Allow students to physically move objects or point to categories. Offer simplified language for rules if needed.

                                                                                                                        T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                        Evidence limitations: The provided text mentions 'analysis' and 'evaluation' of information but does not explicitly define 'classification rule application' as a specific learning outcome. The connection is made through pedagogical interpretation.

                                                                                                                        Safety: All activities involve only paper, cards, and common classroom objects. No electrical components or hazardous materials are used. Ensure adequate space for sorting activities to prevent tripping hazards.

                                                                                                                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                        Success criteria
                                                                                                                        • Students correctly sort at least 8 out of 10 examples according to the given rule.
                                                                                                                        • Students can articulate the rule they used for sorting.
                                                                                                                        Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                        • Diagnosis: Ask students to sort a mixed set of objects (e.g., school supplies) without giving a rule. Observe their implicit sorting strategies.
                                                                                                                        • Practice: Introduce a clear, simple rule (e.g., 'all red items go here'). Model applying the rule with a few examples. Then, have students practice with new examples.
                                                                                                                        • Feedback: Review sorted items, highlighting correct applications and discussing why certain items might have been misplaced, linking back to the rule.
                                                                                                                        Misconceptions
                                                                                                                        • Applying personal preferences instead of the stated rule.
                                                                                                                        • Overlooking specific conditions within a rule.
                                                                                                                        Required resources
                                                                                                                        • Sets of picture cards or physical objects
                                                                                                                        • Labeled containers or areas for categories
                                                                                                                        • Written and/or verbal classification rules
                                                                                                                        External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                        • Basic understanding of 'if/then' logic (Language Arts/Math)
                                                                                                                        Unit limitations
                                                                                                                        • The provided source documents offer general guidance on data literacy and information analysis but do not contain specific pedagogical instructions or empirical evidence for teaching classification rules to this age group. The micro-skills are derived through interpretation and pedagogical best practices.

Students will not interact with actual AI or machine learning algorithms.
