                                                                                                                          Students will evaluate whether an example fits a given classification rule and justify their decision.

                                                                                                                          micro-evaluate-rule-fit-76113c7e197faf40 · data_and_ai · T0 · 55 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                          Why this learning matters: This micro-skill moves beyond simple application to critical thinking. Students must not only apply the rule but also articulate *why* an example does or does not fit, fostering deeper understanding of the rule's logic and its boundaries. This directly aligns with 'evaluación' of information.

                                                                                                                          Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 4, students are ready to articulate their reasoning. This skill encourages them to think metacognitively about the classification process and justify their choices, which is a higher-order thinking skill.

                                                                                                                          Evidence of mastery: Students correctly determine if an example fits a rule and provide a valid reason based on the rule's criteria.

                                                                                                                          Assessment task: Given a classification rule (e.g., 'A fruit is sweet and grows on a tree') and a picture of an item (e.g., a tomato, a mushroom, an apple), students will state whether it is a fruit according to the rule and explain why.

                                                                                                                          Access and participation: Allow verbal justifications. Provide sentence starters for explanations (e.g., 'It fits because...', 'It does not fit because...'). Use visual cues to highlight parts of the rule.

                                                                                                                          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                          Evidence limitations: The term 'evaluación' in the source is broad and does not specifically detail the evaluation of rule fit for classification. The application here is an interpretation within the context of data and AI.

                                                                                                                          Safety: All activities involve only paper, cards, and common classroom objects. No electrical components or hazardous materials are used. Ensure adequate space for sorting activities to prevent tripping hazards.

                                                                                                                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                          Success criteria
                                                                                                                          • Students correctly identify if the example fits the rule for at least 3 out of 4 items.
                                                                                                                          • The student's justification must explicitly reference at least one specific condition from the provided rule.
                                                                                                                          Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                          • Diagnosis: Present a simple rule and an example. Ask students if it fits and why. Observe if they can articulate their reasoning.
                                                                                                                          • Practice: Provide examples that clearly fit, clearly don't fit, and some ambiguous cases. Guide students to use the rule's specific conditions for justification.
                                                                                                                          • Feedback: Discuss student justifications, emphasizing the importance of referencing the rule's criteria rather than personal opinion or general knowledge.
                                                                                                                          Misconceptions
                                                                                                                          • Justifying based on general knowledge rather than the specific rule.
                                                                                                                          • Ignoring parts of a compound rule.
                                                                                                                          Required resources
                                                                                                                          • Picture cards or physical objects
                                                                                                                          • Clearly written classification rules
                                                                                                                          • Worksheets for written justifications or a method for recording verbal responses
                                                                                                                          External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                          • Basic logical connectors (e.g., 'and', 'or', 'not') (Language Arts)
                                                                                                                          • Vocabulary for describing attributes (Language Arts)

                                                                                                                            Identify the precise and unambiguous instructions within an algorithm.

                                                                                                                            micro-identify-algorithm-steps-af3db2875475431f · computational_thinking · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                            Why this learning matters: Understanding that algorithms are sequences of precise, unambiguous instructions is foundational for both designing and debugging programs. This micro-skill ensures students grasp the core concept before moving to implementation.

                                                                                                                            Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 5, students are ready to move beyond simply following instructions to analyzing their structure and precision, a key step in developing computational thinking as per the progression from 'understand what algorithms are' to 'design, construction and debugging' mentioned in the provided sources.

                                                                                                                            Evidence of mastery: Students correctly identify the individual steps and their order in a given algorithm, explaining why each step is precise and unambiguous.

                                                                                                                            Assessment task: Given a pre-written algorithm for a simple, everyday task (e.g., 'making a sandwich') that contains 2-3 ambiguous or imprecise steps, students will identify these steps and rewrite them for clarity and precision.

                                                                                                                            Access and participation: Provide visual aids (e.g., flowcharts, picture sequences) for algorithms. Allow students to dictate their instructions or use assistive writing tools. Pair students for peer review to support diverse communication styles.

                                                                                                                            T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                            Evidence limitations: The cited evidence broadly states the importance of precise instructions in programs but does not specify pedagogical methods or age-appropriateness for this specific skill. The 'Argentina' reference mentions 'diseño, la construcción y la depuración de secuencias de programación' which implies understanding of precise instructions, but doesn't detail the 'identification' aspect.

                                                                                                                            Safety: Ensure proper ergonomics when using computers or tablets. Remind students about responsible use of digital devices and collaborative etiquette.

                                                                                                                            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                            Success criteria
                                                                                                                            • All ambiguous steps in the provided algorithm are correctly identified.
                                                                                                                            • Each identified ambiguous step is rewritten clearly, avoiding ambiguity.
                                                                                                                            • Students can explain why their rewritten steps are more precise than the original.
                                                                                                                            Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                            • Diagnosis: Ask students to write instructions for a simple task (e.g., 'tie your shoelaces') and then have a peer try to follow them exactly, identifying points of confusion.
                                                                                                                            • Practice: Introduce the concept of 'precise and unambiguous instructions' using examples like recipes or assembly guides. Provide several short, simple algorithms (e.g., for brushing teeth, packing a backpack) and ask students to identify and correct ambiguous steps.
                                                                                                                            • Feedback: Review student work, focusing on common ambiguities and discussing how to rephrase instructions for maximum clarity. Emphasize the importance of a single, clear interpretation for each step.
                                                                                                                            Misconceptions
                                                                                                                              Required resources
                                                                                                                              • Worksheets with example tasks and algorithms
                                                                                                                              • Pencils and paper
                                                                                                                              External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                • The provided evidence is high-level and does not offer specific pedagogical strategies or detailed age-appropriate content for each micro-skill. Curriculum design relies on interpretation and best practices.
                                                                                                                                • The 'Argentina' curriculum is a general framework for 'Segundo Ciclo' (Primary second cycle) and does not specify grade-level granularity, requiring assumptions about 5th-grade readiness. The micro-skills are adapted to fit this grade level based on common pedagogical progressions.
                                                                                                                                • The 'England' curriculum provides learning objectives but lacks explicit guidance on how to teach or assess these concepts in a classroom setting.
                                                                                                                                • The estimated minutes are hypothetical and may vary significantly based on student prior knowledge, class size, and teaching style.
                                                                                                                                • The unit's learning goal and micro-skills focus specifically on sequence and debugging. While the original progression statement mentioned 'selection and repetition', these concepts are not covered in this unit to maintain focus and align with the specific learning goal. They are addressed in other units or grades as appropriate for a comprehensive curriculum.

                                                                                                                                  Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                  • major: The unit's 'progression' field states it will cover 'sequence, selection, and repetition'. However, the unit's 'limitations' section and content explicitly state that 'selection and repetition' are deferred to other units to maintain focus. This creates a direct contradiction between the stated scope and the actual content. Suggested correction: Either revise the 'progression' field to accurately reflect the unit's content (focusing on sequence, decomposition, and debugging/testing), or add micro-skills to cover the basics of selection and repetition as stated in the progression.
                                                                                                                                  • minor: The assessment task for 'identify-algorithm-steps' requires students to 'rewrite' ambiguous steps, which is a synthesis-level skill (creating). However, the observable verb is 'Identify' and the cognitive domain is 'knowing', which are lower-level skills. This creates a mismatch between the assessment and the stated cognitive demand. Suggested correction: Align the assessment with the verb and domain. Either change the assessment to focus only on identifying and explaining the ambiguity, or update the observable verb to 'Correct' or 'Refine' and the cognitive domain to 'applying' or 'creating' to match the task of rewriting.

                                                                                                                                  Decompose a small problem into a sequence of named, manageable steps.

                                                                                                                                  micro-decompose-problem-into-steps-b06ea7cf4d400cab · computational_thinking · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                  Why this learning matters: Decomposition is a core computational thinking skill, enabling students to tackle complex problems by breaking them into simpler, more manageable parts. This is crucial for designing effective algorithms and programs, aligning with the 'design' aspect of programming sequences.

                                                                                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 5 is an appropriate stage for students to practice systematic decomposition, moving from identifying existing steps to actively creating them for a given problem. This supports the 'design' component of programming sequences mentioned in the 'Segundo Ciclo' curriculum.

                                                                                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students successfully break down a given problem into a logical series of smaller, distinct steps, each clearly named and contributing to the overall solution.

                                                                                                                                  Assessment task: Students will be given a problem (e.g., 'Plan a birthday party for a friend'). They must list the main tasks involved, then for each main task, break it down into 3-5 smaller, sequential steps. They should present this as a hierarchical list or a simple flowchart.

                                                                                                                                  Access and participation: Provide pre-printed templates for hierarchical lists or flowcharts. Allow students to work in small groups to leverage diverse perspectives. Use real-world, tangible problems that students can physically manipulate or act out to aid understanding.

                                                                                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                  Evidence limitations: The evidence broadly supports 'designing programming sequences' but does not specifically define 'decomposition' or provide guidance on its teaching at this grade level. The connection is inferred from the general nature of computational thinking and the need to break down problems to design solutions.

                                                                                                                                  Safety: Ensure proper ergonomics when using computers or tablets. Remind students about responsible use of digital devices and collaborative etiquette.

                                                                                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                                                                                  • The problem is broken into at least three main tasks.
                                                                                                                                  • Each main task is further broken down into logical, sequential sub-steps.
                                                                                                                                  • The steps are clearly named and contribute to solving the overall problem.
                                                                                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                  • Diagnosis: Present a moderately complex task (e.g., 'getting ready for school') and ask students to list all the steps they would take. Observe if they naturally group related steps or list them linearly.
                                                                                                                                  • Practice: Introduce the concept of decomposition using examples like building with LEGOs or following a recipe. Guide students through breaking down a new problem (e.g., 'making a paper airplane') into main steps and then sub-steps. Use visual organizers like mind maps or hierarchical lists.
                                                                                                                                  • Feedback: Review student decompositions, highlighting effective strategies for breaking down problems and providing constructive criticism on areas where steps might be too large or out of order. Emphasize that there can be multiple valid ways to decompose a problem.
                                                                                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                    Required resources
                                                                                                                                    • Whiteboard or large paper for group work
                                                                                                                                    • Worksheets with problem scenarios
                                                                                                                                    • Pencils and paper
                                                                                                                                    External knowledge to verify

Curriculum design relies on interpretation and best practices.
                                                                                                                                      • The 'Argentina' curriculum is a general framework for 'Segundo Ciclo' (Primary second cycle) and does not specify grade-level granularity, requiring assumptions about 5th-grade readiness. The micro-skills are adapted to fit this grade level based on common pedagogical progressions.

                                                                                                                                        Debug a block-based program by identifying and correcting errors in its sequence of instructions and testing the corrected program.

                                                                                                                                        micro-debug-block-program-sequence-f0ca96730f580e4c · computational_thinking · T2 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                        Why this learning matters: Debugging is an essential skill in programming. By focusing on sequence errors in block-based environments, students learn systematic problem-solving and logical reasoning in a visual, accessible way, directly addressing the 'depuración de secuencias de programación' goal. Incorporating testing ensures students understand the importance of verifying fixes and distinguishing a single successful run from robust functionality.

                                                                                                                                        Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 5, students are ready to engage with debugging simple programs. Block-based environments provide a scaffolded approach, allowing them to focus on logical flow rather than syntax, aligning with 'estructuras simples de código' mentioned in the 'Segundo Ciclo' curriculum. Explicitly including testing reinforces the scientific method of hypothesis and verification.

                                                                                                                                        Evidence of mastery: Students successfully identify and correct at least two sequencing errors in a provided block-based program, and then run a test to confirm the program executes its intended function.

                                                                                                                                        Assessment task: Students will be given a block-based program (e.g., in Scratch or Blockly) designed to make a character move in a specific pattern, but with 2-3 deliberate errors in the order of blocks. They must run the program, identify where it deviates from the intended behavior, rearrange the blocks to fix the sequence, and then describe a test they would use to confirm the fix and why it's a good test.

                                                                                                                                        Access and participation: Provide programs with clear visual feedback for movement or changes. Use large, high-contrast blocks. Offer verbal prompts or checklists for debugging steps. Allow students to work in pairs to discuss and identify errors.

                                                                                                                                        T0 alternative and limits: Without digital devices, students can debug paper-based 'programs' using physical cards representing blocks. However, the immediate feedback and interactive nature of digital block environments are lost, making the debugging process less engaging and potentially less effective for understanding dynamic execution and testing.

                                                                                                                                        Evidence limitations: The 'Argentina' reference mentions 'depuración de secuencias de programación' but does not specify the type of programming environment (block-based vs. text-based) or the specific types of errors to focus on, nor does it explicitly detail the concept of 'testing' beyond debugging. The 'England' reference mentions 'use logical reasoning... to detect and correct errors' but not testing specifically. The choice of block-based programming and explicit testing is a pedagogical adaptation.

                                                                                                                                        Safety: Ensure proper ergonomics when using computers or tablets. Remind students about responsible use of digital devices and collaborative etiquette.

                                                                                                                                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                                                                        Success criteria
                                                                                                                                        • The student identifies the incorrect behavior of the program.
                                                                                                                                        • The student correctly rearranges the blocks to fix the sequencing errors.
                                                                                                                                        • The program executes the intended function after corrections.
                                                                                                                                        • The student describes a relevant test case to verify the fix.
                                                                                                                                        • The student explains why their chosen test case effectively confirms the program's correct operation.
                                                                                                                                        Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                        • Diagnosis: Present a very simple block program with one obvious sequencing error. Ask students to predict its outcome and then run it. Discuss why the outcome was different from the prediction.
                                                                                                                                        • Practice: Introduce common types of sequencing errors (e.g., steps out of order, missing steps, redundant steps). Provide several block-based puzzles where the goal is to achieve a specific outcome by correcting the sequence of blocks. Encourage 'trace' or 'step-through' debugging. Introduce the concept of testing: after making a change, how do you know it's fixed? What inputs or conditions would you test?
                                                                                                                                        • Feedback: Review student solutions, emphasizing the process of identifying the error (what happened vs. what was expected) and systematically testing changes. Discuss how to use trial and error effectively and how to read the code block by block. Discuss the importance of testing different scenarios, not just the one that initially failed.
                                                                                                                                        Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                        • Believing that a program will 'know' what you intend, even if the instructions are out of order.
                                                                                                                                        • Randomly moving blocks without a hypothesis about the error.
                                                                                                                                        • Not testing the program after making a change.
                                                                                                                                        • Believing that one successful run means the program is fully correct and bug-free.
                                                                                                                                        Required resources
                                                                                                                                        • Computers or tablets with a block-based programming environment (e.g., Scratch, Blockly)
                                                                                                                                        • Pre-prepared block-based programs with sequencing errors
                                                                                                                                        External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                        • Basic understanding of cause and effect
                                                                                                                                        • Familiarity with a block-based programming environment (e.g., Scratch, Blockly)

Curriculum design relies on interpretation and best practices.

                                                                                                                                          Responsible information use

                                                                                                                                          Identify simple ways to protect personal data and privacy in digital environments, distinguishing between technical access and permission.

                                                                                                                                          micro-identify-personal-data-protection-7823ef056b00d490 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 55 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                          Why this learning matters: This micro-skill is foundational for digital citizenship, empowering students to take initial steps in safeguarding their online presence. Understanding basic protective measures and the crucial distinction between technical access and permission is vital before engaging with more complex digital interactions.

                                                                                                                                          Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 5, students are increasingly exposed to digital environments, making it appropriate to introduce basic concepts of personal data protection and the nuanced concept of permission versus technical access. This aligns with the 'basic level' guidance for identifying simple ways to protect data.

                                                                                                                                          Evidence of mastery: Students can list or describe at least three simple methods to protect their personal data and privacy in digital contexts, and explain the difference between having technical access to information and having permission to use it.

                                                                                                                                          Assessment task: Students will be given scenarios involving sharing personal information online (e.g., posting a friend's photo, sharing location data) and asked to identify and explain simple protective actions they could take, explicitly addressing whether they have permission to share, even if technically possible.

                                                                                                                                          Access and participation: Provide visual aids and simplified language for scenarios. Allow students to respond verbally, through drawing, or by selecting from a list of options. Use real-world analogies to explain permission vs. technical access.

                                                                                                                                          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                          Evidence limitations: The evidence suggests 'selecting' simple ways, which implies a choice from known options. It does not explicitly detail the depth of understanding required for 'identifying' or 'explaining' these ways, nor does it explicitly mention the distinction between technical access and permission, which is an extension for pedagogical clarity. The success criterion 'Students differentiate between public and private information' was removed as it was not directly measured by the assessment task and is considered prerequisite knowledge rather than a target of this micro-skill.

                                                                                                                                          Safety: All digital content used for examples and practice must be age-appropriate and pre-screened by the teacher to ensure safety and relevance. Discussions should be guided to maintain a respectful and inclusive classroom environment. Emphasize that online safety is a shared responsibility and encourage students to report any concerns to a trusted adult.

                                                                                                                                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                          Success criteria
                                                                                                                                          • Students correctly identify at least three protective measures for each scenario.
                                                                                                                                          • Students explain why each measure helps protect personal data.
                                                                                                                                          • Students correctly differentiate between having technical access to information and having explicit permission to share it in the given scenarios.
                                                                                                                                          Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                          • **Diagnosis:** Ask students what 'personal information' means and where they might share it online. Introduce the concept of 'permission' versus 'technical access' with simple, non-digital examples (e.g., opening a friend's backpack vs. asking to borrow something).
                                                                                                                                          • **Practice:** Present various digital scenarios (e.g., creating an online profile, playing a game, using a messaging app, sharing photos). For each, brainstorm protective strategies as a class, explicitly discussing if permission is needed before sharing, even if the technology allows it.
                                                                                                                                          • **Feedback:** Review student responses, correcting misconceptions and reinforcing effective protection methods and the permission/access distinction.
                                                                                                                                          Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                            Required resources
                                                                                                                                            • Whiteboard or projector
                                                                                                                                            • Markers or pens
                                                                                                                                            • Scenario cards (printed or displayed)
                                                                                                                                            External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                              Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                              • The provided evidence does not offer specific pedagogical strategies or assessment methods, requiring the curriculum designer to infer and create these based on general educational best practices.
                                                                                                                                              • The term 'simple' in relation to privacy policies and data protection is open to interpretation and requires careful contextualization for 5th graders.
                                                                                                                                              • The international nature of the source documents means that specific legal or cultural nuances of El Salvador regarding privacy and data protection are not addressed and would need local adaptation.
                                                                                                                                              • The micro-skill 'evaluate-information-reliability' depends on foundational skills like distinguishing fact from opinion, which may not be universally mastered by all 5th graders. This is addressed by adding 'Basic understanding of facts vs. opinions' to external_knowledge.

This concept is not present in the cited evidence. Suggested correction: Either provide evidence that supports the concept of distinguishing technical access from permission, or remove this concept from the micro-skill statement, evidence of mastery, and assessment task to align with the provided evidence.

                                                                                                                                                Identify simple privacy policy statements regarding how personal data is used in digital services.

                                                                                                                                                micro-identify-privacy-policy-statements-1c9336f69c91ddb3 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                Why this learning matters: Understanding that digital services have policies about data use is a critical step towards informed consent and responsible digital engagement. This skill introduces students to the concept of transparency in data handling.

                                                                                                                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: For 5th graders, identifying 'simple' statements is appropriate, laying the groundwork for later, more complex analysis of privacy policies. This aligns with the 'basic level' of identifying such statements.

                                                                                                                                                Evidence of mastery: Students can point out or describe the section of a simplified privacy policy that explains how personal data is used.

                                                                                                                                                Assessment task: Provide students with simplified excerpts from privacy policies (e.g., for a fictional game or app) and ask them to highlight or verbally indicate the parts that describe how personal data is used.

                                                                                                                                                Access and participation: Use large print and clear, concise language for policy excerpts. Provide audio readings of the text. Allow students to use color-coding or drawing to indicate sections.

                                                                                                                                                  Evaluate the reliability of information and recommendations from digital sources by cross-referencing multiple independent sources.

                                                                                                                                                  micro-evaluate-information-reliability-39c7f76956b9eed3 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                  Why this learning matters: In an age of abundant digital information, critical evaluation of sources and cross-referencing is paramount. This skill equips students to question what they encounter online, fostering media literacy and protecting them from misinformation.

                                                                                                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: Fifth graders are capable of basic critical thinking and can begin to discern obvious signs of reliability or bias in digital content. Expanding this to cross-referencing multiple sources aligns with the progression of evaluating information.

                                                                                                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students can identify at least two indicators of reliability or unreliability across multiple digital sources for a given claim and justify their evaluation.

                                                                                                                                                  Assessment task: Students will be presented with a digital claim and three to four simplified digital 'articles' or 'posts' (some reliable, some unreliable, some contradictory). They will be asked to determine the claim's validity by comparing the sources and explaining their reasoning.

                                                                                                                                                  Access and participation: Use simplified texts and clear visual cues for source evaluation. Provide a checklist of reliability indicators. Allow for verbal responses or drawing to express reasoning. Highlight key phrases in different sources for comparison.

                                                                                                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                  Evidence limitations: The evidence supports the general concept of being 'wary of the reliability of recommendations' but does not explicitly detail the process of cross-referencing or the specific criteria for reliability at this age level. The pedagogical approach for comparing multiple sources is an interpretation. The quote refers to an attitude ('wary') rather than a direct cognitive skill, requiring pedagogical interpretation to translate into an observable performance.

                                                                                                                                                  Safety: All digital content used for examples and practice must be age-appropriate and pre-screened by the teacher to ensure safety and relevance. Discussions should be guided to maintain a respectful and inclusive classroom environment. Emphasize that online safety is a shared responsibility and encourage students to report any concerns to a trusted adult.

                                                                                                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                  • Students correctly identify which sources support or contradict the claim.
                                                                                                                                                  • Students provide at least two valid reasons for their evaluation of each source's reliability (e.g., author, source type, tone, evidence).
                                                                                                                                                  • Students synthesize information from multiple sources to form a conclusion about the claim's validity.
                                                                                                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                  • **Diagnosis:** Ask students how they decide if something they read online is true or false. Discuss the idea that one source might not tell the whole story.
                                                                                                                                                  • **Practice:** Introduce simple criteria for evaluating sources (e.g., 'Who wrote it?', 'Is it a fact or an opinion?', 'Does it look professional?'). Present a simple claim and several contrasting digital sources. Guide students to analyze each source individually, then compare them to cross-check the claim.
                                                                                                                                                  • **Feedback:** Discuss student evaluations, highlighting effective reasoning and addressing common pitfalls in source assessment and the importance of seeking multiple perspectives.
                                                                                                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                  • Believing that anything published online is true.
                                                                                                                                                  • Assuming that popular content is always reliable.
                                                                                                                                                  • Confusing opinion with factual reporting.
                                                                                                                                                  • Believing that if one source says something, it must be true.
                                                                                                                                                  Required resources
                                                                                                                                                  • Printed or displayed examples of digital content (simplified, multiple sources for one claim)
                                                                                                                                                  • Whiteboard or projector
                                                                                                                                                  • Checklist of reliability indicators
                                                                                                                                                  External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                  • Basic understanding of facts vs. opinions.
                                                                                                                                                  • Familiarity with different types of digital content (e.g., news articles, social media posts, advertisements).
                                                                                                                                                  Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                  • The provided evidence does not offer specific pedagogical strategies or assessment methods, requiring the curriculum designer to infer and create these based on general educational best practices.
                                                                                                                                                  • The term 'simple' in relation to privacy policies and data protection is open to interpretation and requires careful contextualization for 5th graders.
                                                                                                                                                  • The international nature of the source documents means that specific legal or cultural nuances of El Salvador regarding privacy and data protection are not addressed and would need local adaptation.
                                                                                                                                                  • The micro-skill 'evaluate-information-reliability' depends on foundational skills like distinguishing fact from opinion, which may not be universally mastered by all 5th graders. This is addressed by adding 'Basic understanding of facts vs. opinions' to external_knowledge.

                                                                                                                                                    Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                    • major: The micro-skill 'evaluate-information-reliability' is based on the evidence 'Wary of the reliability of recommendations'. This evidence describes an attitude ('Wary'), not the cognitive skill of evaluating or cross-referencing multiple sources.                                                                                                                                                     Cite the source of digital information when using or sharing it.

                                                                                                                                                    micro-cite-digital-source-ce40c4f308d81d84 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 40 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                    Why this learning matters: Teaching students to cite sources is fundamental to digital literacy and academic integrity. It fosters respect for intellectual property, helps others verify information, and aligns with responsible information use, preparing them for more formal citation practices.

                                                                                                                                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 5, students are beginning to create and share their own digital content. Introducing the concept of citing sources at a basic level prepares them for more formal citation practices in later grades and promotes ethical digital behavior.

                                                                                                                                                    Evidence of mastery: Students can identify the original source of a piece of digital information and explain why it's important to acknowledge and cite it, demonstrating a simple citation method.

                                                                                                                                                    Assessment task: Students will be given a piece of digital content (e.g., an image, a short text, a video clip) and asked to identify its source (e.g., website, author, platform) and then provide a simple citation for it, explaining why citing is important.

                                                                                                                                                    Access and participation: Provide visual examples of source identification and citation formats. Use simplified language for explanations. Allow students to verbally state sources or point to them on a screen.

                                                                                                                                                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                    Evidence limitations: The evidence 'presenting data and information' broadly supports the idea of sharing information, but does not explicitly detail the act of 'acknowledging' or 'citing' sources. This micro-skill is derived from the learning goal's requirement to 'cite its source' and interpreted for age-appropriateness. The quote itself is a general statement about handling information, not specifically about citing.

                                                                                                                                                    Safety: All digital content used for examples and practice must be age-appropriate and pre-screened by the teacher to ensure safety and relevance. Discussions should be guided to maintain a respectful and inclusive classroom environment. Emphasize that online safety is a shared responsibility and encourage students to report any concerns to a trusted adult.

                                                                                                                                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                    Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                    • Students correctly identify the primary source of the digital content.
                                                                                                                                                    • Students can articulate at least one reason why citing the source is important (e.g., giving credit, allowing others to check, avoiding plagiarism).
                                                                                                                                                    • Students can demonstrate a simple way to cite the source (e.g., stating 'Image from [website name]' or 'Article by [author] on [website]').
                                                                                                                                                    Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                    • **Diagnosis:** Ask students if they've ever used someone else's picture or words online. Discuss what they did or should do.
                                                                                                                                                    • **Practice:** Show examples of digital content (images, short articles). Guide students to find the source information (website URL, author, copyright notice). Discuss why it's important to give credit and how to do it simply (e.g., 'Image from Wikipedia', 'Article by BBC News'). Introduce simple citation formats.
                                                                                                                                                    • **Feedback:** Review student responses, emphasizing the ethical implications of using others' work without acknowledgment and practicing simple ways to cite.
                                                                                                                                                    Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                    • Believing that anything found online is free to use without credit.
                                                                                                                                                    • Not understanding that images or text have owners.
                                                                                                                                                    • Thinking that simply copying and pasting is sufficient.
                                                                                                                                                    Required resources
                                                                                                                                                    • Examples of digital content with clear sources (printed or displayed)
                                                                                                                                                    • Whiteboard or projector
                                                                                                                                                    External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                    • Basic understanding of what constitutes a 'source' (e.g., website, author, publication).
                                                                                                                                                    Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                    • The provided evidence does not offer specific pedagogical strategies or assessment methods, requiring the curriculum designer to infer and create these based on general educational best practices.
                                                                                                                                                    • The term 'simple' in relation to privacy policies and data protection is open to interpretation and requires careful contextualization for 5th graders.
                                                                                                                                                    • The international nature of the source documents means that specific legal or cultural nuances of El Salvador regarding privacy and data protection are not addressed and would need local adaptation.
                                                                                                                                                    • The micro-skill 'evaluate-information-reliability' depends on foundational skills like distinguishing fact from opinion, which may not be universally mastered by all 5th graders. This is addressed by adding 'Basic understanding of facts vs. opinions' to external_knowledge.

                                                                                                                                                      Constraints and trade-offs

                                                                                                                                                      Distinguish necessary functions from optional features; test the revised version.

                                                                                                                                                      Evaluate a prototype against established design criteria, identifying areas for improvement.

                                                                                                                                                      micro-evaluate-prototype-criteria-7eb4b29c12d8b1a2 · design_process · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                      Why this learning matters: This skill is fundamental to the design process, moving students beyond simply creating to critically assessing their work. It reinforces the iterative nature of design and prepares them for making informed revisions.

                                                                                                                                                      Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 5, students are capable of applying criteria to evaluate objects. This builds on earlier design experiences by formalizing the evaluation step, aligning with the unit's goal of revising prototypes under constraints.

                                                                                                                                                      Evidence of mastery: The student provides a clear, structured evaluation of a prototype, linking observations directly to specific design criteria and suggesting actionable improvements.

                                                                                                                                                      Assessment task: Given a prototype (either their own or a provided one) and a list of 3-5 design criteria, students will complete an evaluation sheet, noting how well the prototype meets each criterion, proposing one specific improvement for each unmet criterion, and justifying their evaluation with an observable feature.

                                                                                                                                                      Access and participation: Provide evaluation sheets with clear, simple language and visual aids (icons) for criteria. Allow verbal responses or drawing for students with writing difficulties. Offer sentence starters for justifications.

                                                                                                                                                      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                      Evidence limitations: The source provides a general statement about evaluating against design criteria but does not detail specific methods, tools, or pedagogical approaches for teaching this skill to 5th graders.

                                                                                                                                                      Safety: All activities involving materials should use non-toxic, child-safe items. When using tools, ensure proper supervision and instruction on safe handling. Emphasize that prototypes should be handled carefully to avoid damage or injury.

                                                                                                                                                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                      Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                      • Identifies at least one area where the prototype meets or exceeds a criterion.
                                                                                                                                                      • Identifies at least one area where the prototype does not meet a criterion.
                                                                                                                                                      • Proposes a specific, feasible improvement for each identified unmet criterion.
                                                                                                                                                      • Justifies evaluations with observable features of the prototype.
                                                                                                                                                      Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                      • Diagnosis: Ask students to describe what makes a good toy/tool. Discuss how they know if something is 'good'.
                                                                                                                                                      • Instruction: Introduce the concept of design criteria and how they are used to judge a product's success. Model evaluating a simple object (e.g., a pencil, a chair) against pre-defined criteria.
                                                                                                                                                      • Practice: Students work in pairs to evaluate a simple classroom object using a provided checklist of criteria.
                                                                                                                                                      • Feedback: Review student evaluations, emphasizing objective observation and linking observations to criteria. Discuss common pitfalls like subjective opinions vs. objective assessment.
                                                                                                                                                      • Application: Students apply the skill to their own or a peer's prototype.
                                                                                                                                                      Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                      • Evaluation is just stating whether they 'like' the prototype.
                                                                                                                                                      • Criteria are optional suggestions rather than requirements.
                                                                                                                                                      • Improvements must be major redesigns, not small adjustments.
                                                                                                                                                      Required resources
                                                                                                                                                      • Prototypes (student-made or provided)
                                                                                                                                                      • Design criteria checklist/evaluation sheet
                                                                                                                                                      • Pencils/pens
                                                                                                                                                      External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                      • Basic understanding of what a prototype is.
                                                                                                                                                      • Ability to articulate simple observations.
                                                                                                                                                      Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                      • The concept of 'trade-offs' is central to the unit's learning goal and a dedicated micro-skill, but it is not explicitly mentioned in the provided source texts. Its inclusion is based on pedagogical inference as a necessary outcome of evaluating against conflicting criteria under constraints.
                                                                                                                                                      • The learning goal and the 'propose-trade-off' skill address 'resource constraints' (e.g., limited access to materials), which is inferred from the need to select materials based on functional properties and make design choices under practical limitations, rather than explicitly stated in the source texts.
                                                                                                                                                      • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and notation, which is not directly applicable to the 'design process' strand or the specific learning goal of this unit. Therefore, it has been removed from the unit's evidence base.
                                                                                                                                                      • The learning goal has been adjusted to 'Revise a prototype under material and resource constraints, explaining a trade-off' to align with the scope of the provided evidence, as 'safety' as a design constraint is not explicitly mentioned in the sources.
                                                                                                                                                      Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                        Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                        • major: The learning goal 'Revise a prototype under material and resource constraints, explaining a trade-off' is not fully supported by the provided evidence. The source 'eng-dt' supports evaluation and material selection, but the concepts of 'revising a prototype', 'resource constraints', and 'explaining a trade-off' are pedagogical inferences, not explicit learning outcomes in the text. Suggested correction: Adjust the learning goal to more closely reflect the cited evidence, such as 'Evaluate a prototype against design criteria and select materials based on functional properties to identify potential improvements.' Alternatively, provide a source that explicitly mentions revising prototypes, managing constraints, and making trade-offs at this grade level.
                                                                                                                                                        • major: The micro-skill 'propose-trade-off' is not directly supported by the provided evidence. The cited quote 'evaluate their ideas and products against their own design criteria' does not mention making compromises or trade-offs when criteria conflict. Suggested correction: Either remove this micro-skill or find a source that explicitly requires students to identify conflicting criteria and propose trade-offs. The proposer correctly identifies this as an inference; however, it constitutes a core component of the unit without direct evidentiary backing.
                                                                                                                                                        • minor: The learning goal and the 'propose-trade-off' skill include 'resource constraints', but the provided evidence does not explicitly mention this concept. The source 'eng-dt' focuses on selecting materials based on properties, not on limitations in their availability. Suggested correction: To align with the evidence, reframe the constraint as being inherent to the material's properties (e.g., a material is strong but heavy) rather than an external constraint like scarcity or cost, unless a source for 'resource constraints' is provided.
                                                                                                                                                        • minor: The assessment task for 'evaluate-prototype-criteria' requires students to 'proposing one specific improvement for each unmet criterion'. The cited evidence supports evaluation ('evaluate their ideas and products') and improvement ('to improve their work') but does not specify that an improvement must be proposed for *each* unmet criterion, which could be an excessive requirement for a single task. Suggested correction: Modify the assessment task to require proposing improvements for at least one unmet criterion, which is a more reasonable and directly supported expectation.
                                                                                                                                                        • minor: The unit's progression states 'test the revised version'. However, none of the micro-skills or their associated tasks involve the actual testing of a revised prototype. The unit culminates in proposing a trade-off, not in implementing and testing it. Suggested correction: Remove 'test the revised version' from the unit's progression description to accurately reflect the scope of the included micro-skills.
