                                                                                                                                                        Identify relevant functional properties of common materials for a given design task.

                                                                                                                                                        micro-identify-material-properties-92259a2b7a07c1b5 · design_process · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                        Why this learning matters: Understanding material properties is crucial for making informed design choices and addressing constraints. This skill helps students move beyond arbitrary material selection to purposeful choices based on function.

                                                                                                                                                        Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 5 students can observe and categorize. This skill introduces the vocabulary and conceptual framework for material selection, which is essential for revising prototypes under material constraints.

                                                                                                                                                        Evidence of mastery: The student correctly matches at least three common materials (e.g., paper, cardboard, plastic, fabric) to their primary functional properties (e.g., flexibility, rigidity, absorbency, strength) and explains why a specific property is important for a given design use.

                                                                                                                                                        Assessment task: Given a design challenge (e.g., 'build a waterproof shelter for a small toy') and a selection of materials (e.g., paper, fabric, plastic wrap, cardboard), students will choose the most appropriate material and justify their choice based on its functional properties.

                                                                                                                                                        Access and participation: Provide tactile examples of materials. Use visual cards with property names and corresponding images. Allow students to work in pairs or small groups for hands-on exploration.

                                                                                                                                                        T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                        Evidence limitations: The source mentions 'select from and use a wider range of materials... according to their functional properties' but does not specify which properties or how to teach their identification. The depth of understanding required for 'functional properties' is not detailed.

                                                                                                                                                        Safety: All activities involving materials should use non-toxic, child-safe items. When using tools, ensure proper supervision and instruction on safe handling. Emphasize that prototypes should be handled carefully to avoid damage or injury.

                                                                                                                                                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 1

                                                                                                                                                        Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                        • Selects a material appropriate for the given design challenge.
                                                                                                                                                        • Names at least one functional property of the chosen material.
                                                                                                                                                        • Explains how that property helps meet the design challenge.
                                                                                                                                                        Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                        • Diagnosis: Ask students to sort a collection of materials (e.g., paper, fabric, plastic) based on how they feel or look. Discuss their observations.
                                                                                                                                                        • Instruction: Introduce common material properties (e.g., strong, flexible, waterproof, absorbent). Demonstrate each property with examples. Explain how these properties make materials suitable for different uses.
                                                                                                                                                        • Practice: Provide various materials and ask students to test and describe their properties. Use a graphic organizer to record observations.
                                                                                                                                                        • Feedback: Discuss student findings, correcting misconceptions about properties and reinforcing the link between property and function.
                                                                                                                                                        • Application: Present design scenarios and ask students to select materials based on required properties.
                                                                                                                                                        Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                        • All materials are equally strong/flexible.
                                                                                                                                                        • A material's color is a functional property.
                                                                                                                                                        • The 'best' material is always the most expensive or complex one.
                                                                                                                                                        Required resources
                                                                                                                                                        • Various common materials (e.g., paper, cardboard, fabric, plastic, wood scraps)
                                                                                                                                                        • Tools for testing properties (e.g., water, weights, rulers)
                                                                                                                                                        • Property description cards/chart
                                                                                                                                                        External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                        • Basic vocabulary for describing physical characteristics (e.g., hard, soft, wet, dry).
                                                                                                                                                        Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                        • The concept of 'trade-offs' is central to the unit's learning goal and a dedicated micro-skill, but it is not explicitly mentioned in the provided source texts. Its inclusion is based on pedagogical inference as a necessary outcome of evaluating against conflicting criteria under constraints.
                                                                                                                                                        • The learning goal and the 'propose-trade-off' skill address 'resource constraints' (e.g., limited access to materials), which is inferred from the need to select materials based on functional properties and make design choices under practical limitations, rather than explicitly stated in the source texts.
                                                                                                                                                        • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and notation, which is not directly applicable to the 'design process' strand or the specific learning goal of this unit. Therefore, it has been removed from the unit's evidence base.
                                                                                                                                                        • The learning goal has been adjusted to 'Revise a prototype under material and resource constraints, explaining a trade-off' to align with the scope of the provided evidence, as 'safety' as a design constraint is not explicitly mentioned in the sources.
                                                                                                                                                        Publication corrections after model review

                                                                                                                                                          Propose a design trade-off to address conflicting criteria, explaining the compromise.

                                                                                                                                                          micro-propose-trade-off-aa2a11f95909ae60 · design_process · T0 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                          Why this learning matters: Understanding trade-offs is central to real-world design, where resources are limited and perfect solutions are rare. This skill develops critical thinking and problem-solving by forcing students to weigh competing factors, which naturally arises when evaluating against multiple, potentially conflicting, design criteria.

                                                                                                                                                          Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 5, students can grasp cause-and-effect relationships and make simple decisions. Introducing trade-offs helps them understand the complexities of design beyond simple 'good' or 'bad' outcomes, aligning with the unit's focus on revising prototypes under constraints. This builds directly on evaluating against criteria, especially when those criteria cannot all be perfectly met.

                                                                                                                                                          Evidence of mastery: Given a design problem with conflicting criteria (e.g., durability vs. cost, or strength vs. weight), the student proposes a specific change to the design that sacrifices one aspect to improve another, clearly articulating the compromise made.

                                                                                                                                                          Assessment task: Students are presented with a scenario: 'You designed a toy car that is very fast but breaks easily. You have limited access to stronger, but heavier, materials. What trade-off would you make to improve durability, and what would you sacrifice?' Students must write or draw their proposed change and explain the compromise.

                                                                                                                                                          Access and participation: Use visual scenarios and concrete examples. Allow students to act out or draw their proposed trade-offs. Provide sentence frames for explaining the compromise (e.g., 'If I make X, then Y will happen, but Z will be better').

                                                                                                                                                          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                          Evidence limitations: The provided text implies evaluation against criteria, which can lead to identifying areas for improvement, but it does not explicitly mention 'trade-offs' or how to teach students to make and explain them. This skill is inferred as a necessary step when revising under conflicting criteria and material constraints.

                                                                                                                                                          Safety: All activities involving materials should use non-toxic, child-safe items. When using tools, ensure proper supervision and instruction on safe handling. Emphasize that prototypes should be handled carefully to avoid damage or injury.

                                                                                                                                                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                          Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                          • Identifies the two conflicting aspects of the design problem (e.g., speed vs. durability, strength vs. weight).
                                                                                                                                                          • Proposes a specific design modification.
                                                                                                                                                          • Clearly states what is gained by the modification.
                                                                                                                                                          • Clearly states what is sacrificed or reduced by the modification.
                                                                                                                                                          Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                          • Diagnosis: Present a simple dilemma (e.g., 'You want a big, delicious cake, but you only have a small amount of flour. What do you do?'). Discuss their solutions.
                                                                                                                                                          • Instruction: Introduce the concept of 'trade-off' as making a choice where you give up something to get something else. Provide examples from everyday life (e.g., choosing a cheaper item means it might not last as long). Emphasize that this often happens when design criteria conflict.
                                                                                                                                                          • Practice: Present several simple design scenarios with conflicting constraints (e.g., 'make a strong bridge with limited materials', 'make a comfortable chair that is also easy to move'). In groups, students discuss and propose trade-offs.
                                                                                                                                                          • Feedback: Discuss the proposed trade-offs, highlighting how different choices lead to different compromises. Emphasize that there isn't always one 'right' answer.
                                                                                                                                                          • Application: Students apply the concept to a more complex design problem related to their prototype revision.
                                                                                                                                                          Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                          • A trade-off means finding a perfect solution without any sacrifice.
                                                                                                                                                          • There is always a way to get everything you want in a design.
                                                                                                                                                          • Trade-offs are only about cost, not other factors like performance or aesthetics.
                                                                                                                                                          Required resources
                                                                                                                                                          • Scenario cards/descriptions
                                                                                                                                                          • Whiteboard/chart paper for brainstorming
                                                                                                                                                          • Drawing materials (optional)
                                                                                                                                                          External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                          • Basic understanding of cause and effect.
                                                                                                                                                          • Ability to identify problems and potential solutions.
                                                                                                                                                          Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                          • The concept of 'trade-offs' is central to the unit's learning goal and a dedicated micro-skill, but it is not explicitly mentioned in the provided source texts. Its inclusion is based on pedagogical inference as a necessary outcome of evaluating against conflicting criteria under constraints.
                                                                                                                                                          • The learning goal and the 'propose-trade-off' skill address 'resource constraints' (e.g., limited access to materials), which is inferred from the need to select materials based on functional properties and make design choices under practical limitations, rather than explicitly stated in the source texts.
                                                                                                                                                          • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and notation, which is not directly applicable to the 'design process' strand or the specific learning goal of this unit. Therefore, it has been removed from the unit's evidence base.
                                                                                                                                                          • The learning goal has been adjusted to 'Revise a prototype under material and resource constraints, explaining a trade-off' to align with the scope of the provided evidence, as 'safety' as a design constraint is not explicitly mentioned in the sources.
                                                                                                                                                          Publication corrections after model review

                                                                                                                                                            Mechanisms and control

                                                                                                                                                            Compare open-loop action with feedback using observable states; distinguish a model from a working device.

                                                                                                                                                            Identifies and names common mechanical components within a simple technical system.

                                                                                                                                                            micro-identify-mechanical-components-1a22ec0f813467d1 · technical_systems · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                            Why this learning matters: Understanding the names and basic functions of mechanical components is foundational for analyzing how technical systems work. This skill is durable as the components themselves are fundamental and their names do not change.

                                                                                                                                                            Proposed grade placement rationale: Fifth graders are ready to move beyond simple identification to understanding the role of components in a system, making component identification a prerequisite for deeper analysis.

                                                                                                                                                            Evidence of mastery: Students can point to and correctly name at least three different mechanical components (e.g., gears, pulleys, levers) in a given simple machine or diagram.

                                                                                                                                                            Assessment task: Given a diagram or a physical model of a simple machine (e.g., a bicycle chain and gears, a lever system), students will label or verbally identify at least three distinct mechanical components.

                                                                                                                                                            Access and participation: Provide tactile models for students with visual impairments. Use clear, large print labels and diagrams. Allow verbal responses for students with fine motor challenges.

                                                                                                                                                            T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                            Evidence limitations: The evidence supports the inclusion of mechanical systems but does not specify the level of detail for component identification at this grade level. The quote is from an international curriculum, requiring adaptation to the specific context of El Salvador's 5th grade.

                                                                                                                                                            Safety: All electrical activities must use low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D cells). Students must be supervised at all times when working with electrical components. Ensure wires are insulated and connections are secure to prevent short circuits or overheating. Emphasize that household electricity is dangerous and not to be experimented with.

                                                                                                                                                            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 1

                                                                                                                                                            Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                            • Correctly identifies and names gears.
                                                                                                                                                            • Correctly identifies and names pulleys.
                                                                                                                                                            • Correctly identifies and names levers.
                                                                                                                                                            • Correctly identifies and names cams or linkages (if present).
                                                                                                                                                            Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                            • Diagnosis: Show images of simple machines; ask students to identify any parts they recognize.
                                                                                                                                                            • Practice: Introduce common mechanical components (gears, pulleys, levers, cams, linkages) using visual aids and physical examples. Students practice naming them individually and in groups.
                                                                                                                                                            • Feedback: Provide immediate correction and reinforcement during practice. Use flashcards or interactive quizzes for self-assessment.
                                                                                                                                                            Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                            • Confusing different types of gears (e.g., spur vs. bevel).
                                                                                                                                                            • Mistaking a shaft for a lever.
                                                                                                                                                            • Believing all moving parts are 'motors'.
                                                                                                                                                            Required resources
                                                                                                                                                            • Physical models of simple machines (e.g., toy cars with gears, pulley systems, levers)
                                                                                                                                                            • Diagrams or posters of mechanical components
                                                                                                                                                            • Flashcards with component names and images
                                                                                                                                                            External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                            • Basic understanding of what a 'machine' is.
                                                                                                                                                            Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                            • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and metrology, which, while foundational, is not directly about 'Mechanisms and control' in a technological sense. Therefore, the core micro-skills for this unit are primarily derived from the international curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted for the Salvadoran context and grade level.
                                                                                                                                                            • The international curriculum (eng-dt) provides broad statements about 'understanding and using' systems, but lacks specific detail on the atomic skills, observable criteria, or pedagogical approaches suitable for 5th grade. This requires significant interpretation and design to create actionable micro-skills.
                                                                                                                                                            • The unit focuses on basic identification and function. Deeper analysis of system efficiency, design, or complex control loops is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided evidence.
                                                                                                                                                            • The 'teacher_prep_level' values are estimates based on the complexity of setting up and guiding the activities, assuming a generalist elementary teacher. They do not assume specialized training is available, but rather reflect the time and effort a teacher might need to prepare materials and understand the concepts sufficiently to teach them effectively.
                                                                                                                                                            • The inclusion of a human feedback adjustment micro-skill is an analogy to introduce the concept of observation and adjustment. It is not intended to teach automated feedback theory, which is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided source material. The source does not certify the grade placement for this specific analogy, but it is deemed appropriate for developing basic systems thinking.
                                                                                                                                                            Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                              Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                              • major: The provided Salvadoran curriculum source (sv-cyt) does not support the content of this unit. The source focuses on scientific metrology, measurement, units (SI), and error analysis, not on the design, function, or control of mechanical or electrical systems. Suggested correction: Acknowledge that this unit is based on international standards (eng-dt) and does not align with the provided local curriculum evidence (sv-cyt). Seek appropriate local curriculum standards for technology or design education if available, or justify the unit's inclusion based on other pedagogical needs.
                                                                                                                                                              • minor: The micro-skill 'adjust-based-on-observation' introduces the concept of human feedback adjustment. While pedagogically sound as an introduction to control loops, it is not explicitly supported by the provided evidence. The proposal correctly identifies this as a limitation. Suggested correction: Retain the skill but ensure it is framed as a pedagogical bridge to the concept of control, acknowledging its basis in instructional design rather than direct curriculum evidence. The current limitation note is sufficient.
                                                                                                                                                              • minor: The micro-skill 'model-control-action-effect' requires students to create a model of a control action's effect. The provided evidence supports 'understanding' and 'using' systems, but does not explicitly mention the creation of abstract models (diagrams, structured sentences) by students at this level. Suggested correction: Acknowledge that modeling is an interpretation of 'understanding'. The skill is a reasonable extension for assessing understanding, but it is not directly cited. The current 'evidence_limitations' note is appropriate.

                                                                                                                                                              Describes the basic function of identified mechanical components within a simple technical system.

                                                                                                                                                              micro-describe-component-function-82aa928e131be1ed · technical_systems · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                              Why this learning matters: Moving from identification to understanding function is crucial for developing a conceptual model of how systems work. This skill is durable as the fundamental functions of these components are constant.

                                                                                                                                                              Proposed grade placement rationale: At 5th grade, students can begin to articulate cause-and-effect relationships within simple mechanical systems, building on their identification skills.

                                                                                                                                                              Evidence of mastery: Students can explain in their own words how a gear, pulley, or lever contributes to the operation of a simple machine.

                                                                                                                                                              Assessment task: Students will be given a simple mechanical system (e.g., a hand-cranked toy, a block and tackle) and asked to describe the role of specific components (e.g., 'What does this gear do?', 'How does this pulley help?').

                                                                                                                                                              Access and participation: Provide visual aids with arrows indicating force and motion. Allow students to demonstrate function physically. Offer sentence starters or word banks for verbal descriptions.

                                                                                                                                                              T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                              Evidence limitations: The evidence broadly supports understanding mechanical systems but does not detail the specific depth of functional understanding expected for each component at this grade level. The quote is from an international curriculum, requiring adaptation to the specific context of El Salvador's 5th grade.

                                                                                                                                                              Safety: All electrical activities must use low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D cells). Students must be supervised at all times when working with electrical components. Ensure wires are insulated and connections are secure to prevent short circuits or overheating. Emphasize that household electricity is dangerous and not to be experimented with.

                                                                                                                                                              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                              Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                              • Explains that gears transfer motion and change speed/direction.
                                                                                                                                                              • Explains that pulleys change direction of force or provide mechanical advantage.
                                                                                                                                                              • Explains that levers multiply force or distance.
                                                                                                                                                              • Uses appropriate vocabulary (e.g., 'rotate', 'lift', 'transfer').
                                                                                                                                                              Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                              • Diagnosis: Ask students to predict what a specific component does in a system they haven't seen before.
                                                                                                                                                              • Practice: Demonstrate the function of each component using working models. Students manipulate models and describe what they observe. Group discussions on component roles.
                                                                                                                                                              • Feedback: Provide constructive feedback on descriptions, clarifying misconceptions and refining vocabulary. Peer review of explanations.
                                                                                                                                                              Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                              • Believing gears only increase speed.
                                                                                                                                                              • Thinking pulleys only change direction, not force.
                                                                                                                                                              • Confusing the input and output of a component's function.
                                                                                                                                                              Required resources
                                                                                                                                                              • Working models of simple machines demonstrating component functions
                                                                                                                                                              • Videos or animations illustrating component operation
                                                                                                                                                              External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                              • Basic concepts of force and motion (from Science).
                                                                                                                                                              Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                              • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and metrology, which, while foundational, is not directly about 'Mechanisms and control' in a technological sense. Therefore, the core micro-skills for this unit are primarily derived from the international curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted for the Salvadoran context and grade level.

This requires significant interpretation and design to create actionable micro-skills.

Deeper analysis of system efficiency, design, or complex control loops is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided evidence.
                                                                                                                                                              • The 'teacher_prep_level' values are estimates based on the complexity of setting up and guiding the activities, assuming a generalist elementary teacher. They do not assume specialized training is available, but rather reflect the time and effort a teacher might need to prepare materials and understand the concepts sufficiently to teach them effectively.
                                                                                                                                                              • The inclusion of a human feedback adjustment micro-skill is an analogy to introduce the concept of observation and adjustment. It is not intended to teach automated feedback theory, which is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided source material. The source does not certify the grade placement for this specific analogy, but it is deemed appropriate for developing basic systems thinking.

                                                                                                                                                                Identifies and names common electrical components (e.g., battery, bulb, switch, wire) and their schematic symbols.

                                                                                                                                                                micro-identify-electrical-components-281cd3cec185f0a7 · technical_systems · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                Why this learning matters: Identifying electrical components and their symbols is a foundational step for understanding and safely working with electrical circuits. This skill is durable as the components and their representations are standardized.

                                                                                                                                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: At 5th grade, students are ready to learn the basic components of electrical systems, paralleling their understanding of mechanical components, before attempting to assemble circuits.

                                                                                                                                                                Evidence of mastery: Students can point to and correctly name at least three different electrical components (e.g., battery, bulb, switch) and match them to their schematic symbols.

                                                                                                                                                                Assessment task: Given a set of physical electrical components and a list of schematic symbols, students will match each component to its correct symbol and name.

                                                                                                                                                                Access and participation: Provide tactile components for students with visual impairments. Use clear, large print labels and diagrams. Allow verbal responses for students with fine motor challenges.

                                                                                                                                                                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                Evidence limitations: The evidence supports the inclusion of electrical systems but does not specify the level of detail for component identification or symbol recognition at this grade level. The quote is from an international curriculum, requiring adaptation to the specific context of El Salvador's 5th grade.

                                                                                                                                                                Safety: All electrical activities must use low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D cells). Students must be supervised at all times when working with electrical components. Ensure wires are insulated and connections are secure to prevent short circuits or overheating. Emphasize that household electricity is dangerous and not to be experimented with.

                                                                                                                                                                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 1

                                                                                                                                                                Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                • Correctly identifies a battery and its symbol.
                                                                                                                                                                • Correctly identifies a bulb and its symbol.
                                                                                                                                                                • Correctly identifies a switch and its symbol.
                                                                                                                                                                • Correctly identifies a wire and its representation.
                                                                                                                                                                Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                • Diagnosis: Show images of various electrical components; ask students to identify any they recognize.
                                                                                                                                                                • Practice: Introduce common electrical components (battery, bulb, switch, wire) and their schematic symbols using visual aids and physical examples. Students practice naming and matching them.
                                                                                                                                                                • Feedback: Provide immediate correction and reinforcement during practice. Use flashcards or interactive quizzes for self-assessment.
                                                                                                                                                                Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                • Confusing different types of switches.
                                                                                                                                                                • Believing all wires are the same regardless of insulation.
                                                                                                                                                                • Not understanding the polarity of a battery.
                                                                                                                                                                Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                • Physical electrical components (batteries, bulbs, switches, wires)
                                                                                                                                                                • Diagrams or posters of electrical components and their symbols
                                                                                                                                                                • Flashcards with component names, images, and symbols
                                                                                                                                                                External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                • Basic understanding of electricity (from Science).
                                                                                                                                                                Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and metrology, which, while foundational, is not directly about 'Mechanisms and control' in a technological sense. Therefore, the core micro-skills for this unit are primarily derived from the international curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted for the Salvadoran context and grade level.
                                                                                                                                                                • The international curriculum (eng-dt) provides broad statements about 'understanding and using' systems, but lacks specific detail on the atomic skills, observable criteria, or pedagogical approaches suitable for 5th grade. This requires significant interpretation and design to create actionable micro-skills.
                                                                                                                                                                • The unit focuses on basic identification and function. Deeper analysis of system efficiency, design, or complex control loops is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided evidence.
                                                                                                                                                                • The 'teacher_prep_level' values are estimates based on the complexity of setting up and guiding the activities, assuming a generalist elementary teacher. They do not assume specialized training is available, but rather reflect the time and effort a teacher might need to prepare materials and understand the concepts sufficiently to teach them effectively.
                                                                                                                                                                • The inclusion of a human feedback adjustment micro-skill is an analogy to introduce the concept of observation and adjustment. It is not intended to teach automated feedback theory, which is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided source material. The source does not certify the grade placement for this specific analogy, but it is deemed appropriate for developing basic systems thinking.
                                                                                                                                                                Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                  Uncalibrated model critique: points to review

The source focuses on scientific metrology, measurement, units (SI), and error analysis, not on the design, function, or control of mechanical or electrical systems. Suggested correction: Acknowledge that this unit is based on international standards (eng-dt) and does not align with the provided local curriculum evidence (sv-cyt). Seek appropriate local curriculum standards for technology or design education if available, or justify the unit's inclusion based on other pedagogical needs.
                                                                                                                                                                  • minor: The micro-skill 'adjust-based-on-observation' introduces the concept of human feedback adjustment. While pedagogically sound as an introduction to control loops, it is not explicitly supported by the provided evidence. The proposal correctly identifies this as a limitation. Suggested correction: Retain the skill but ensure it is framed as a pedagogical bridge to the concept of control, acknowledging its basis in instructional design rather than direct curriculum evidence. The current limitation note is sufficient.
                                                                                                                                                                  • minor: The micro-skill 'model-control-action-effect' requires students to create a model of a control action's effect. The provided evidence supports 'understanding' and 'using' systems, but does not explicitly mention the creation of abstract models (diagrams, structured sentences) by students at this level. Suggested correction: Acknowledge that modeling is an interpretation of 'understanding'. The skill is a reasonable extension for assessing understanding, but it is not directly cited. The current 'evidence_limitations' note is appropriate.

                                                                                                                                                                  Assembles a simple series circuit incorporating a switch, bulb, and battery.

                                                                                                                                                                  micro-assemble-simple-circuit-1a1e38f62a2b9d45 · technical_systems · T0 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                  Why this learning matters: Hands-on assembly of a basic circuit provides concrete experience with electrical systems, which are fundamental to control. This skill is durable as the principles of simple circuits remain constant.

                                                                                                                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: Fifth graders are capable of following sequential instructions and observing cause-and-effect in electrical systems, making this an appropriate practical skill.

                                                                                                                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students can successfully connect a battery, switch, and bulb to create a working series circuit that lights the bulb when the switch is closed.

                                                                                                                                                                  Assessment task: Given a battery, a bulb in a holder, a switch, and connecting wires, students will construct a series circuit that illuminates the bulb when the switch is activated.

                                                                                                                                                                  Access and participation: Use large, easy-to-handle components. Provide color-coded wires. Offer visual step-by-step instructions or a pre-assembled example. Allow peer support.

                                                                                                                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Without physical components, students can draw circuit diagrams and label components, but they lose the operational experience of building and troubleshooting a working circuit, which is critical for understanding electrical systems.

                                                                                                                                                                  Evidence limitations: The evidence supports the inclusion of series circuits but does not specify the exact components or the level of complexity for assembly at this grade. The quote is from an international curriculum, requiring adaptation to the specific context of El Salvador's 5th grade.

                                                                                                                                                                  Safety: All electrical activities must use low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D cells). Students must be supervised at all times when working with electrical components. Ensure wires are insulated and connections are secure to prevent short circuits or overheating. Emphasize that household electricity is dangerous and not to be experimented with.

                                                                                                                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                  • Connects components in a series configuration.
                                                                                                                                                                  • Ensures all connections are secure.
                                                                                                                                                                  • Activates the switch to complete the circuit.
                                                                                                                                                                  • The bulb illuminates when the switch is closed.
                                                                                                                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                  • Diagnosis: Ask students to draw how they think a light bulb turns on. Discuss basic circuit components.
                                                                                                                                                                  • Practice: Demonstrate correct assembly of a series circuit. Students work in pairs or small groups to assemble their own circuits, following step-by-step instructions.
                                                                                                                                                                  • Feedback: Circulate to provide immediate feedback on connections. Troubleshoot non-working circuits together, guiding students to identify errors.
                                                                                                                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                  • Believing electricity flows only one way through a component.
                                                                                                                                                                  • Not understanding the need for a complete circuit (closed loop).
                                                                                                                                                                  • Confusing parallel and series connections.
                                                                                                                                                                  • Thinking a switch generates power.
                                                                                                                                                                  Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                  • Low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D cells)
                                                                                                                                                                  • Battery holders
                                                                                                                                                                  • Small light bulbs (e.g., flashlight bulbs)
                                                                                                                                                                  • Bulb holders
                                                                                                                                                                  • Simple switches (e.g., knife switches, push-button switches)
                                                                                                                                                                  • Insulated connecting wires with alligator clips
                                                                                                                                                                  External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                  • Basic understanding of electricity and conductors/insulators (from Science).
                                                                                                                                                                  Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                  • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and metrology, which, while foundational, is not directly about 'Mechanisms and control' in a technological sense. Therefore, the core micro-skills for this unit are primarily derived from the international curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted for the Salvadoran context and grade level.
                                                                                                                                                                  • The international curriculum (eng-dt) provides broad statements about 'understanding and using' systems, but lacks specific detail on the atomic skills, observable criteria, or pedagogical approaches suitable for 5th grade. This requires significant interpretation and design to create actionable micro-skills.
                                                                                                                                                                  • The unit focuses on basic identification and function. Deeper analysis of system efficiency, design, or complex control loops is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided evidence.
                                                                                                                                                                  • The 'teacher_prep_level' values are estimates based on the complexity of setting up and guiding the activities, assuming a generalist elementary teacher. They do not assume specialized training is available, but rather reflect the time and effort a teacher might need to prepare materials and understand the concepts sufficiently to teach them effectively.
                                                                                                                                                                  • The inclusion of a human feedback adjustment micro-skill is an analogy to introduce the concept of observation and adjustment. It is not intended to teach automated feedback theory, which is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided source material. The source does not certify the grade placement for this specific analogy, but it is deemed appropriate for developing basic systems thinking.

                                                                                                                                                                    Predicts the effect of opening or closing a switch on a simple series circuit.

                                                                                                                                                                    micro-predict-circuit-behavior-2993a20af5afda56 · technical_systems · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                    Why this learning matters: Predicting circuit behavior demonstrates an understanding of the control action (switch) and its effect on the system's output (bulb). This reasoning skill is durable and transferable to more complex systems.

                                                                                                                                                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: Fifth graders can apply their understanding of circuit completion to predict outcomes, moving beyond simple observation to basic causal reasoning.

                                                                                                                                                                    Evidence of mastery: Students can accurately state whether a bulb will light up or turn off when a switch in a series circuit is opened or closed, and explain why.

                                                                                                                                                                    Assessment task: Given a diagram of a simple series circuit with a switch, students will be asked: 'What will happen to the bulb if the switch is opened/closed?' and 'Why?'

                                                                                                                                                                    Access and participation: Use clear, large circuit diagrams. Allow verbal predictions and explanations. Provide sentence frames for explanations.

                                                                                                                                                                    T0 alternative and limits: Without physical circuits, students can only make predictions based on diagrams and theoretical understanding, losing the empirical validation and hands-on experience that reinforces the concept.

                                                                                                                                                                    Evidence limitations: The evidence supports understanding electrical systems but does not explicitly detail the prediction of switch behavior. The quote is from an international curriculum, requiring adaptation to the specific context of El Salvador's 5th grade.

                                                                                                                                                                    Safety: All electrical activities must use low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D cells). Students must be supervised at all times when working with electrical components. Ensure wires are insulated and connections are secure to prevent short circuits or overheating. Emphasize that household electricity is dangerous and not to be experimented with.

                                                                                                                                                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                    Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                    • Correctly predicts that the bulb will turn off when the switch is opened.
                                                                                                                                                                    • Correctly predicts that the bulb will light up when the switch is closed.
                                                                                                                                                                    • Explains that an open switch breaks the circuit, stopping the flow of electricity.
                                                                                                                                                                    • Explains that a closed switch completes the circuit, allowing electricity to flow.
                                                                                                                                                                    Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                    • Diagnosis: Present a circuit diagram and ask students to draw what happens when the switch is flipped.
                                                                                                                                                                    • Practice: Students experiment with their assembled circuits, observing the effect of the switch. Discuss observations and formulate predictions for different scenarios (e.g., 'What if we add another switch?').
                                                                                                                                                                    • Feedback: Review predictions against actual observations. Guide students to articulate the underlying principles of circuit completion and interruption.
                                                                                                                                                                    Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                    • Believing the switch 'stores' electricity.
                                                                                                                                                                    • Thinking the switch only controls the bulb, not the entire circuit.
                                                                                                                                                                    • Not understanding that an open switch creates a gap.
                                                                                                                                                                    Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                    • Diagrams of simple series circuits with switches
                                                                                                                                                                    • Assembled simple series circuits (from previous micro-skill)
                                                                                                                                                                    External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                    • Concept of a complete circuit (from Science).
                                                                                                                                                                    Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                    • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and metrology, which, while foundational, is not directly about 'Mechanisms and control' in a technological sense. Therefore, the core micro-skills for this unit are primarily derived from the international curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted for the Salvadoran context and grade level.
                                                                                                                                                                    • The international curriculum (eng-dt) provides broad statements about 'understanding and using' systems, but lacks specific detail on the atomic skills, observable criteria, or pedagogical approaches suitable for 5th grade. This requires significant interpretation and design to create actionable micro-skills.
                                                                                                                                                                    • The unit focuses on basic identification and function. Deeper analysis of system efficiency, design, or complex control loops is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided evidence.
                                                                                                                                                                    • The 'teacher_prep_level' values are estimates based on the complexity of setting up and guiding the activities, assuming a generalist elementary teacher. They do not assume specialized training is available, but rather reflect the time and effort a teacher might need to prepare materials and understand the concepts sufficiently to teach them effectively.
                                                                                                                                                                    • The inclusion of a human feedback adjustment micro-skill is an analogy to introduce the concept of observation and adjustment. It is not intended to teach automated feedback theory, which is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided source material. The source does not certify the grade placement for this specific analogy, but it is deemed appropriate for developing basic systems thinking.
