                                                                                                                                                                      Adjusts a simple control action based on observing the system's output to achieve a desired result.

                                                                                                                                                                      micro-adjust-based-on-observation-3a44872b9b1d4c7f · technical_systems · T0 · 45 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                      Why this learning matters: This micro-skill introduces the fundamental concept of human feedback in a practical, observable way, distinguishing it from automated feedback. It reinforces the idea that control actions are often iterative and require observation and adjustment, a durable skill applicable across many contexts.

                                                                                                                                                                      Proposed grade placement rationale: Fifth graders are capable of observing outcomes and making simple, iterative adjustments, which is a foundational step for understanding control systems.

                                                                                                                                                                      Evidence of mastery: Students can make a deliberate change to a control (e.g., turn a knob, push a lever) after observing the system's output (e.g., light intensity, object position) to move closer to a target.

                                                                                                                                                                      Assessment task: Given a simple system (e.g., a toy car with a steering wheel, a light with a dimmer switch, a catapult aiming at a target), students will be asked to adjust the control to achieve a specific output (e.g., make the car go straight, set the light to a medium brightness, hit the target).

                                                                                                                                                                      Access and participation: Provide controls that are easy to manipulate. Use clear visual or auditory cues for system output. Allow for verbal descriptions of observations and intended adjustments.

                                                                                                                                                                      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                      Evidence limitations: The provided evidence broadly supports understanding and using mechanical/electrical systems but does not explicitly detail human feedback adjustment as a specific skill. The quote is used to broadly support the interaction with and control of systems.

                                                                                                                                                                      Safety: All electrical activities must use low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D cells). Students must be supervised at all times when working with electrical components. Ensure wires are insulated and connections are secure to prevent short circuits or overheating. Emphasize that household electricity is dangerous and not to be experimented with.

                                                                                                                                                                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 1

                                                                                                                                                                      Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                      • Observes the current output of the system.
                                                                                                                                                                      • Identifies the discrepancy between the current and desired output.
                                                                                                                                                                      • Makes a deliberate adjustment to the control.
                                                                                                                                                                      • Repeats the observation and adjustment process until the desired output is achieved or approximated.
                                                                                                                                                                      Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                      • Diagnosis: Ask students how they would adjust a faucet to get the right water temperature. Discuss how they know if it's right.
                                                                                                                                                                      • Practice: Provide simple systems (e.g., a toy car that veers, a light with a dimmer, a simple catapult). Students practice adjusting controls to achieve a target output, focusing on observation and iterative adjustment.
                                                                                                                                                                      • Feedback: Discuss strategies for adjustment. Emphasize that it's okay to make small changes and observe, rather than one big guess. Highlight the 'observe-adjust-observe' cycle.
                                                                                                                                                                      Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                      • Believing a single adjustment will always be perfect.
                                                                                                                                                                      • Not understanding that observation is key to effective adjustment.
                                                                                                                                                                      • Confusing the control mechanism with the feedback loop itself.
                                                                                                                                                                      Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                      • Simple mechanical or electrical systems with adjustable controls and observable outputs (e.g., toy cars with steering, dimmer lights, simple catapults, water faucets)
                                                                                                                                                                      • Targets or desired output states for adjustment tasks
                                                                                                                                                                      External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                        Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                        • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and metrology, which, while foundational, is not directly about 'Mechanisms and control' in a technological sense. Therefore, the core micro-skills for this unit are primarily derived from the international curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted for the Salvadoran context and grade level.
                                                                                                                                                                        • The international curriculum (eng-dt) provides broad statements about 'understanding and using' systems, but lacks specific detail on the atomic skills, observable criteria, or pedagogical approaches suitable for 5th grade. This requires significant interpretation and design to create actionable micro-skills.
                                                                                                                                                                        • The unit focuses on basic identification and function. Deeper analysis of system efficiency, design, or complex control loops is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided evidence.
                                                                                                                                                                        • The 'teacher_prep_level' values are estimates based on the complexity of setting up and guiding the activities, assuming a generalist elementary teacher. They do not assume specialized training is available, but rather reflect the time and effort a teacher might need to prepare materials and understand the concepts sufficiently to teach them effectively.
                                                                                                                                                                        • The inclusion of a human feedback adjustment micro-skill is an analogy to introduce the concept of observation and adjustment. It is not intended to teach automated feedback theory, which is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided source material. The source does not certify the grade placement for this specific analogy, but it is deemed appropriate for developing basic systems thinking.

                                                                                                                                                                          Models how a simple control action changes the output of a technical system using diagrams or verbal descriptions.

                                                                                                                                                                          micro-model-control-action-effect-11df503683df4bc5 · technical_systems · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                          Why this learning matters: This micro-skill directly addresses the unit's learning goal by requiring students to synthesize their understanding of components, functions, and control actions into a coherent model. It reinforces cause-and-effect relationships in technical systems.

                                                                                                                                                                          Proposed grade placement rationale: At 5th grade, students are capable of abstracting observed phenomena into simple models, which is a key step in developing systems thinking.

                                                                                                                                                                          Evidence of mastery: Students can create a labeled diagram or a structured sentence that accurately represents a control action (e.g., closing a switch, turning a crank) and its resulting change in system output (e.g., bulb lights up, object moves).

                                                                                                                                                                          Assessment task: Students will choose one simple technical system (either mechanical or electrical) and create a model (diagram with labels and arrows, or a structured paragraph) that shows: 'When I [control action], the [system component] does [function], which causes the [output] to change.'

                                                                                                                                                                          Access and participation: Provide templates for diagrams or sentence frames for verbal models. Allow students to use preferred modalities (drawing, writing, speaking). Offer examples of well-constructed models.

                                                                                                                                                                          T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                          Evidence limitations: The evidence supports understanding and using systems but does not explicitly detail the creation of models for control actions at this grade level. The quote is from an international curriculum, requiring adaptation to the specific context of El Salvador's 5th grade.

                                                                                                                                                                          Safety: All electrical activities must use low-voltage batteries (e.g., AA, D cells). Students must be supervised at all times when working with electrical components. Ensure wires are insulated and connections are secure to prevent short circuits or overheating. Emphasize that household electricity is dangerous and not to be experimented with.

                                                                                                                                                                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                          Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                          • Clearly identifies the control action (input).
                                                                                                                                                                          • Clearly identifies the system component affected by the control action.
                                                                                                                                                                          • Accurately describes the function or change in state of the component.
                                                                                                                                                                          • Clearly identifies the resulting change in the system's output.
                                                                                                                                                                          • Uses appropriate labels and arrows in diagrams, or clear language in descriptions.
                                                                                                                                                                          Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                          • Diagnosis: Ask students to verbally describe how a simple device (e.g., a flashlight) works from input to output.
                                                                                                                                                                          • Practice: Introduce different ways to model system behavior (e.g., flowcharts, labeled diagrams, structured sentences). Students practice modeling various simple mechanical and electrical systems they have studied.
                                                                                                                                                                          • Feedback: Review student models, providing feedback on clarity, accuracy, and completeness. Encourage peer review and refinement of models.
                                                                                                                                                                          Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                          • Confusing the control action with the system's internal function.
                                                                                                                                                                          • Omitting intermediate steps in the cause-and-effect chain.
                                                                                                                                                                          • Failing to clearly distinguish between input, process, and output.
                                                                                                                                                                          Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                          • Examples of simple technical systems (physical or diagrams)
                                                                                                                                                                          • Modeling tools (paper, pencils, digital drawing tools if T2/T3)
                                                                                                                                                                          • Templates for structured sentences or diagrams
                                                                                                                                                                          External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                            Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                            • The provided source material from El Salvador (sv-cyt) is heavily focused on scientific measurement and metrology, which, while foundational, is not directly about 'Mechanisms and control' in a technological sense. Therefore, the core micro-skills for this unit are primarily derived from the international curriculum (eng-dt) and adapted for the Salvadoran context and grade level.
                                                                                                                                                                            • The international curriculum (eng-dt) provides broad statements about 'understanding and using' systems, but lacks specific detail on the atomic skills, observable criteria, or pedagogical approaches suitable for 5th grade. This requires significant interpretation and design to create actionable micro-skills.
                                                                                                                                                                            • The unit focuses on basic identification and function. Deeper analysis of system efficiency, design, or complex control loops is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided evidence.
                                                                                                                                                                            • The 'teacher_prep_level' values are estimates based on the complexity of setting up and guiding the activities, assuming a generalist elementary teacher. They do not assume specialized training is available, but rather reflect the time and effort a teacher might need to prepare materials and understand the concepts sufficiently to teach them effectively.
                                                                                                                                                                            • The inclusion of a human feedback adjustment micro-skill is an analogy to introduce the concept of observation and adjustment. It is not intended to teach automated feedback theory, which is beyond the scope of this grade level and the provided source material. The source does not certify the grade placement for this specific analogy, but it is deemed appropriate for developing basic systems thinking.
                                                                                                                                                                            Publication corrections after model review

Suggested correction: Acknowledge that this unit is based on international standards (eng-dt) and does not align with the provided local curriculum evidence (sv-cyt). Seek appropriate local curriculum standards for technology or design education if available, or justify the unit's inclusion based on other pedagogical needs.
                                                                                                                                                                              • minor: The micro-skill 'adjust-based-on-observation' introduces the concept of human feedback adjustment.                                                                                                                                                                               Samples and prediction

                                                                                                                                                                              Separate practice examples from test examples; interpret mistakes without probability formulas.

                                                                                                                                                                              Evaluates the credibility and reliability of simple data sources for a given purpose.

                                                                                                                                                                              micro-evaluate-data-reliability-3b03139ee6309f32 · data_and_ai · T1 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                              Why this learning matters: Before students can understand how unrepresentative data affects predictions, they must first grasp the concept of 'good' versus 'bad' data. Evaluating reliability is a critical precursor to understanding data quality and potential bias.

                                                                                                                                                                              Proposed grade placement rationale: Fifth graders are developing critical thinking skills and can begin to question sources, especially with guidance. This aligns with the 'detect the credibility' competency for basic levels.

                                                                                                                                                                              Evidence of mastery: Students can compare two simple data sources (e.g., two short texts or images) and articulate reasons why one might be more credible or reliable than the other for a specific question.

                                                                                                                                                                              Assessment task: Students are given two short, contrasting 'facts' or 'observations' about a simple, non-personal topic (e.g., 'all birds fly' vs. 'some birds fly'), each attributed to a different, simple source (e.g., 'a friend said' vs. 'a nature documentary'). They must choose which source is more reliable and explain why.

                                                                                                                                                                              Access and participation: Provide audio versions of text sources. Use visual cues to highlight key information. Allow group discussion and peer support for analysis. Offer sentence starters for explanations.

                                                                                                                                                                              T0 alternative and limits: Teacher prints out curated examples of simple data sources or writes them on a whiteboard for students to analyze. Discussion and responses are verbal or written on paper.

                                                                                                                                                                              Evidence limitations: The quote refers to 'common sources of data, information and their digital content' but doesn't specify the complexity or type of data. The focus here is on simple, easily discernible reliability factors. The source does not explicitly mention 'bias' or 'unrepresentative data' in the context of evaluation, but 'credibility and reliability' are foundational to understanding data quality.

                                                                                                                                                                              Safety: No specific safety notes are required for this unit as it involves no physical components or high-risk digital activities. Ensure appropriate digital citizenship guidelines are followed when accessing any online resources.

                                                                                                                                                                              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                              Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                              • Student identifies the more reliable source.
                                                                                                                                                                              • Student provides at least one valid reason for their choice (e.g., expertise, potential bias, evidence).
                                                                                                                                                                              • Student can articulate why the other source is less reliable.
                                                                                                                                                                              Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                              • Diagnostic: Present a 'fact' and ask students how they would check if it's true.
                                                                                                                                                                              • Practice: Discuss criteria for reliability (who said it, where did it come from, is there evidence?).
                                                                                                                                                                              • Practice: Analyze simple examples of reliable vs. unreliable information (e.g., a news report vs. a rumor).
                                                                                                                                                                              • Feedback: Discuss common pitfalls in evaluating sources.
                                                                                                                                                                              Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                              • All information found online is true.
                                                                                                                                                                              • More information means more reliable information.
                                                                                                                                                                              Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                              • Teacher device with projector (optional)
                                                                                                                                                                              • Curated examples of simple data sources (text, images)
                                                                                                                                                                              • Worksheets or digital forms for responses
                                                                                                                                                                              External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                              • Basic understanding of different types of information sources (e.g., personal opinion, expert opinion, observation)
                                                                                                                                                                              • Concept of bias
                                                                                                                                                                              Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                              • The provided source texts offer general competencies for data and information literacy but do not specify the depth or complexity of 'prediction' or 'unrepresentative examples' for 5th grade. The micro-skills are designed to be age-appropriate interpretations.
                                                                                                                                                                              • The unit does not delve into complex statistical concepts or machine learning algorithms, focusing instead on observable patterns and logical reasoning.
                                                                                                                                                                              • The 'T1' tier for some skills implies a teacher device, but the core concepts can be introduced with T0 alternatives, albeit with reduced efficiency or engagement.
                                                                                                                                                                              • The unit does not claim empirical validation or equivalence with international standards; it is a curricular proposal based on the provided texts and pedagogical principles.
                                                                                                                                                                              • The source statements about 'evaluation' (e.g., 'recolección, análisis, evaluación y presentación de información') provide a general basis for critical thinking about data, but they do not empirically validate specific instructional methods for teaching data bias or the impact of unrepresentative samples. The connection is a pedagogical interpretation.
                                                                                                                                                                              • The unit focuses on concrete, non-personal data examples to ensure age-appropriateness and avoid privacy concerns.
                                                                                                                                                                              Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                • major: The learning goal 'Test how missing or unrepresentative examples affect a simple classifier or prediction' is not directly supported by the provided evidence. The sources mention general data analysis and evaluation but do not specify concepts like 'unrepresentative examples', 'classifiers', or the impact of data quality on predictions. Suggested correction: Either provide evidence that explicitly supports teaching about unrepresentative data's impact on predictions for this grade level, or reframe the learning goal to align with the available evidence, such as 'Evaluate and analyze simple datasets to make predictions.'
                                                                                                                                                                                • minor: The skill 'evaluate-data-reliability' lists 'Concept of bias' as external knowledge. This concept is complex and is not explicitly supported by the provided evidence for this grade level. Introducing it may exceed the intended scope of 'detecting credibility'. Suggested correction: Reframe the external knowledge requirement to focus on more concrete concepts appropriate for the grade, such as 'why someone might say something' or 'the purpose of the information', rather than using the abstract term 'bias'.
                                                                                                                                                                                • minor: The pedagogical progression of the unit is logical, but its terminal skill, 'identify-unrepresentative-data-impact', lacks evidentiary support. This creates a sequence where foundational skills are justified by the sources, but the culminating objective is not. Suggested correction: Re-evaluate the unit's final objective to ensure it is an achievable and evidenced-based synthesis of the foundational skills. The current progression builds towards an unsupported goal.

                                                                                                                                                                                Makes simple predictions based on observed patterns in a given dataset.

                                                                                                                                                                                micro-predict-from-data-patterns-8094520e6b31c16c · data_and_ai · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                Why this learning matters: This skill directly addresses the 'prediction' aspect of the unit goal. Students need to experience making predictions from data before they can understand how flawed data impacts those predictions. It builds on pattern recognition, a core computational thinking skill.

                                                                                                                                                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: Pattern recognition and simple prediction are appropriate for 5th grade, aligning with the 'analysis' of information mentioned in the 'Segundo Ciclo' competencies.

                                                                                                                                                                                Evidence of mastery: Given a small, simple dataset (e.g., a sequence of colored blocks, a list of animal characteristics), students can identify a pattern and predict the next item or characteristic.

                                                                                                                                                                                Assessment task: Students are presented with a visual sequence of objects (e.g., 'red square, blue circle, red square, blue circle, ?') or a simple table of non-personal data (e.g., 'Day 1: sunny, Day 2: sunny, Day 3: cloudy, Day 4: sunny, Day 5: ?'). They must predict the next item and explain their reasoning.

                                                                                                                                                                                Access and participation: Use large, high-contrast visuals for patterns. Provide manipulatives for tactile learners. Allow verbal explanations instead of written ones. Offer pre-defined choices for predictions if open-ended is too challenging.

                                                                                                                                                                                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                Evidence limitations: The quote broadly mentions 'analysis' of information, which supports pattern recognition, but does not explicitly mention 'prediction' or 'datasets'. The connection to prediction is an extension inferred from the unit's learning goal and pedagogical progression.

                                                                                                                                                                                Safety: No specific safety notes are required for this unit as it involves no physical components or high-risk digital activities. Ensure appropriate digital citizenship guidelines are followed when accessing any online resources.

                                                                                                                                                                                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                • Student correctly identifies the underlying pattern.
                                                                                                                                                                                • Student makes a logical prediction based on the identified pattern.
                                                                                                                                                                                • Student can articulate the rule or pattern they used for prediction.
                                                                                                                                                                                Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                • Diagnostic: Present a simple pattern and ask students to complete it.
                                                                                                                                                                                • Practice: Introduce various types of patterns (visual, numerical, categorical) using small datasets.
                                                                                                                                                                                • Practice: Students work individually or in pairs to identify patterns and make predictions.
                                                                                                                                                                                • Feedback: Discuss different patterns found and the certainty of predictions.
                                                                                                                                                                                Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                • Every sequence has a simple, obvious pattern.
                                                                                                                                                                                • A single instance of something makes a pattern.
                                                                                                                                                                                Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                • Pattern cards or visual aids
                                                                                                                                                                                • Manipulatives (e.g., colored blocks, shapes)
                                                                                                                                                                                • Worksheets with simple datasets
                                                                                                                                                                                External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                • Basic observation skills
                                                                                                                                                                                • Understanding of sequences
                                                                                                                                                                                Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                • The provided source texts offer general competencies for data and information literacy but do not specify the depth or complexity of 'prediction' or 'unrepresentative examples' for 5th grade. The micro-skills are designed to be age-appropriate interpretations.

                                                                                                                                                                                • The 'T1' tier for some skills implies a teacher device, but the core concepts can be introduced with T0 alternatives, albeit with reduced efficiency or engagement.
                                                                                                                                                                                • The unit does not claim empirical validation or equivalence with international standards; it is a curricular proposal based on the provided texts and pedagogical principles.
                                                                                                                                                                                • The source statements about 'evaluation' (e.g., 'recolección, análisis, evaluación y presentación de información') provide a general basis for critical thinking about data, but they do not empirically validate specific instructional methods for teaching data bias or the impact of unrepresentative samples. The connection is a pedagogical interpretation.

                                                                                                                                                                                  Identifies how missing or unrepresentative data can affect a simple prediction.

                                                                                                                                                                                  micro-identify-unrepresentative-data-impact-eea6dc881df7a44a · data_and_ai · T1 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                  Why this learning matters: This is the core skill of the unit, directly addressing the learning goal. It integrates previous skills of data reliability and prediction. Understanding this impact is crucial for developing data literacy and critical thinking about how data influences outcomes.

                                                                                                                                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 5, students can begin to grasp cause-and-effect relationships in data, especially when presented with clear, simple examples. This aligns with the 'evaluation' of information.

                                                                                                                                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Given a simple prediction scenario and two datasets (one complete/representative, one missing/unrepresentative), students can explain how the flawed dataset might lead to a different, less accurate prediction.

                                                                                                                                                                                  Assessment task: Students are given a scenario where a 'machine' predicts a characteristic (e.g., 'most common color of cars in a city') based on a small, representative dataset. They are then shown a second dataset that is either incomplete (missing many examples) or unrepresentative (only shows cars from one street). Students must explain how the second dataset might lead to a wrong prediction.

                                                                                                                                                                                  Access and participation: Use highly visual examples and concrete scenarios. Provide sentence frames for explanations. Allow students to work in small groups to discuss and formulate answers. Offer simplified vocabulary.

                                                                                                                                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Teacher prints scenario cards and datasets or writes them on a whiteboard. Students discuss and record predictions and explanations on paper.

                                                                                                                                                                                  Evidence limitations: The provided text mentions 'recolección, análisis, evaluación' of information but does not explicitly detail the impact of 'missing or unrepresentative examples' on predictions. This specific application is derived from the unit's learning goal and general principles of data literacy, applying the general competency of 'evaluation' to a specific scenario (data bias). The source does not empirically validate the instruction of data bias.

                                                                                                                                                                                  Safety: No specific safety notes are required for this unit as it involves no physical components or high-risk digital activities. Ensure appropriate digital citizenship guidelines are followed when accessing any online resources.

                                                                                                                                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Student correctly identifies that the second dataset is flawed (missing or unrepresentative).
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Student explains how the flaw in the dataset could lead to a different prediction.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Student articulates that the prediction from the flawed data is likely less accurate or fair.
                                                                                                                                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Diagnostic: Ask students if they've ever made a wrong guess because they didn't have enough information.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Practice: Present scenarios with complete data leading to a prediction. Then, remove data or introduce biased data and ask for new predictions.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Practice: Discuss the differences in predictions and the reasons for them (e.g., 'not enough information', 'only saw one type').
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Feedback: Emphasize the importance of diverse and sufficient data for accurate predictions.
                                                                                                                                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Any data is good data for prediction.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • More data always means better prediction, regardless of quality.
                                                                                                                                                                                  Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Teacher device with projector (optional)
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Scenario cards with datasets (complete and flawed)
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Worksheets for recording predictions and explanations
                                                                                                                                                                                  External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                  • Basic understanding of cause and effect
                                                                                                                                                                                  Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                  • The provided source texts offer general competencies for data and information literacy but do not specify the depth or complexity of 'prediction' or 'unrepresentative examples' for 5th grade. The micro-skills are designed to be age-appropriate interpretations.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • The unit does not delve into complex statistical concepts or machine learning algorithms, focusing instead on observable patterns and logical reasoning.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • The 'T1' tier for some skills implies a teacher device, but the core concepts can be introduced with T0 alternatives, albeit with reduced efficiency or engagement.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • The unit does not claim empirical validation or equivalence with international standards; it is a curricular proposal based on the provided texts and pedagogical principles.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • The source statements about 'evaluation' (e.g., 'recolección, análisis, evaluación y presentación de información') provide a general basis for critical thinking about data, but they do not empirically validate specific instructional methods for teaching data bias or the impact of unrepresentative samples. The connection is a pedagogical interpretation.
                                                                                                                                                                                  • The unit focuses on concrete, non-personal data examples to ensure age-appropriateness and avoid privacy concerns.
                                                                                                                                                                                  Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                    Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                    • major: The learning goal 'Test how missing or unrepresentative examples affect a simple classifier or prediction' is not directly supported by the provided evidence. The sources mention general data analysis and evaluation but do not specify concepts like 'unrepresentative examples', 'classifiers', or the impact of data quality on predictions. Suggested correction: Either provide evidence that explicitly supports teaching about unrepresentative data's impact on predictions for this grade level, or reframe the learning goal to align with the available evidence, such as 'Evaluate and analyze simple datasets to make predictions.'
