                                                                                                                                                                                    Grade 6

                                                                                                                                                                                    Proposed core: 22.67 hours. Scenario capacity: 160 hours. Unallocated capacity: 137.33 hours. These are planning assumptions, not official hours.

                                                                                                                                                                                    Comparing algorithms

                                                                                                                                                                                    Justify a design choice with evidence; avoid formal asymptotic complexity and unsupported far-transfer claims.

                                                                                                                                                                                    Trace the execution of a simple algorithm step-by-step, identifying the sequence of operations.

                                                                                                                                                                                    micro-trace-algorithm-execution-e411d1aa9fc13bf3 · computational_thinking · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                    Why this learning matters: Before comparing algorithms, students must be able to understand how a single algorithm functions. Tracing helps them internalize the concept of sequential, conditional, and repetitive instructions, which are fundamental building blocks. This skill is crucial for later identifying inefficiencies or errors.

                                                                                                                                                                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 6, students are ready to move beyond simply writing algorithms to understanding their operational mechanics. Tracing provides a concrete way to observe the 'how' of an algorithm, preparing them for analysis and comparison.

                                                                                                                                                                                    Evidence of mastery: Students can accurately follow and record the state changes of variables or outputs for a given algorithm and input.

                                                                                                                                                                                    Assessment task: Given a simple algorithm (e.g., finding the largest number in a small list, or a simple sorting algorithm like bubble sort on 3-4 elements) and a specific input, students will record the value of key variables at each step of execution on a worksheet.

                                                                                                                                                                                    Access and participation: Provide algorithms in both visual (flowchart-like) and textual formats. Allow students to use manipulatives (e.g., index cards for variables) to represent state changes. Offer pre-filled templates for recording traces.

                                                                                                                                                                                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                    Evidence limitations: The cited evidence supports understanding how programs execute and using sequence/selection/repetition, but does not explicitly detail 'tracing' as a pedagogical method or assessment. Its effectiveness for this specific skill is inferred. The Argentinian curriculum mentions 'debugging sequences of programming' (argentina p.20 ¶paragraph-p113-695922b327fa7b1d), which implies understanding execution flow, but does not specify tracing as a method.

                                                                                                                                                                                    Safety: No specific safety notes are required for this unit as it involves conceptual work with algorithms, primarily using paper-based activities or simple digital tools for tracing and comparison. No electrical components or physical construction are involved.

                                                                                                                                                                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                    Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Correctly identifies the initial state of all variables.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Accurately records variable changes after each instruction.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Correctly determines the final output of the algorithm.
                                                                                                                                                                                    Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Diagnosis: Present a very simple algorithm (e.g., 'add 3, then multiply by 2') and ask students to predict the output for a given input. Discuss their reasoning.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Practice: Model tracing an algorithm step-by-step on the board, verbalizing each decision and variable change. Provide several algorithms of increasing complexity for students to trace individually or in pairs, using paper and pencil or whiteboards.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Feedback: Review student traces, highlighting common errors like skipping steps, misinterpreting conditions, or incorrect arithmetic. Provide immediate corrective feedback and opportunities for re-tracing.
                                                                                                                                                                                    Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Algorithms execute instantly without intermediate steps.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • A variable's value changes automatically without an explicit assignment.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Conditions are evaluated only once, not repeatedly in a loop.
                                                                                                                                                                                    Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Worksheets with algorithms and tracing tables
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Pencils/pens
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Whiteboard/projector
                                                                                                                                                                                    External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Basic arithmetic operations
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Understanding of conditional statements (if/else)
                                                                                                                                                                                    • Understanding of loops (repeat/for/while)
                                                                                                                                                                                    Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                    • The unit deliberately avoids formal asymptotic complexity notation (e.g., Big O notation) as it is beyond the scope of Grade 6. Comparisons are based on concrete operation counts for specific inputs, not theoretical worst-case scenarios.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • The 'utility' of algorithms is narrowed to correctness and operation count. Other factors like memory usage, readability, or ease of implementation are not explicitly covered.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • The provided evidence, while supportive of the general concepts, does not prescribe specific pedagogical methods like 'counting operations' for comparison, which is an interpretation for this grade level. The learning goal 'Compare two algorithms for the same task using correctness, clear tests and counted operations' is an adaptation to make the abstract concept of algorithm comparison concrete and measurable for primary students.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • The unit does not involve actual programming or execution on digital devices, focusing instead on the abstract logic of algorithms. While beneficial for conceptual understanding, it limits practical application experience. The Argentinian curriculum (argentina p.20 ¶paragraph-p113-695922b327fa7b1d, point 8) emphasizes 'design, construction and debugging of programming sequences and robotics to develop projects', which implies a more hands-on, device-based approach. This unit focuses on the analytical aspect of algorithms, which is a foundational skill for such practical application but does not directly fulfill the 'construction' or 'robotics' components.
                                                                                                                                                                                    • The Argentinian curriculum evidence focuses on creating programs and robotics projects to solve concrete problems, implying a more applied, project-based approach rather than the abstract, paper-based analysis proposed in this unit. The activities are a pedagogical interpretation to build foundational concepts not explicitly detailed in this source, but are necessary for the analytical comparison of algorithms.
                                                                                                                                                                                    Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                      Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                      • minor: The Argentinian curriculum source (argentina p.20, point 8) emphasizes a hands-on approach involving 'construction and debugging of programming and robotics sequences to develop projects'. The proposed unit is entirely abstract and paper-based, creating a significant mismatch with this source's pedagogical approach. Suggested correction: Acknowledge this as a limitation or add an optional extension activity that connects the abstract analysis to a simple block-based programming environment to better align with the spirit of the Argentinian curriculum.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • minor: The micro-skill 'trace-algorithm-execution' lists 'Understanding of conditional statements (if/else)' and 'Understanding of loops (repeat/for/while)' as external knowledge but has no formal prerequisite micro-skills. These concepts are non-trivial and foundational for tracing. Suggested correction: Either create explicit prerequisite micro-skills for understanding sequence, selection, and repetition, or ensure that the 'Diagnosis' step in the teaching sequence for 'trace-algorithm-execution' is robust enough to identify and remediate gaps in this knowledge before proceeding.

                                                                                                                                                                                      Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

                                                                                                                                                                                      Sources: source-eng-computing-570e16e9afcbc1c7 (page HTML, Key stage 1, paragraph-p90-7d8109e0e4d36796, original characters 98–164)

                                                                                                                                                                                      Identify and count the significant operations within an algorithm for a given input.

                                                                                                                                                                                      micro-identify-algorithm-operations-c23e31069012073f · computational_thinking · T0 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                      Why this learning matters: Counting operations is a foundational step towards understanding algorithm efficiency without delving into formal complexity theory. It provides a tangible metric for comparison, allowing students to see why one algorithm might be 'better' than another for certain tasks or inputs. This builds on the tracing skill by adding a quantitative analysis layer.

                                                                                                                                                                                      Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 6 students are capable of systematic counting and basic quantitative comparison. This skill introduces the idea of 'cost' in computation, which is a key aspect of computational thinking and algorithm evaluation.

                                                                                                                                                                                      Evidence of mastery: Students can correctly identify a predefined 'significant operation' (e.g., comparison, swap, arithmetic calculation) within an algorithm and accurately count its occurrences during execution for a specific input.

                                                                                                                                                                                      Assessment task: Given two simple algorithms (e.g., two different methods to find a specific item in a list, or two sorting methods for a small list) and a specific input, students will trace each algorithm and count the number of 'comparison' operations (or another clearly defined operation) performed by each.

                                                                                                                                                                                      Access and participation: Use color-coding or distinct symbols to mark operations being counted during tracing. Provide checklists for operations. Allow verbal counting alongside written tracing. Use visual aids to represent data structures (e.g., physical cards for lists).

                                                                                                                                                                                      T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                      Evidence limitations: While the evidence supports explaining how algorithms work and detecting errors, it does not explicitly mention 'counting operations' as the method for comparison. This specific technique is a pedagogical interpretation to meet the learning goal at this grade level. The Argentinian curriculum mentions 'debugging sequences of programming' (argentina p.20 ¶paragraph-p113-695922b327fa7b1d), which implies understanding execution, but not necessarily quantitative comparison of operations.

                                                                                                                                                                                      Safety: No specific safety notes are required for this unit as it involves conceptual work with algorithms, primarily using paper-based activities or simple digital tools for tracing and comparison. No electrical components or physical construction are involved.

                                                                                                                                                                                      Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                      Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Correctly defines what constitutes a 'significant operation' for the task.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Accurately traces the algorithm's execution.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Correctly counts the occurrences of the specified operation for each algorithm.
                                                                                                                                                                                      Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Diagnosis: Ask students to trace an algorithm and then informally describe which parts 'take the most work'. Discuss their interpretations.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Practice: Introduce the concept of a 'significant operation' (e.g., a comparison, a swap). Model tracing an algorithm while explicitly counting these operations. Provide practice problems where students trace and count operations for various algorithms and inputs.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Feedback: Review student counts and traces. Discuss discrepancies and refine their understanding of what to count and why. Emphasize that different inputs might lead to different operation counts.
                                                                                                                                                                                      Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                      • All steps in an algorithm take the same amount of 'work'.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • The number of operations is always the same regardless of the input.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Counting operations is the only way to compare algorithms.
                                                                                                                                                                                      Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Worksheets with algorithms and tracing/counting tables
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Pencils/pens
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Colored markers (optional)
                                                                                                                                                                                      External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                      • Basic understanding of data structures like lists/arrays
                                                                                                                                                                                      Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                      • The unit deliberately avoids formal asymptotic complexity notation (e.g., Big O notation) as it is beyond the scope of Grade 6. Comparisons are based on concrete operation counts for specific inputs, not theoretical worst-case scenarios.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • The 'utility' of algorithms is narrowed to correctness and operation count. Other factors like memory usage, readability, or ease of implementation are not explicitly covered.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • The provided evidence, while supportive of the general concepts, does not prescribe specific pedagogical methods like 'counting operations' for comparison, which is an interpretation for this grade level. The learning goal 'Compare two algorithms for the same task using correctness, clear tests and counted operations' is an adaptation to make the abstract concept of algorithm comparison concrete and measurable for primary students.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • The unit does not involve actual programming or execution on digital devices, focusing instead on the abstract logic of algorithms. While beneficial for conceptual understanding, it limits practical application experience. The Argentinian curriculum (argentina p.20 ¶paragraph-p113-695922b327fa7b1d, point 8) emphasizes 'design, construction and debugging of programming sequences and robotics to develop projects', which implies a more hands-on, device-based approach. This unit focuses on the analytical aspect of algorithms, which is a foundational skill for such practical application but does not directly fulfill the 'construction' or 'robotics' components.
                                                                                                                                                                                      • The Argentinian curriculum evidence focuses on creating programs and robotics projects to solve concrete problems, implying a more applied, project-based approach rather than the abstract, paper-based analysis proposed in this unit. The activities are a pedagogical interpretation to build foundational concepts not explicitly detailed in this source, but are necessary for the analytical comparison of algorithms.
                                                                                                                                                                                      Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                        Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                        • minor: The Argentinian curriculum source (argentina p.20, point 8) emphasizes a hands-on approach involving 'construction and debugging of programming and robotics sequences to develop projects'. The proposed unit is entirely abstract and paper-based, creating a significant mismatch with this source's pedagogical approach. Suggested correction: Acknowledge this as a limitation or add an optional extension activity that connects the abstract analysis to a simple block-based programming environment to better align with the spirit of the Argentinian curriculum.

                                                                                                                                                                                        Internal prerequisite IDs: micro-trace-algorithm-execution-e411d1aa9fc13bf3

                                                                                                                                                                                        Sources: source-eng-computing-570e16e9afcbc1c7 (page HTML, Key stage 2, paragraph-p112-55b0739086dd3e68, original characters 0–59)

                                                                                                                                                                                        Compare two algorithms for the same task based on their correctness and the number of operations for specific inputs.

                                                                                                                                                                                        micro-compare-algorithm-efficiency-408e6a2e34237f95 · computational_thinking · T0 · 90 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                        Why this learning matters: This micro-skill integrates tracing and counting into a higher-order thinking task: comparison and justification. It moves students from 'how it works' to 'which is better and why', directly addressing the unit's learning goal. Understanding correctness is paramount, as an incorrect but 'fast' algorithm is useless.

                                                                                                                                                                                        Proposed grade placement rationale: At Grade 6, students are developing their ability to make reasoned arguments and justify choices. Comparing algorithms based on concrete metrics like operation counts and correctness provides an excellent context for this development in computational thinking.

                                                                                                                                                                                        Evidence of mastery: Given two algorithms for the same problem and a set of test cases, students can determine which algorithm is more efficient (performs fewer operations) for each test case and justify their choice based on the traced operations and correctness.

                                                                                                                                                                                        Assessment task: Students are given two algorithms designed to solve the same problem (e.g., finding the smallest number in a list, or a simple search). For three different input lists (e.g., small, medium, already sorted), they must: 1) determine if each algorithm produces the correct output, 2) count a specified operation for each algorithm and input, and 3) write a short paragraph explaining which algorithm they would choose for each input and why, referencing their counts and correctness.

                                                                                                                                                                                        Access and participation: Provide sentence starters or a structured template for the justification paragraph. Use visual aids to represent the comparison data (e.g., bar graphs of operation counts). Allow peer discussion before individual writing. Ensure algorithms are clearly presented and easy to trace.

                                                                                                                                                                                        T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                        Evidence limitations: The evidence supports explaining how algorithms work and detecting errors, but the specific method of 'counting operations' as the primary metric for comparison at this grade level is a pedagogical choice, not explicitly stated. The concept of 'utility' is broad and could encompass other factors not covered here. The Argentinian curriculum mentions 'debugging sequences of programming' (argentina p.20 ¶paragraph-p113-695922b327fa7b1d), which supports error detection, but does not explicitly cover quantitative comparison of algorithms.

                                                                                                                                                                                        Safety: No specific safety notes are required for this unit as it involves conceptual work with algorithms, primarily using paper-based activities or simple digital tools for tracing and comparison. No electrical components or physical construction are involved.

                                                                                                                                                                                        Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                                                                                                                        Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Accurately determines the correctness of each algorithm for the given test cases.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Correctly counts the specified operations for both algorithms across all inputs.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Provides a clear justification for algorithm choice, linking it to operation counts and correctness for each input scenario.
                                                                                                                                                                                        Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Diagnosis: Present two algorithms and ask students which they think is 'better' and why, without formal counting. Discuss their intuitive reasoning.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Practice: Introduce the concept of 'test cases' and 'correctness'. Model comparing two algorithms for a specific input, tracing both, counting operations, and then discussing which is 'better' based on the results. Provide structured comparison tasks where students apply their tracing and counting skills to multiple algorithms and inputs.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Feedback: Facilitate a class discussion on their comparisons. Challenge students to justify their choices with evidence (their counts). Address scenarios where one algorithm might be better for certain inputs and another for different ones. Discuss the importance of correctness before efficiency.
                                                                                                                                                                                        Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                        • The algorithm with fewer lines of code is always more efficient.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • An algorithm that works for one input will work for all inputs.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Efficiency is always more important than correctness.
                                                                                                                                                                                        Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Worksheets with pairs of algorithms and comparison tables
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Pencils/pens
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Graph paper (optional for visualization)
                                                                                                                                                                                        External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Basic understanding of problem-solving strategies
                                                                                                                                                                                        • Ability to articulate reasoning in written form
                                                                                                                                                                                        Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                        • The unit deliberately avoids formal asymptotic complexity notation (e.g., Big O notation) as it is beyond the scope of Grade 6. Comparisons are based on concrete operation counts for specific inputs, not theoretical worst-case scenarios.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • The 'utility' of algorithms is narrowed to correctness and operation count. Other factors like memory usage, readability, or ease of implementation are not explicitly covered.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • The provided evidence, while supportive of the general concepts, does not prescribe specific pedagogical methods like 'counting operations' for comparison, which is an interpretation for this grade level. The learning goal 'Compare two algorithms for the same task using correctness, clear tests and counted operations' is an adaptation to make the abstract concept of algorithm comparison concrete and measurable for primary students.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • The unit does not involve actual programming or execution on digital devices, focusing instead on the abstract logic of algorithms. While beneficial for conceptual understanding, it limits practical application experience. The Argentinian curriculum (argentina p.20 ¶paragraph-p113-695922b327fa7b1d, point 8) emphasizes 'design, construction and debugging of programming sequences and robotics to develop projects', which implies a more hands-on, device-based approach. This unit focuses on the analytical aspect of algorithms, which is a foundational skill for such practical application but does not directly fulfill the 'construction' or 'robotics' components.
                                                                                                                                                                                        • The Argentinian curriculum evidence focuses on creating programs and robotics projects to solve concrete problems, implying a more applied, project-based approach rather than the abstract, paper-based analysis proposed in this unit. The activities are a pedagogical interpretation to build foundational concepts not explicitly detailed in this source, but are necessary for the analytical comparison of algorithms.
                                                                                                                                                                                        Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                          Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                          • major: The core learning goal 'Compare two algorithms for the same task using... counted operations' and the associated micro-skills 'identify-algorithm-operations' and 'compare-algorithm-efficiency' are not directly supported by the provided evidence. The proposal correctly notes this as a limitation, stating that counting operations is a pedagogical interpretation. Suggested correction: Either provide evidence that supports quantitative comparison of algorithms via operation counting for this grade level, or reframe the learning goal to align with the existing evidence, which focuses on explaining how algorithms work and detecting errors (e.g., 'Explain how different algorithms for the same task work and identify potential errors').
                                                                                                                                                                                          • minor: The Argentinian curriculum source (argentina p.20, point 8) emphasizes a hands-on approach involving 'construction and debugging of programming and robotics sequences to develop projects'. The proposed unit is entirely abstract and paper-based, creating a significant mismatch with this source's pedagogical approach. Suggested correction: Acknowledge this as a limitation or add an optional extension activity that connects the abstract analysis to a simple block-based programming environment to better align with the spirit of the Argentinian curriculum.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • minor: The assessment task for 'compare-algorithm-efficiency' requires students to test algorithms against three different inputs. However, the success criteria do not explicitly require the student to recognize or articulate that an algorithm's efficiency can change with different inputs, which is a key insight from this task. Suggested correction: Add a success criterion such as: 'Articulates how the choice of 'better' algorithm can depend on the specific input, using data from the tests.'
                                                                                                                                                                                          • minor: The estimated time for 'compare-algorithm-efficiency' is 90 minutes. This may be insufficient for Grade 6 students to trace two algorithms across three different inputs each (six traces total), count operations, determine correctness, and write a justification paragraph. Suggested correction: Consider reducing the number of required inputs for the assessment task (e.g., to two contrasting inputs) or increasing the estimated time to ensure students can complete the task without being rushed.

                                                                                                                                                                                          Evaluate the appropriateness of privacy policy statements regarding personal data use in digital services.

                                                                                                                                                                                          micro-evaluate-privacy-policies-0e1c8205c99c385b · digital_citizenship · T1 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                          Why this learning matters: At Grade 6, students move beyond simply identifying privacy policies to critically assessing their content. This skill is crucial for making informed decisions about sharing personal data, aligning with the unit's goal of justifying decisions based on evidence. Understanding the 'why' behind policies fosters a deeper sense of digital responsibility.

                                                                                                                                                                                          Proposed grade placement rationale: This micro-skill aligns with the DigComp framework's Level 6 for 'Protecting Personal Data and Privacy,' which involves evaluating privacy policy statements. This level is appropriate for 6th graders, building on earlier grades' ability to identify and explain policies.

                                                                                                                                                                                          Evidence of mastery: Students will critically analyze a given privacy policy statement and articulate its strengths and weaknesses regarding personal data protection, proposing improvements or identifying potential risks.

                                                                                                                                                                                          Assessment task: Given a simplified privacy policy statement from a fictional social media app, students will highlight clauses that protect user data effectively (green) and clauses that are vague or potentially risky (red). They will then write a short paragraph explaining one identified risk and suggesting an improvement.

                                                                                                                                                                                          Access and participation: Provide policies in simplified language and visual aids (e.g., icons for data types). Allow students to work in pairs or small groups to discuss and interpret clauses. Offer sentence starters for written explanations.

                                                                                                                                                                                          T0 alternative and limits: Without digital devices, students can analyze printed privacy policies. However, the direct interaction with digital service interfaces and their policy links is lost, reducing the authenticity of the learning experience.

                                                                                                                                                                                          Evidence limitations: The cited evidence describes the skill but does not provide specific pedagogical methods or empirical data on its efficacy in a classroom setting for this age group. The 'appropriateness' is subjective and requires careful teacher guidance.

                                                                                                                                                                                          Safety: All online scenarios used for teaching and assessment must be age-appropriate and carefully vetted to avoid causing distress. Emphasize that students should never share real personal information or engage in actual risky online behavior during activities. Discussions should be facilitated in a supportive and non-judgmental environment.

                                                                                                                                                                                          Teacher preparation level (0–3): 3

                                                                                                                                                                                          Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Identifies at least two protective clauses.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Identifies at least two vague/risky clauses.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Provides a clear explanation for one identified risk.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Proposes a relevant and actionable improvement.
                                                                                                                                                                                          Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Diagnosis: Ask students to define 'privacy policy' and share any experiences with them. Discuss what they think a good policy should include.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Practice: Introduce simplified examples of privacy policy clauses. Guide students through analyzing them, focusing on what data is collected, how it's used, and who it's shared with. Use a 'traffic light' system (green for good, red for risky).
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Feedback: Review student analyses, providing specific feedback on their interpretations and suggestions. Facilitate a class discussion on common pitfalls and best practices in privacy policies.
                                                                                                                                                                                          Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Believing that if a service has a privacy policy, it automatically means their data is safe.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Assuming that all data collected is necessary for the service to function.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Not understanding that 'sharing with third parties' can mean advertising or data brokers.
                                                                                                                                                                                          Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Simplified privacy policy examples (printed or digital)
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Highlighters or digital annotation tools
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Worksheets for analysis
                                                                                                                                                                                          External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Basic understanding of personal data types (name, age, location, photos)
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Basic understanding of online services (social media, games)
                                                                                                                                                                                          • Understanding of simple privacy policy statements
                                                                                                                                                                                          Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                          • The effectiveness of these micro-skills is highly dependent on the quality of the scenarios and examples provided, which must be relevant and relatable to students' experiences.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • The 'discussion' and 'evaluation' aspects require strong facilitation skills from the teacher to ensure all students participate meaningfully and receive constructive feedback.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • The rapid evolution of digital technologies means that specific examples and reporting mechanisms may quickly become outdated, requiring continuous curriculum updates.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • The ability to 'justify' decisions, as per the unit goal, is a complex cognitive skill that requires consistent practice across multiple contexts, not just within this unit.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • The unit's learning goal originally included 'AI-use decision', but no micro-skills were developed to address AI-specific concepts due to the scope of the provided source materials and the constraint against inventing new content. Therefore, the learning goal has been adjusted to reflect the covered skills.
                                                                                                                                                                                          • The DigComp framework, while providing levels, does not explicitly define grade-level equivalencies for primary education. The placement of DigComp Level 4 and 6 skills for Grade 6 is an interpretation based on the progression of complexity and the general cognitive development expected at this age, rather than a direct mapping. This is not an inference of country grade equivalence, but an internal interpretation of the DigComp framework's progression for primary education.
                                                                                                                                                                                          Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                            Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                            • major: The unit's learning goal is to 'Justify a privacy, information-sharing... decision'. However, the micro-skills focus on identifying concerns, discussing implications, and evaluating policies. There is no micro-skill that explicitly requires students to synthesize these abilities into a final, justified decision as the goal states. Suggested correction: Add a capstone micro-skill where students are given a complex scenario and must write or present a justification for a specific course of action, drawing upon their ability to evaluate policies and weigh the implications of sharing information.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • minor: The pedagogical sequence of the unit is illogical. The prerequisite for 'evaluate-privacy-policies' is 'discuss-data-sharing-implications', which in turn has 'identify-and-report-concerns' as its prerequisite. This places the most foundational safety skill ('identify-and-report-concerns') last in the learning progression. Suggested correction: Reorder the micro-skills to follow a logical progression: 1) 'identify-and-report-concerns', 2) 'discuss-data-sharing-implications', 3) 'evaluate-privacy-policies'. This ensures students first learn to recognize and report immediate dangers before moving to more nuanced discussions and policy analysis.

                                                                                                                                                                                            Internal prerequisite IDs: micro-discuss-data-sharing-implications-dc82104b41fbc09c

                                                                                                                                                                                            Sources: source-digcomp-d371a960318a1a45 (page 42, Safety, paragraph-p213-ebc8533b000d9c93, original characters 1622–1679)

                                                                                                                                                                                            Discuss ways to use and share personally identifiable information while protecting oneself and others from damages.

                                                                                                                                                                                            micro-discuss-data-sharing-implications-dc82104b41fbc09c · digital_citizenship · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                            Why this learning matters: This micro-skill encourages students to articulate their understanding of data sharing risks and protective strategies. Discussion fosters critical thinking and empathy, as students consider the impact on themselves and others. It moves beyond passive reception of information to active engagement with ethical dilemmas.

                                                                                                                                                                                            Proposed grade placement rationale: This skill aligns with DigComp Level 4 for 'Protecting Personal Data and Privacy,' which involves discussing ways to use and share information safely. While the unit targets Grade 6 (DigComp Level 6), this Level 4 skill serves as a foundational step, allowing students to articulate and explore the nuances of data sharing before moving to more complex evaluation and justification tasks.

                                                                                                                                                                                            Evidence of mastery: Students will participate in a structured debate or group discussion, presenting arguments for and against sharing specific types of personal information in various scenarios, demonstrating an understanding of potential risks and protective measures.

                                                                                                                                                                                            Assessment task: In small groups, students will be given a scenario (e.g., 'Should you share your location with friends on a gaming app?'). They must discuss the pros and cons, identify potential damages, and propose protective measures. Each group will present their conclusions to the class.

                                                                                                                                                                                            Access and participation: Provide visual scenario cards. Allow students to use drawing or role-playing to express their ideas if verbal communication is challenging. Ensure group compositions are balanced to support all learners.

                                                                                                                                                                                            T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                            Evidence limitations: The evidence indicates the ability to 'discuss' but does not specify the depth or complexity of discussion expected, nor does it provide guidance on how to assess the quality of such discussions. The effectiveness depends heavily on teacher facilitation.

                                                                                                                                                                                            Safety: All online scenarios used for teaching and assessment must be age-appropriate and carefully vetted to avoid causing distress. Emphasize that students should never share real personal information or engage in actual risky online behavior during activities. Discussions should be facilitated in a supportive and non-judgmental environment.

                                                                                                                                                                                            Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                            Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Identifies at least two potential benefits of sharing.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Identifies at least two potential damages/risks.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Proposes at least one protective measure.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Participates actively and respectfully in group discussion.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Group presentation clearly articulates the identified pros, cons, and protective measures.
                                                                                                                                                                                            Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Diagnosis: Present a simple scenario and ask students to individually write down one reason to share and one reason not to share personal information.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Practice: Introduce various scenarios involving sharing personal information (e.g., photos, location, contact details). Guide students in small groups to discuss the implications, focusing on 'who, what, why, when, how' of sharing.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Feedback: Facilitate a class debrief where groups share their findings. Provide feedback on the depth of their analysis and the practicality of their proposed protective measures. Emphasize the importance of considering 'others' in their decisions.
                                                                                                                                                                                            Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Believing that if friends ask for information, it's always safe to share.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Underestimating the permanence of shared information online.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Not realizing that 'damages' can extend beyond direct harm (e.g., identity theft, cyberbullying).
                                                                                                                                                                                            Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Scenario cards (printed)
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Whiteboard or large paper for group notes
                                                                                                                                                                                            External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Basic understanding of online communication and social platforms
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Concepts of privacy and personal boundaries
                                                                                                                                                                                            • Ability to identify personally identifiable information
                                                                                                                                                                                            Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                            • The effectiveness of these micro-skills is highly dependent on the quality of the scenarios and examples provided, which must be relevant and relatable to students' experiences.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • The 'discussion' and 'evaluation' aspects require strong facilitation skills from the teacher to ensure all students participate meaningfully and receive constructive feedback.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • The rapid evolution of digital technologies means that specific examples and reporting mechanisms may quickly become outdated, requiring continuous curriculum updates.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • The ability to 'justify' decisions, as per the unit goal, is a complex cognitive skill that requires consistent practice across multiple contexts, not just within this unit.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • The unit's learning goal originally included 'AI-use decision', but no micro-skills were developed to address AI-specific concepts due to the scope of the provided source materials and the constraint against inventing new content. Therefore, the learning goal has been adjusted to reflect the covered skills.
                                                                                                                                                                                            • The DigComp framework, while providing levels, does not explicitly define grade-level equivalencies for primary education. The placement of DigComp Level 4 and 6 skills for Grade 6 is an interpretation based on the progression of complexity and the general cognitive development expected at this age, rather than a direct mapping. This is not an inference of country grade equivalence, but an internal interpretation of the DigComp framework's progression for primary education.
