This ensures students first learn to recognize and report immediate dangers before moving to more nuanced discussions and policy analysis.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • minor: The success criteria for 'discuss-data-sharing-implications' includes 'Participates actively and respectfully in group discussion.' This is a behavioral or dispositional criterion, not a direct measure of skill mastery related to the knowledge object. Suggested correction: Remove 'Participates actively and respectfully in group discussion' from the success criteria. While important for classroom management, it does not assess whether the student can analyze the implications of data sharing. Focus assessment on the quality of the arguments and evidence presented.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • minor: The 'discuss-data-sharing-implications' skill is mapped to DigComp Level 4, while 'evaluate-privacy-policies' is mapped to Level 6. The grade rationale justifies using a Level 4 skill as foundational for Grade 6, but this creates an internal inconsistency where skills at different proficiency levels are taught within the same grade-level unit without a clear developmental bridge. Suggested correction: Either find evidence for a Grade 6-appropriate version of the 'discuss' skill (e.g., from DigComp Level 5 or 6) or explicitly frame the unit as bridging from an intermediate to an advanced understanding, clarifying this in the unit rationale.

                                                                                                                                                                                              Internal prerequisite IDs: micro-identify-and-report-concerns-6d3ca06e693518d9

                                                                                                                                                                                              Sources: source-digcomp-d371a960318a1a45 (page 42, Safety, paragraph-p212-7bc703f369a6eab0, original characters 3113–3178)

                                                                                                                                                                                              Identify inappropriate content, contact, and conduct, and know how to report concerns.

                                                                                                                                                                                              micro-identify-and-report-concerns-6d3ca06e693518d9 · digital_citizenship · T0 · 50 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                              Why this learning matters: This micro-skill is foundational for online safety. By Grade 6, students are likely to encounter a wider range of online interactions. Equipping them with the ability to recognize and report concerns empowers them to protect themselves and seek help, fostering a sense of agency in their digital lives.

                                                                                                                                                                                              Proposed grade placement rationale: This skill is explicitly mentioned in the England Computing programmes of study for primary education, which is suitable for 6th graders. It builds on earlier recognition of basic online dangers by adding the crucial 'how to report' component.

                                                                                                                                                                                              Evidence of mastery: Students will correctly categorize various simulated online situations as appropriate or inappropriate (content, contact, conduct) and articulate the correct steps to report each concern to a trusted adult or platform.

                                                                                                                                                                                              Assessment task: Students will be presented with 3-4 short written scenarios describing online interactions (e.g., a strange message, an upsetting image, someone asking for personal details). For each, they must state if it's inappropriate (and why) and describe who they would tell and what information they would provide.

                                                                                                                                                                                              Access and participation: Use clear, simple language for scenarios. Provide visual cues or icons for different types of inappropriateness. Offer a list of trusted adults or reporting options to choose from. Allow verbal responses if writing is a barrier.

                                                                                                                                                                                              T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                              Evidence limitations: The evidence states the need to 'identify where to go for help and support' but does not detail the specific types of inappropriate content/contact/conduct or the complexity of reporting mechanisms expected for this age group. The effectiveness relies on realistic scenario design.

                                                                                                                                                                                              Safety: All online scenarios used for teaching and assessment must be age-appropriate and carefully vetted to avoid causing distress. Emphasize that students should never share real personal information or engage in actual risky online behavior during activities. Discussions should be facilitated in a supportive and non-judgmental environment.

                                                                                                                                                                                              Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                              Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Correctly identifies inappropriate content/contact/conduct in each scenario.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Clearly explains why the situation is inappropriate.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Identifies a trusted adult or appropriate reporting mechanism.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Describes relevant information to provide when reporting.
                                                                                                                                                                                              Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Diagnosis: Ask students to brainstorm what 'inappropriate' means online. Discuss who they would talk to if something made them uncomfortable online.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Practice: Present various age-appropriate scenarios (text-based or visual). Guide students to identify the type of inappropriateness (content, contact, conduct) and discuss reporting pathways (e.g., parent, teacher, platform report button). Role-play reporting conversations.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Feedback: Review student responses to scenarios, correcting misunderstandings about what constitutes inappropriate behavior and reinforcing the importance of immediate reporting to trusted adults. Emphasize that reporting is not 'telling tales' but seeking help.
                                                                                                                                                                                              Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Believing they can handle all online problems themselves.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Fearing they will get in trouble for reporting something they saw or received.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Not knowing the difference between blocking someone and reporting them.
                                                                                                                                                                                              Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Scenario cards (printed)
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Worksheets for responses
                                                                                                                                                                                              External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Basic understanding of online communication platforms
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Concept of trusted adults
                                                                                                                                                                                              • Understanding of basic online safety rules
                                                                                                                                                                                              Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                              • The effectiveness of these micro-skills is highly dependent on the quality of the scenarios and examples provided, which must be relevant and relatable to students' experiences.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • The 'discussion' and 'evaluation' aspects require strong facilitation skills from the teacher to ensure all students participate meaningfully and receive constructive feedback.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • The rapid evolution of digital technologies means that specific examples and reporting mechanisms may quickly become outdated, requiring continuous curriculum updates.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • The ability to 'justify' decisions, as per the unit goal, is a complex cognitive skill that requires consistent practice across multiple contexts, not just within this unit.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • The unit's learning goal originally included 'AI-use decision', but no micro-skills were developed to address AI-specific concepts due to the scope of the provided source materials and the constraint against inventing new content. Therefore, the learning goal has been adjusted to reflect the covered skills.
                                                                                                                                                                                              • The DigComp framework, while providing levels, does not explicitly define grade-level equivalencies for primary education. The placement of DigComp Level 4 and 6 skills for Grade 6 is an interpretation based on the progression of complexity and the general cognitive development expected at this age, rather than a direct mapping. This is not an inference of country grade equivalence, but an internal interpretation of the DigComp framework's progression for primary education.
                                                                                                                                                                                              Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                                Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                                • major: The unit's learning goal is to 'Justify a privacy, information-sharing... decision'. However, the micro-skills focus on identifying concerns, discussing implications, and evaluating policies. There is no micro-skill that explicitly requires students to synthesize these abilities into a final, justified decision as the goal states. Suggested correction: Add a capstone micro-skill where students are given a complex scenario and must write or present a justification for a specific course of action, drawing upon their ability to evaluate policies and weigh the implications of sharing information.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • minor: The pedagogical sequence of the unit is illogical. The prerequisite for 'evaluate-privacy-policies' is 'discuss-data-sharing-implications', which in turn has 'identify-and-report-concerns' as its prerequisite. This places the most foundational safety skill ('identify-and-report-concerns') last in the learning progression. Suggested correction: Reorder the micro-skills to follow a logical progression: 1) 'identify-and-report-concerns', 2) 'discuss-data-sharing-implications', 3) 'evaluate-privacy-policies'. This ensures students first learn to recognize and report immediate dangers before moving to more nuanced discussions and policy analysis.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • minor: The evidence quote for 'identify-and-report-concerns' from the England Computing curriculum supports identifying where to go for help, but it does not explicitly mention identifying 'inappropriate content, contact, and conduct' as stated in the skill. Suggested correction: Revise the skill statement to more closely match the evidence, such as 'Identify where to go for help and support when concerned about online content or contact.' Alternatively, acknowledge this as an interpretation that expands on the source to define what might trigger a need for help.

                                                                                                                                                                                                Evaluate a design lifecycle

                                                                                                                                                                                                Explain limits of a test and propose repair or reuse; avoid unsupported lifecycle calculations.

                                                                                                                                                                                                Define design criteria for a simple artifact, considering user needs and functional requirements.

                                                                                                                                                                                                micro-define-design-criteria-28a9ae2034a142aa · design_process · T0 · 60 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                                Why this learning matters: Establishing clear design criteria is the foundational step in the design process, enabling effective evaluation. This skill ensures students understand what makes a 'good' design from the outset, moving beyond subjective preferences to objective measures. It directly supports the 'Design' stage by providing a framework for subsequent evaluation.

                                                                                                                                                                                                Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 6, students are capable of moving beyond simple problem identification to defining specific, measurable criteria that guide their design and evaluation efforts, aligning with the progression towards more structured design thinking.

                                                                                                                                                                                                Evidence of mastery: Students articulate clear, measurable design criteria for a given design challenge, linking them to user needs and functional aspects.

                                                                                                                                                                                                Assessment task: Given a scenario (e.g., 'Design a simple device to help organize school supplies on a desk'), students will individually list at least three design criteria. For each criterion, they must explain why it is important for the user and how it could be measured or observed.

                                                                                                                                                                                                Access and participation: Provide sentence starters or a word bank for defining criteria. Use visual aids to represent user needs. Allow verbal responses or dictation for students with writing difficulties. Pair students for collaborative brainstorming.

                                                                                                                                                                                                T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                                Evidence limitations: The source mentions 'develop design criteria' but does not detail the pedagogical approach, specific types of criteria, or how to teach students to make them measurable. The connection to user needs is inferred from 'fit for purpose, aimed at particular individuals or groups'.

                                                                                                                                                                                                Safety: All activities involving physical artifacts should ensure items are clean, safe to handle, and free from sharp edges or hazardous materials. When discussing repair, emphasize that actual repairs should only be attempted under adult supervision and with appropriate tools and safety gear. For electrical components, ensure they are disconnected from power sources and are low-voltage/non-hazardous.

                                                                                                                                                                                                Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                                Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Lists at least three distinct design criteria.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Each criterion is relevant to the given design scenario.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Each criterion addresses a user need or functional requirement.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Provides a clear explanation of how each criterion can be measured or observed.
                                                                                                                                                                                                Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Diagnosis: Ask students to brainstorm what makes a 'good' product. Discuss their answers, highlighting subjective vs. objective qualities.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Instruction: Introduce the concept of design criteria, explaining that they are specific, measurable goals for a design. Provide examples of criteria related to function, aesthetics, and user experience. Emphasize linking criteria to user needs.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Guided Practice: As a class, define criteria for a familiar object (e.g., a school backpack), discussing how to make them measurable.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Independent Practice: Students work in small groups to define criteria for a new, simple design challenge, then share and refine their criteria.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Feedback: Review student-defined criteria, providing feedback on specificity, measurability, and relevance to user needs.
                                                                                                                                                                                                Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Confusing personal preferences with objective design criteria.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Creating vague criteria that are not measurable or observable.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Focusing solely on aesthetics without considering function or user needs.
                                                                                                                                                                                                Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Whiteboard or large paper for brainstorming
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Markers
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Design scenario prompts
                                                                                                                                                                                                External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                                • Understanding of user needs and problem identification
                                                                                                                                                                                                Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                                • The provided source material ('sv-cyt') is primarily a content list for science education and does not offer detailed pedagogical strategies, specific assessment methods, or explicit connections to the broader design process beyond listing 'make' and 'evaluate' as general competencies. The unit's focus is therefore on the technical aspects of evaluation rather than the full design lifecycle (e.g., repairability, material impact).
                                                                                                                                                                                                • The evidence does not provide empirical validation for the effectiveness of these specific micro-skills or their sequencing.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • The international references (eng-dt) are general design and technology guidelines and do not specifically address electrical measurement or circuit construction at this level, requiring significant adaptation for the Salvadoran context and grade level. The micro-skills are derived from the Salvadoran science curriculum, not the UK design framework.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • The unit focuses on basic electrical circuits and measurements; it does not cover advanced topics like electromagnetism or complex circuit analysis, which are mentioned in the source but are beyond the scope of foundational evaluation skills for this grade.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • The critique correctly identified that the previous micro-skills were too focused on scientific measurement rather than design evaluation. This revision attempts to re-center on design process evaluation, but the source material from 'sv-cyt' still heavily emphasizes electrical concepts, making a full 'design lifecycle' unit challenging without external curriculum support. The 'eng-dt' source provides a framework for design practices, but specific content for 'repairability' and 'material use' at this grade level is not detailed.
                                                                                                                                                                                                • The 'sv-cyt' source does not explicitly mention 'repairability', 'material use', or 'foreseeable impact' in the context of design evaluation for this grade. The micro-skill 'propose-repair-reuse' is an interpretation of 'improve their work' from the 'eng-dt' source, extended to cover lifecycle considerations, but lacks direct textual support for these specific terms within the Salvadoran curriculum.
                                                                                                                                                                                                Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                                  Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • major: The unit is titled 'Technical Evaluation of Electrical Circuits', but the micro-skills and learning goal focus on general design principles (defining criteria, evaluating prototypes, proposing repair/reuse) without any specific application to electrical circuits. Suggested correction: Align the unit title with its content. Either change the title to reflect a general design process unit (e.g., 'Introduction to Design Evaluation') or rewrite the micro-skills to specifically address the evaluation of electrical circuits as suggested by the 'sv-cyt' source.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • major: The primary source for the unit's content, 'sv-cyt', is a science curriculum focused on electricity and magnetism. It does not contain content related to the proposed design skills of defining criteria, evaluating against them, or considering repairability and reuse. Suggested correction: Acknowledge that the unit's content is derived almost entirely from the 'eng-dt' source. The 'sv-cyt' source does not support the pedagogical content of this design unit. The unit should be re-scoped based on evidence that actually supports design process education.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Internal prerequisite IDs: None proposed

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Sources: source-eng-dt-bac6168fed01542b (page 3, Key stage 2, paragraph-p29-380fcda918046e73, original characters 58–102)

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Evaluate a simple artifact or prototype against defined design criteria, proposing improvements.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  micro-evaluate-against-criteria-26d668ee2a7e98aa · design_process · T0 · 90 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Why this learning matters: This skill is central to the design process, allowing students to objectively assess their work and iterate. It moves beyond simply identifying flaws to proposing actionable solutions, fostering critical thinking and problem-solving. It directly addresses the 'Evaluate' stage by applying criteria to real-world objects.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Proposed grade placement rationale: Grade 6 students can apply previously defined criteria to analyze existing products or prototypes, making informed judgments and suggesting improvements, which is a key aspect of design thinking at this level.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Evidence of mastery: Students systematically assess an artifact's performance against established criteria, identify areas for improvement, and suggest concrete modifications.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Assessment task: Students will be provided with a simple, non-functional prototype (e.g., a cardboard model of a phone holder) and a set of pre-defined design criteria (e.g., stability, ease of use, material efficiency). They must evaluate the prototype against each criterion, assign a rating (e.g., 'meets', 'partially meets', 'does not meet'), and for each criterion not fully met, propose a specific improvement.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Access and participation: Provide evaluation checklists with simplified language. Use visual scales (e.g., happy/sad faces) for ratings. Allow students to verbally describe improvements, which can be transcribed. Provide physical prototypes that are easy to manipulate.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Evidence limitations: The source states 'evaluate their ideas and products against their own design criteria' but does not specify the method of evaluation, the types of products, or the depth of improvement suggestions expected. It also doesn't detail how to teach students to propose improvements effectively.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Safety: All activities involving physical artifacts should ensure items are clean, safe to handle, and free from sharp edges or hazardous materials. When discussing repair, emphasize that actual repairs should only be attempted under adult supervision and with appropriate tools and safety gear. For electrical components, ensure they are disconnected from power sources and are low-voltage/non-hazardous.

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                                  Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Systematically evaluates the prototype against all provided criteria.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Provides a clear rating for each criterion.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • For each criterion not fully met, proposes a specific and relevant improvement.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Improvements are actionable and address the identified shortcomings.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Diagnosis: Show students a common object and ask them to identify its strengths and weaknesses. Discuss how they made those judgments.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Instruction: Explain the process of evaluating an artifact against specific criteria. Demonstrate how to use a rubric or checklist for evaluation. Emphasize providing constructive feedback and proposing concrete improvements.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Guided Practice: As a class, evaluate a simple object (e.g., a pencil sharpener) using a shared set of criteria, practicing how to rate and suggest improvements.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Independent Practice: Students work in pairs to evaluate a provided prototype using a given set of criteria, recording their findings and proposed improvements.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Feedback: Discuss student evaluations, focusing on the clarity of their ratings and the practicality of their proposed improvements.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Confusing personal opinion with objective evaluation based on criteria.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Providing vague or unspecific suggestions for improvement.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Focusing only on negative aspects without acknowledging strengths.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Simple prototypes (e.g., cardboard models, simple constructed items)
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Pre-defined design criteria checklists/rubrics
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Pencils/pens, recording sheets
                                                                                                                                                                                                  External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • Basic understanding of product function and user interaction
                                                                                                                                                                                                  Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • The provided source material ('sv-cyt') is primarily a content list for science education and does not offer detailed pedagogical strategies, specific assessment methods, or explicit connections to the broader design process beyond listing 'make' and 'evaluate' as general competencies. The unit's focus is therefore on the technical aspects of evaluation rather than the full design lifecycle (e.g., repairability, material impact).
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • The evidence does not provide empirical validation for the effectiveness of these specific micro-skills or their sequencing.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • The international references (eng-dt) are general design and technology guidelines and do not specifically address electrical measurement or circuit construction at this level, requiring significant adaptation for the Salvadoran context and grade level. The micro-skills are derived from the Salvadoran science curriculum, not the UK design framework.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • The unit focuses on basic electrical circuits and measurements; it does not cover advanced topics like electromagnetism or complex circuit analysis, which are mentioned in the source but are beyond the scope of foundational evaluation skills for this grade.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • The critique correctly identified that the previous micro-skills were too focused on scientific measurement rather than design evaluation. This revision attempts to re-center on design process evaluation, but the source material from 'sv-cyt' still heavily emphasizes electrical concepts, making a full 'design lifecycle' unit challenging without external curriculum support. The 'eng-dt' source provides a framework for design practices, but specific content for 'repairability' and 'material use' at this grade level is not detailed.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  • The 'sv-cyt' source does not explicitly mention 'repairability', 'material use', or 'foreseeable impact' in the context of design evaluation for this grade. The micro-skill 'propose-repair-reuse' is an interpretation of 'improve their work' from the 'eng-dt' source, extended to cover lifecycle considerations, but lacks direct textual support for these specific terms within the Salvadoran curriculum.
                                                                                                                                                                                                  Publication corrections after model review
                                                                                                                                                                                                    Uncalibrated model critique: points to review
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • major: The unit is titled 'Technical Evaluation of Electrical Circuits', but the micro-skills and learning goal focus on general design principles (defining criteria, evaluating prototypes, proposing repair/reuse) without any specific application to electrical circuits. Suggested correction: Align the unit title with its content. Either change the title to reflect a general design process unit (e.g., 'Introduction to Design Evaluation') or rewrite the micro-skills to specifically address the evaluation of electrical circuits as suggested by the 'sv-cyt' source.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Sources: source-eng-dt-bac6168fed01542b (page 3, Key stage 2, paragraph-p31-9e287d9b54a4b40e, original characters 130–197)

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Propose ways to repair or reuse components of a simple artifact to extend its lifecycle.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    micro-propose-repair-reuse-6cabb1594cb2110c · design_process · T0 · 75 instruction/practice minutes

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Why this learning matters: This skill introduces students to the concept of product lifecycle and sustainability within design. It encourages creative problem-solving by thinking about extending product utility rather than immediate disposal, aligning with responsible design practices. While the quote is about 'improving work', extending lifecycle through repair/reuse is a form of improvement in a broader sense.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Proposed grade placement rationale: At grade 6, students can begin to understand the environmental and economic implications of product lifecycles and apply creative thinking to propose solutions for repair and reuse, fostering a sense of responsibility in design.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Evidence of mastery: Students identify components of a broken or discarded artifact and suggest practical methods for repair or alternative uses for its parts.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Assessment task: Students will be given a simple, non-functional artifact (e.g., a broken toy, a discarded electronic component like a non-working flashlight). They must identify at least two components that could be repaired or reused, describe how they would repair them (if applicable), and propose a new, creative use for the component if it cannot be repaired.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Access and participation: Provide visual examples of repaired and reused items. Use simplified language for describing repair processes. Allow students to draw or verbally explain their ideas. Provide a variety of broken items with different levels of complexity.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    T0 alternative and limits: Not applicable

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Evidence limitations: The provided text does not explicitly mention 'repairability' or 'material use' in the context of design evaluation, nor does it detail pedagogical approaches for teaching lifecycle extension. The connection is made through 'improve their work' and the broader goal of the unit.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Safety: All activities involving physical artifacts should ensure items are clean, safe to handle, and free from sharp edges or hazardous materials. When discussing repair, emphasize that actual repairs should only be attempted under adult supervision and with appropriate tools and safety gear. For electrical components, ensure they are disconnected from power sources and are low-voltage/non-hazardous.

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Teacher preparation level (0–3): 2

                                                                                                                                                                                                    Success criteria
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Identifies at least two distinct components suitable for repair or reuse.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Provides a plausible method for repairing identified components.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Proposes a creative and practical new use for components that cannot be repaired.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Explanations demonstrate an understanding of the component's function or material properties.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    Teaching sequence
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Diagnosis: Ask students what happens to broken items. Discuss the concepts of 'throw away' vs. 'fix' or 'repurpose'.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Instruction: Introduce the concepts of repair, reuse, and recycling. Show examples of items that have been repaired or repurposed. Discuss the benefits of extending product lifecycles.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Guided Practice: As a class, examine a broken object (e.g., a broken chair leg) and brainstorm ways to repair it or reuse its parts.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Independent Practice: Students work in small groups with various broken or discarded items. They analyze the items, identify components, and propose repair or reuse strategies.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Feedback: Groups present their ideas, and the class discusses the feasibility and creativity of the proposed solutions.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    Misconceptions
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Believing that all broken items must be discarded.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Underestimating the potential for creative reuse of components.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Not considering the safety implications of repair or reuse.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    Required resources
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Various simple broken or discarded artifacts (e.g., old toys, non-working flashlights, empty containers)
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Whiteboard/markers or paper for brainstorming
                                                                                                                                                                                                    External knowledge to verify
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Basic understanding of material properties and common repair techniques
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • Environmental concepts of waste reduction
                                                                                                                                                                                                    Unit limitations
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • The provided source material ('sv-cyt') is primarily a content list for science education and does not offer detailed pedagogical strategies, specific assessment methods, or explicit connections to the broader design process beyond listing 'make' and 'evaluate' as general competencies. The unit's focus is therefore on the technical aspects of evaluation rather than the full design lifecycle (e.g., repairability, material impact).
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • The evidence does not provide empirical validation for the effectiveness of these specific micro-skills or their sequencing.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • The international references (eng-dt) are general design and technology guidelines and do not specifically address electrical measurement or circuit construction at this level, requiring significant adaptation for the Salvadoran context and grade level. The micro-skills are derived from the Salvadoran science curriculum, not the UK design framework.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • The unit focuses on basic electrical circuits and measurements; it does not cover advanced topics like electromagnetism or complex circuit analysis, which are mentioned in the source but are beyond the scope of foundational evaluation skills for this grade.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • The critique correctly identified that the previous micro-skills were too focused on scientific measurement rather than design evaluation. This revision attempts to re-center on design process evaluation, but the source material from 'sv-cyt' still heavily emphasizes electrical concepts, making a full 'design lifecycle' unit challenging without external curriculum support. The 'eng-dt' source provides a framework for design practices, but specific content for 'repairability' and 'material use' at this grade level is not detailed.
                                                                                                                                                                                                    • The 'sv-cyt' source does not explicitly mention 'repairability', 'material use', or 'foreseeable impact' in the context of design evaluation for this grade. The micro-skill 'propose-repair-reuse' is an interpretation of 'improve their work' from the 'eng-dt' source, extended to cover lifecycle considerations, but lacks direct textual support for these specific terms within the Salvadoran curriculum.
